Tensor structure on perverse Nori motives
Luca Terenzi

Abstract
. Let k k be a field of characteristic 0 0 endowed with a fixed field embedding σ : k ↪ ℂ \sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C} . In this paper we complete the construction of the six functor formalism on perverse Nori motives over quasi-projective k k -varieties initiated by F. Ivorra and S. Morel. Our main contribution is the construction of a canonical closed unitary symmetric monoidal structure on the bounded derived categories of perverse Nori motives compatible with the analogous structure on the underlying constructible derived categories; along the way, we extend some of Nori’s original results to perverse Nori motives. As a consequence, we obtain well-behaved Tannakian categories of motivic local systems over smooth, geometrically connected k k -varieties. Our constructions do not depend on the chosen complex embedding of k k and, in fact, our results generalize to arbitrary base fields of characteristic 0 0 .



†
†

1991
Mathematics Subject Classification
. 14F42, 14F43, 14C15.
†
†

Key words and phrases
. Motives, perverse sheaves, sheaf operations.
†
†

The author acknowledges support by the GK 1821 ”Cohomological Methods in Geometry” at the University of Freiburg and by the Labex Milyon at the ENS Lyon.

Introduction

Motivation and goal of the paper

Let
k
be a field of characteristic
0
endowed with a fixed field embedding into the complex numbers
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
. In the late 1990’s, M. Nori made a spectacular proposal for an unconditional construction of the conjectural
\mathbb{Q}
-linear abelian category of
mixed motives
over
k
. By design, Nori’s category
\mathscr{M}(k)
is the target of the universal cohomology theory on
k
-varieties comparable to
\mathbb{Q}
-linear singular cohomology of the underlying complex-analytic spaces; in particular, it comes equipped with a canonical faithful exact functor into finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces

(1)

\iota_{k}:\mathscr{M}(k)\rightarrow\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}

which recovers singular cohomology groups when applied to the cohomological motives of
k
-varieties. Through the classical comparison isomorphisms relating singular cohomology with algebraic de Rham cohomology and with the various
\ell
-adic cohomologies, Nori’s category
\mathscr{M}(k)
also acquires faithful exact functors into the associated coefficient categories recovering the corresponding cohomology groups of
k
-varieties.

According to the conjectures summarized by Beilinson in
[
Bei87H
, § 5.10]
, the theory of mixed motives should admit an enhancement to a theory of
mixed motivic sheaves
: for every (quasi-projective)
k
-variety
S
there should exist an abelian category of mixed motivic sheaves over
S
providing a universal enhancement of the classical analytic and
\ell
-adic categories of perverse sheaves; as
S
varies, their bounded derived categories should admit a complete six functor formalism analogously to the classical analytic and
\ell
-adic derived categories, as well as a compatible theory of weights.
The theory of
perverse Nori motives
, introduced by F. Ivorra and S. Morel in
[
IM19
]
, provides an unconditional candidate for the conjectural abelian categories of mixed motivic sheaves predicted by Beilinson: for every (quasi-projective)
k
-variety
S
, they define a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear abelian category
\mathscr{M}(S)
which comes equipped with a canonical faithful exact functor into algebraically constructible
\mathbb{Q}
-linear perverse sheaves

(2)

\iota_{S}:\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S).

Their main result
[
IM19
, Thm. 5.1]
asserts that, as
S
varies, the bounded derived categories
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
are canonically endowed with Grothendieck’s operations
f^{*}
,
f_{*}
,
f_{!}
and
f^{!}
, compatibly with the analogous operations on the underlying derived categories
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))
along the triangulated functors

(3)

\iota_{S}:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))

induced by the exact functors (
2
).
The main goal of the present paper is to complete the construction of the six functor formalism on perverse Nori motives by defining a canonical closed unitary symmetric monoidal structure on the categories
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
in such a way that the functors (
3
) are unitary symmetric monoidal and compatible with internal homomorphisms.

History and previous work

Before explaining our results in more detail, let us review the construction of perverse Nori motives. In order to put it into perspective, it is convenient to start from the very beginning of the theory. Nori’s original definition of the abelian category
\mathscr{M}(k)
is based on an abstract category-theoretic result about representations of quivers into vector spaces. The idea is to introduce a quiver
\mathsf{Pairs}_{k}
encoding the basic features of singular cohomology of (pairs of)
k
-varieties: functoriality along morphisms of pairs, long exact cohomology sequences of triples, and Tate twists. Vertices of
\mathsf{Pairs}_{k}
are typically written as quadruples
(X,Y;n,w)
consisting of a
k
-variety
X
, a closed subvariety
Y
, a cohomological degree
n
and a Tate degree
w
. Singular cohomology can be regarded as a quiver representation

(4)

b_{k}:\mathsf{Pairs}_{k}\rightarrow\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}},

and the conjectural category of mixed motives over
k
should be the smallest non-full abelian subcategory of
\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}
containing the image of
b_{k}
. Nori was able to construct such a category explicitly in terms of the representation
b_{k}
, thus obtaining the abelian category
\mathscr{M}(k)
equipped with the forgetful functor (
1
).
While Nori’s construction was tailored to representation of quivers into vector spaces (or, slightly more generally, into finitely generated modules over a Noetherian ring), it was later generalized to representations of quivers into arbitrary abelian categories in
[
BVCL18
]
using the language of categorical logic; the latter result was then translated into the familiar language of categories in
[
BVP18
]
, based on P. Freyd’s classical construction of the abelian hull of an additive category from
[
Fre65
]
.
In its final form, the result asserts that, for every quiver representation into an abelian category

\beta:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{A},

there exists a smallest non-full abelian subcategory of
\mathscr{A}
containing the image of
\beta
, called the associated
universal abelian factorization
.
However, the simplicity of Nori’s construction and of its later variants hides a serious drawback: it does not allow one to compute morphisms or extension groups in
\mathscr{M}(k)
explicitly, except for a few trivial cases. Indeed, since universal abelian factorizations are defined by purely categorical methods, it is typically impossible to describe their inner shape directly.

One of the most surprising achievements of Nori’s work is the construction of a canonical tensor product on
\mathscr{M}(k)
making it a neutral Tannakian category over
\mathbb{Q}
, with canonical fibre functor provided precisely by the forgetful functor (
1
). The Tannaka dual of
\mathscr{M}(k)
, denoted
\mathscr{G}_{mot}(k)
, is called the
motivic Galois group
of
k
. Even if our knowledge of the structure of this group is extremely poor, it allows for an elegant motivic interpretation of the Kontsevich–Zagier conjecture about periods of algebraic varieties; in fact, this interpretation was arguably Nori’s original motivation for introducing
\mathscr{M}(k)
(see
[
HMS17
, §§ II, III]
for a detailed account of this story).

In contrast with the existence of the abelian category
\mathscr{M}(k)
, the existence of a tensor structure on it is a highly non-formal result. The intuitive idea is to get a tensor product on
\mathscr{M}(k)
by defining a suitable pairing on the quiver
\mathsf{Pairs}_{k}
compatible with tensor product of singular cohomology groups and show that this descends to a tensor product on the universal abelian factorization. However, the only reasonable guess for such a pairing on
\mathsf{Pairs}_{k}
, given by the rule

(X_{1},Y_{1};n_{1},w_{1})\otimes(X_{2},Y_{2},n_{2},w_{2}):=(X_{1}\times X_{2},Y_{1}\times X_{2}\cup X_{1}\times Y_{2};n_{1}+n_{2},w_{1}+w_{2}),

is not always compatible with tensor product of singular cohomology groups: using Künneth’s formula, one sees that the pairing is well-behaved only when restricted to the subquiver
\mathsf{Pairs}_{k}^{0}\subset\mathsf{Pairs}_{k}
collecting all those vertices
(X,Y;n,w)
such that the singular cohomology of the pair
(X,Y)
(twisted by
w
) is concentrated precisely in degree
n
. Applying Nori’s categorical construction to the restricted representation

b_{k}^{0}:=b_{k}|_{\mathsf{Pairs}_{k}^{0}}:\mathsf{Pairs}_{k}^{0}\rightarrow\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}},

one obtains an abelian subcategory
\mathscr{M}^{0}(k)
of
\mathscr{M}(k)
which, by design, admits a well-defined tensor product

(5)

-\otimes-:\mathscr{M}^{0}(k)\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(k)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(k)

turning the restriction of (
1
) into a tensor functor. Now the problem becomes to show that the natural inclusion
\mathscr{M}^{0}(k)\subset\mathscr{M}(k)
is in fact an equivalence. This was proven by Nori as a consequence of Beilinson’s result
[
Bei87D
, Lemma 3.3]
, quoted by Nori in
[
Nor18
]
as the Basic Lemma. From Nori’s perspective, the Basic Lemma is a result about the existence of algebraic skeletal filtrations on affine
k
-varieties; in fact, it is the fundamental ingredient in the geometric interpretation of the cohomological perverse filtration described by M.A. de Cataldo and L. Migliorini in
[
DCM10
]
.

The Basic Lemma turns out to be a crucial input into another major result of Nori’s theory: namely, the existence of a canonical triangulated realization

(6)

R_{k}:\mathbf{DM}_{gm}(k,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(k)).

Here,
\mathbf{DM}_{gm}(k,\mathbb{Q})
denotes Voevodsky’s triangulated category of
\mathbb{Q}
-linear geometric motives over
k
, which so far represents the best known candidate for the bounded derived category of the conjectural abelian category of mixed motives over
k
; it is endowed with a triangulated functor

(7)

\mathsf{Bti}_{k}^{*}:\mathbf{DM}_{gm}(k,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}(\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}})

called the Betti realization, which should be regarded as a triangulated analogue of (
1
). Nori’s realization functor (
6
) has the distinctive property that the composite functor

\mathbf{DM}_{gm}(k,\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{R_{k}}D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(k))\xrightarrow{\iota_{k}}D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))\xrightarrow{\real{}_{k}}D^{b}(\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}})

recovers the Betti realization (
7
) up to canonical natural isomorphism. Since, in addition, the singular representation (
4
) factors as the composite

\mathsf{Pairs}_{k}\xrightarrow{M_{k}}\mathbf{DM}_{gm}(k,\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{Bti}_{k}^{*}}D^{b}(\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}})\xrightarrow{H^{0}}\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}

for a suitable map of quivers
M_{k}:\mathsf{Pairs}_{k}\rightarrow\mathbf{DM}_{gm}(k,\mathbb{Q})
, the equivalence
\mathscr{M}^{0}(k)=\mathscr{M}(k)
follows formally. Furthermore, it follows that Nori’s category
\mathscr{M}(k)
can be reconstructed directly from Voevodsky’s category
\mathbf{DM}_{gm}(k,\mathbb{Q})
: it suffices to apply Nori’s category-theoretic construction to the composite functor

\beta_{k}:\mathbf{DM}_{gm}(k,\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{Bti}^{*}_{k}}D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(k))\xrightarrow{H^{0}}\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}},

regarded as a quiver representation. As pointed out in
[
BVHP20
, Thm. 4.10]
, Nori’s tensor product (
5
) can be also constructed starting from the latter presentation of
\mathscr{M}(k)
, and the realization (
6
) is a tensor-triangulated functor.
The existence of a canonical functor relating Voevodsky’s triangulated category with the derived category of Nori’s abelian category is a strong indication that Nori’s construction gives the correct definition of mixed motives over
k
. The triangulated category
\mathbf{DM}_{gm}(k,\mathbb{Q})
is expected to admit a
motivic
t
-structure
rendering the Betti realization (
7
)
t
-exact; its heart should be the true conjectural abelian category of mixed motives (see
[
Ayo17
, § 3.3]
). A consequence of the existence of the realization (
6
) is that, if the motivic
t
-structure on
\mathbf{DM}_{gm}(k,\mathbb{Q})
exists, then its heart is necessarily equivalent to
\mathscr{M}(k)
.
Let us mention that an alternative construction of the motivic Galois group
\mathscr{G}_{mot}(k)
avoiding Nori motives was provided by Ayoub in
[
Ayo14H1
,
Ayo14H2
]
based on his weak Tannakian formalism for monoidal triangulated categories.
The fact that the motivic Galois group arising from Ayoub’s construction coincides with Nori’s Galois group
\mathscr{G}_{mot}(k)
was proved by U. Chudhurry and M. Gallauer in
[
CG17
]
and, up to now, represents the strongest piece of evidence to the general expectations just mentioned; Nori’s realization (
6
) plays a crucial role in the proof.

The theory of Voevodsky motives has been already expanded to a theory of motivic sheaves satisfying a large part of Beilinson’s predictions: for every (quasi-projective)
k
-variety
S
there exists a triangulated category
\mathbf{DM}_{gm}(S,\mathbb{Q})
of
\mathbb{Q}
-linear geometric motives over
S
; as
S
varies, they enjoy a complete six functor formalism and admit a compatible weight structure in the sense of Bondarko’s paper
[
Bon14
]
. The construction of the six functors in the motivic world was sketched by V. Voevodsky himself in his lecture
[
Del01
]
, developed systematically in J. Ayoub’s thesis work
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
]
, and further refined in D.-C. Cisinski and F. Déglise’s book
[
CD19
]
. By
[
Ayo14E
, Thm. B.1]
, Voevodsky’s category
\mathbf{DM}_{gm}(S,\mathbb{Q})
is canonically equivalent to Ayoub’s category
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})
of constructible étale motives over
S
without transfers defined in
[
Ayo14E
, § 3]
; by
[
CD19
, Thm. 16.2.18]
, the latter is also equivalent to the category of constructible Beilinson motives defined in
[
CD19
, § IV]
. The theory of
[
IM19
]
is developed using mostly Ayoub’s categories
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})
and adopting the language of
stable homotopy
2
-functors
developed in
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
]
,
[
Ayo10
]
and
[
Ayo14E
]
. For every (quasi-projective)
k
-variety
S
, Ayoub constructed in
[
Ayo10
]
a triangulated Betti realization

(8)

\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})

into the usual algebraically constructible derived category
D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
associated to the complex-analytic space
S^{\sigma}
. By
[
Ayo10
, Thm. 3.19]
, as
S
varies the functors (
8
) are canonically compatible with the six operations on the two sides, thereby defining a morphism of stable homotopy
2
-functors

(9)

\mathsf{Bti}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q}).

Moreover, by
[
Ayo10
, Prop. 4.7]
, the realization functors (
8
) are also compatible with nearby and vanishing cycles functors on the two sides.

The
\mathbb{Q}
-linear abelian category of perverse Nori motives
\mathscr{M}(S)
is defined in
[
IM19
, § 2.1]
by taking the universal abelian factorization of the homological functor

(10)

\beta_{S}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}}D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{{{}^{p}H^{0}}}\mathsf{Perv}(S),

which may be also regarded as a mere quiver representation. The abelian category
\mathscr{M}(S)
comes equipped with the faithful exact functor (
2
), which extends to the conservative triangulated functor (
3
). As a consequence of Nori’s results mentioned above, the category
\mathscr{M}(S)
recovers Nori’s original category
\mathscr{M}(k)
when
S=\textup{Spec}(k)
. By
[
IM19
, Thm. 5.1]
, the triangulated categories
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
canonically assemble into a stable homotopy
2
-functor in the sense of
[
Ayo07a
]
, and the triangulated functors (
3
) canonically assemble into a morphism of stable homotopy
2
-functors

(11)

\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))

in the sense of
[
Ayo10
]
. Furthermore, as proved in
[
IM19
, § 3]
, the theory of perverse Nori motives is endowed with the analogue of Beilinson’s gluing functors from
[
Bei87G
]
. These functors, whose definition builds upon the theory of specialization systems developed in
[
Ayo07b
]
, play a crucial role in the construction of motivic inverse and direct images obtained in
[
IM19
, §§ 4, 5]
.

Main results

Our main result asserts that the four functor formalism on perverse Nori motives constructed in
[
IM19
]
admits a canonical enhancement to a complete six functor formalism. In the language of stable homotopy
2
-functors, it can be conveniently summarized as follows:

Theorem

(
2.1
,
5.1
)
.

The stable homotopy
2
-functor
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
obtained in
[
IM19
, Thm. 5.1]
is canonically closed unitary symmetric monoidal. Moreover, the morphism of stable homotopy
2
-functors (
11
) is canonically a unitary symmetric monoidal morphism which also respects internal homomorphisms.

The first intermediate result is the construction of the monoidal structure. The basic principle - as always, in the theory of Nori motives - is that the universal property of the categories
\mathscr{M}(S)
can be exploited to lift functors from the underlying constructible derived categories, provided these functors can be expressed in terms of the six functor formalism and are
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structures. One basic technical issue is that the internal tensor product

-\otimes-:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))

is not
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structures unless
\dim(S)=0
. The abstract theory developed in
[
Ter23T
]
allows us to overcome this issue by working with the external tensor product

-\boxtimes-:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S_{1}))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S_{2}))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S_{1}\times S_{2})),

which is
t
-exact in each variable by
[
BBD82
, § 4.2.7]
. But this is still not enough: as explained in the previous paragraph, lifting the external tensor product directly to
\mathscr{M}(S)
is not allowed even over
S=\textup{Spec}(k)
, for the simple reason that, given two complexes
K_{1}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}))
and
K_{2}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S_{2}))
, the equality

{{}^{p}H^{0}}(K_{1}^{\bullet})\boxtimes{{}^{p}H^{0}}(K_{2}^{\bullet})={{}^{p}H^{0}}(K_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes K_{2}^{\bullet})

does not hold unless one among
K_{1}^{\bullet}
and
K_{2}^{\bullet}
is concentrated in perverse degree
0
. In fact, this is just a different incarnation of the Künneth formula issue already encountered for the tensor product on
\mathscr{M}(k)
; of course, defining Nori motives starting from the quiver of Voevodsky motives
\mathbf{DM}_{gm}(k,\mathbb{Q})
instead than from Nori’s original quiver
\mathsf{Pairs}_{k}
does not remove this technical issue.
By mimicking Nori’s strategy, for every
k
-variety
S
we introduce a convenient abelian subcategory
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
of
\mathscr{M}(S)
on which the external tensor product can be defined directly, and we then show that the natural inclusion
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\subset\mathscr{M}(S)
is in fact an equivalence. Here is the precise statement:

Theorem

(
1.12
)
.

For every (quasi-projective)
k
-variety
S
, consider the full additive subcategory

\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét},0}(S,\mathbb{Q}):=\left\{A\in\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\;|\;\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(A)\in\mathsf{Perv}(S)\right\}\subset\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q}),

and let
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
denote the universal abelian factorization of the additive functor

\beta_{S}^{0}:=\beta_{S}|_{\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét},0}(S,\mathbb{Q})}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét},0}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S).

Then the canonical faithful exact functor
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)
is in fact an equivalence.

Note that our definition of
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét},0}(S,\mathbb{Q})
is just the natural analogue of Nori’s definition of the subquiver
\mathsf{Pairs}_{k}^{0}
in the setting of Voevodsky motives. In order to make our constructions uniform, it is convenient to view the original category
\mathscr{M}(S)
as the universal abelian factorization of (
10
) regarded as an additive functor.
Unfortunately, we are not able prove our comparison result by generalizing Nori’s geometric ideas over arbitrary base varieties - although we believe that this should be possible. Instead, we use Nori’s equivalence over fields as the starting step of a Noetherian induction argument, the inductive step of which exploits the motivic version of Beilinson’s gluing functors obtained in
[
IM19
, § 3]
.

Once the single external tensor product functors are constructed, turning their collection into the sought-after monoidal structure amounts to constructing monoidality isomorphisms along morphisms of
k
-varieties as well as compatible associativity, commutativity and unit constraints. Using the abstract theory developed in
[
Ter23F
]
and
[
Ter23E
]
, most of the required constructions can be reduced to the level of abelian categories, where the universal property of perverse Nori motives can be exploited to lift natural transformations between exact functors.
The most delicate point is constructing the unit constraint and checking its compatibility with the rest of the structure: this requires a closer analysis of the tensor product with certain distinguished motivic local systems.
The consequences of this analysis play a crucial role also in the construction of internal homomorphisms. The latter result is quite lengthy and technical, but it is based on a simple strategy (inspired to a construction of D. Arapura from
[
Ara13
, § 5]
): in a first step, we extend part of the functoriality of perverse Nori motives to the derived ind-categories
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))
, which allows us to define internal homomorphisms functors abstractly as formal right adjoints of the tensor product; in a second step, we show that these formal internal homomorphisms respect the bounded derived categories
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
and moreover are compatible with the usual internal homomorphisms on the underlying categories of perverse sheaves. Our analysis of the tensor product with distinguished motivic local systems, combined with repeated use of localization triangles, is the key ingredient for the second step.

One of the most interesting consequences of the six functor formalism on perverse Nori motives is the existence of well-behaved Tannakian categories of
motivic local systems
over suitable bases. It can be stated as follows:

Theorem

(
6.2
)
.

For every smooth, geometrically connected
k
-variety
S
of dimension
d
, the full subcategory

\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S):=\left\{M\in\mathscr{M}(S)\;|\;\iota_{S}(M)\in\mathsf{Loc}(S)[d]\right\}\subset\mathscr{M}(S)

is canonically a neutral Tannakian category over
\mathbb{Q}
.

The smoothness assumption in the statement is necessary to ensure that ordinary local systems over
S
are perverse sheaves up to shifting by
d
.

Our categories of motivic local systems provide the ambient categories for E. Jacobsen’s motivic version of Hain’s theorem described in
[
Jac23
, Thm. 8.4]
. We plan to study the categories
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
and their associated motivic Galois groups systematically in future work.

Related and future work

In the same period when the results of the present paper were completed, significant progress in the theory of perverse Nori was achieved in a complementary direction by work of S. Tubach. Building upon Nori’s classical argument for the derived category of constructible sheaves from
[
Nor18
]
, he shows in
[
Tub23
]
that the triangulated category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
is canonically equivalent to the bounded derived category of its so-called constructible heart (see
[
IM19
, § 5.4]
for the definition of the latter). The main consequence of this result is that the six functors on perverse Nori motives can be canonically enhanced to
\infty
-functors. In addition to allowing for an extension of the theory of perverse Nori motives to not necessarily quasi-projective
k
-varieties, this implies the existence of a canonical morphism of stable homotopy
2
-functors

(12)

\mathsf{Nri}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))

lifting the Betti realization (
9
); in fact, with the
\infty
-categorical lifting of the six operations at hand, both the existence and the essential uniqueness of (
12
) follow immediately from B. Drew and M. Gallauer’s universality theorem for triangulated motives from
[
DG22
]
.

Let us stress that even constructing the single realization functors
\mathsf{Nri}_{S}^{*}
over general
S
directly seems a hard task. To the author’s knowledge, the only plausible candidate construction available to date is the one proposed by Ivorra in
[
Ivo17
]
, which only works over smooth bases. Ivorra’s construction is based on cellular complexes of perverse sheaves over
S
attached to relative smooth affine
S
-schemes; it factors trough another abelian category of perverse Nori motives introduced by Ivorra in
[
Ivo17
]
, which is conjectured to be equivalent to
\mathscr{M}(S)
although, a priori, it is only a non-full subcategory of the latter. The main problem with Ivorra’s realization functors is that neither their compatibility with the Betti realization nor their compatibility with the six operations are fully understood yet (mostly due to concrete technical issues concerning filtrations of varieties over a base). The expectation is that Ivorra’s realization functors should recover the functors (
12
) over smooth bases; in fact, this would be a consequence of the expected compatibility of Ivorra’s functors with the Betti realization and with the six operations.

The results of
[
Tub23
]
are related to our results in two ways. On the one hand, the existence of a canonical closed monoidal structure on perverse Nori motives is used crucially in Tubach’s variation of Nori’s argument as described in
[
Tub23
, § 2]
. On the other hand, the existence of the triangulated realization (
12
) allows us to derive some pleasant complementary results concerning the motivic Verdier duality functors: we show that these functors, which were defined in
[
IM19
, § 2.8]
purely in terms of the universal property of perverse Nori motives, admit the usual description in terms of internal homomorphisms to dualizing complexes; and that, as a consequence, internal homomorphisms of perverse Nori motives admit the classical reformulation in terms of tensor product and Verdier duality. Curiously enough, we were able to establish the latter result directly in some cases: this is
3.13
, which in turn was a crucial ingredient in our construction of internal homomorphisms!

In order to make the whole picture terser, it would be useful to have an alternative, more direct construction of perverse Nori motives at our disposal. In his recent preprint
[
Ayo21
]
, J. Ayoub constructed a new stable homotopy
2
-functor enhancing the category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(k))
; while Ayoub’s six functor formalism is obtained all at once and comes equipped with a canonical
\infty
-categorical enhancement, it does not manifestly enjoy a universal property analogous to that of Ivorra and Morel’s categories. In an ongoing project (joint with E. Jacobsen) we aim at showing that Ayoub’s triangulated categories are equivalent to Ivorra and Morel’s categories
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
compatibly with the six functor formalism. A significant step towards this goal consists in giving a precise meaning to the intuition that the Tannakian categories
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
should be thought of as categories of
\mathscr{M}(k)
-valued local systems of geometric origin.

Structure of the paper

Most constructions of the present paper are based on the abstract properties of perverse motives as universal abelian factorizations.
The basic functoriality results for universal abelian factorizations used in the setting of perverse motives are quickly discussed in
[
IM19
, § 2.1]
, based on the detailed results of
[
Ivo17
]
. A more systematic treatment, which also includes the extension of the above discussion to multi-linear functors, is given in our note
[
Ter23U
]
, which we will often refer to.

In
Section

1
we recall and extend the discussion of
[
IM19
, §§ 3, 4]
concerning Beilinson’s gluing functors in the setting of perverse motives. Our first goal is to obtain a gluing result for the abelian categories of perverse motives analogous to Beilinson’s classical result for perverse sheaves (
1.7
). We deduce an abstract comparison criterion asserting that any system of abelian subcategories
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\subset\mathscr{M}(S)
which is stable under the gluing functors and induces equivalences over the generic points of all
k
-varieties must be in fact a system of equivalences (
1.9
). We then construct a system of abelian subcategories
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
(
3.1
) which, by design, are more adapted to defining the external tensor product functors than the original categories
\mathscr{M}(S)
, and we show that they satisfy the assumptions of our abstract comparison criterion (
1.12
).
This allows us to construct the tensor structure on perverse motives by working with the categories
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
in place of the categories
\mathscr{M}(S)
.
Along the way, we study the behavior of Nori motives over function fields of
k
-varieties in some detail (
1.15
and
1.17
).

In
Section

2
we state our first main result concerning the existence of the monoidal structure on perverse motives together with its associativity, commutativity and unit constraints (
2.1
). After some general categorical preliminaries (
2.6
), we construct the whole monoidal structure except for the unit constraint: in the first place, we construct the external monoidality isomorphisms (
2.8
) by exploiting the reduction results of
[
Ter23F
]
; we then endow the external tensor product with compatible associativity and commutativity constraints (
2.11
,
2.12
and
2.13
).

Section

3
is a crucial technical interlude needed both for the unit constraint and for internal homomorphisms of perverse motives. We introduce, for every smooth
k
-variety
S
, a further abelian subcategory
\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)
of
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
(
3.1
) and we study the restriction of the internal tensor product to
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)
: the main result is that this functor, which a priori is only defined indirectly from the external tensor product, can be actually described in terms of the universal property of perverse motives (
3.6
). As a consequence, we readily deduce the existence of internal homomorphisms attached to certain ’distinguished motivic local systems’ (
3.13
).

In
Section

4
we finally complete the proof of
2.1
by constructing the unit constraint for perverse motives (
4.1
). In the first place, we construct the motivic unit section (
4.3
) by exploiting the reduction results of
[
Ter23E
]
. We then use it to construct the motivic unit constraint, and we check that the latter is compatible with the associativity and commutativity constraints defined previously (
4.8
and
4.9
).

In
Section

5
we prove the existence of internal homomorphisms for perverse motives (
5.1
). The strategy consists of two main steps: firstly, we construct an abstract ind-adjoint to the tensor product functors (
5.10
); secondly, using the partial results obtained at the end of
Section

3
, we check that these functors respects the bounded derived categories and that they are compatible with the usual internal homomorphisms on the underlying constructible derived categories by applying an abstract criterion (
5.14
and
5.15
). To conclude, we explain how our results, combined with Tubach’s results from
[
Tub23
]
, imply the expected relations between Verdier duality and internal homomorphisms in the setting of perverse motives (
5.19
).

In the final
Section

6
we introduce abelian categories of motivic local systems over smooth, geometrically connected
k
-varieties (
6.1
) and we show that they are canonically neutral Tannakian over
\mathbb{Q}
(
6.2
). As an application in the direction tensor-triangulated geometry, we describe a topological reconstruction result for perverse motives (
6.5
).

Notation and conventions

Fibered categories

•

We follow the notation and conventions on fibered categories used in
[
Ayo07a
]
and in
[
CD19
]
, as displayed explicitly in
[
Ter23T
, § 1]
.

Algebraic Geometry

•

We fix a field
k
of characteristic
0
endowed with a fixed field embedding
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

•

\mathsf{Var}_{k}
denotes the category of algebraic varieties over
k
: objects are reduced, separated, finite type
k
-schemes; morphisms are
k
-morphisms.

•

By the closed immersion
complementary
to a given open immersion
j:U\hookrightarrow S
in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
we mean the closed immersion
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
where
Z
denotes the closed complement of
U
in
S
endowed with its reduced scheme structure.

•

We consider the following full subcategories of
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
:

–

\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
: the subcategory of smooth
k
-varieties;

–

\mathsf{Aff}_{k}
: the subcategory of affine
k
-varieties.

•

For every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
we write:

–

a_{S}:S\rightarrow\textup{Spec}(k)
for the structural morphism;

–

\mathscr{O}(S)
for the
k
-algebra of global regular functions on
S
;

–

\mathsf{Op}(S)
for the full subcategory of the slice category
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}/S
whose objects are the Zariski-dense open immersions into
S
;

–

\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)
for the full subcategory
\mathsf{Aff}_{k}\subset\mathsf{Op}(S)
;

–

\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
for the full subcategory
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}\subset\mathsf{Op}(S)
.

Analytic sheaves

•

For every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, we use the following notation:

–

S^{\sigma}
denotes the complex analytic space whose points are the
\mathbb{C}
-points of
S
over
k
via
\sigma
.

–

\mathscr{C}\textit{t}(S)
denotes the abelian category of sheaves of
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces on
S^{\sigma}
which are algebraically constructible.

–

\mathsf{Loc}(S)
denotes the full abelian subcategory of
\mathscr{C}\textit{t}(S)
consisting of all locally constant sheaves.

–

D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
denotes the derived category of complexes of sheaves of
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces on
S^{\sigma}
with bounded algebraically constructible cohomology.

–

\mathsf{Perv}(S)
denotes the heart of the self-dual perverse
t
-structure on
D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
as defined in
[
BBD82
]
, and
{{}^{p}H^{0}}:D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S)
denotes the corresponding homological functor.

\ell
-adic sheaves

•

Let
\ell
be a fixed prime number. For every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, we use the following notation:

–

\mathscr{C}\textit{t}(S)_{\ell}
denotes the abelian category of
\ell
-adic algebraically constructible sheaves on the small étale site
S_{\textup{ét}}
.

–

\mathsf{Loc}(S)_{\ell}
denotes the full abelian subcategory of
\mathscr{C}\textit{t}(S)_{\ell}
consisting of all lisse sheaves.

–

D^{b}_{c,\ell}(S,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
denotes the constructible
\ell
-adic derived category as defined by Ekedahl in
[
Eke90
]
or equivalently by Bhatt–Scholze in
[
BS15
]
.

–

\mathsf{Perv}(S)_{\ell}
denotes the heart of the self-dual perverse
t
-structure on
D^{b}_{c,\ell}(S,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
as defined in
[
Mor19
, § 2.2]
, and
{{}^{p}H^{0}}:D^{b}_{c,\ell}(S,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S)_{\ell}
denotes the corresponding homological functor.

Triangulated motives and realizations

•

For every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, we use the following notation:

–

\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})
denotes the triangulated category of constructible étale motives with
\mathbb{Q}
-coefficients constructed in
[
Ayo07b
, § 4.5.2]
(see also
[
Ayo14E
, § 3]
), implicitly identified with the category of constructible Beilinson motives defined in
[
CD19
, § IV]
via the equivalence of
[
CD19
, Thm. 16.2.18]
.

–

We let

\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c,\ell}(S,\mathbb{Q})

denote the
Betti realization
over
S
associated to
\sigma
constructed in
[
Ayo10
]
.

–

Let
\ell
be a fixed prime number. We let

R_{\ell,S}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c,\ell}(S,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})

denote the
\ell
-adic realization
over
S
constructed in
[
Ayo14E
]
.

Universal abelian factorizations

•

For every additive category
\mathscr{D}
, we write:

–

\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D})
for the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Fun}(\mathscr{D}^{op},\mathbf{Ab})
consisting of all presheaves of finite presentation (i.e. cokernels of representable presheaves).

–

\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{D}):=\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D}^{op})^{op}
.

–

\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}):=\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D}))
for Freyd’s abelian hull of
\mathscr{D}
introduced in
[
Fre65
]
(see also
[
Ter23U
, § 1]
).

•

For every additive functor
\beta:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
from an additive category
\mathscr{D}
to an abelian category
\mathscr{A}
, we write:

–

\beta^{+}:\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D})\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
for the unique exact functor extending
\beta
;

–

\mathbf{A}(\beta):\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D})/\ker(\beta^{+})
for the associated universal abelian factorization.

Perverse Nori motives

•

For every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, we write:

–

\mathscr{D}(S)
for the underlying additive category of
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})
;

–

\beta_{S}:\mathscr{D}(S)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S)
for the homological functor

\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}}D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{{{}^{p}H^{0}}}\mathsf{Perv}(S)

regarded as an additive functor.

–

\mathscr{M}(S)
for the category of
perverse Nori motives
over
S
, defined as the universal abelian factorization
\mathbf{A}(\beta_{S})
of the additive functor
\beta_{S}
.

–

\pi_{S}
for the quotient functor
\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}(S))\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)
, and also for the composite functor
\mathscr{D}(S)\rightarrow\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}(S))\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)
,

–

\iota_{S}
for the induced faithful exact functor
\mathscr{M}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S)
.

1.
Beilinson gluing for perverse motives

Our first main goal is to obtain an alternative description of perverse Nori motives which is more adapted to defining the external tensor product. The precise result is
1.12
below, which can be regarded as a generalization of Nori’s philosophy of
good pairs
(see
[
HMS17
, § II.9]
) to arbitrary base varieties. The proof relies on an abstract comparison criterion formalized as
1.9
; this in turn is based on
1.7
, a motivic analogue of the classical gluing formalism for perverse sheaves introduced by Beilinson in
[
Bei87G
]
.

Before stating the main results, we review the construction of
[
IM19
, § 3.5]
which provides all the essential ingredients. We then obtain the motivic gluing formalism by simply adapting Beilinson’s classical argument to the motivic setting; our presentation is modeled on Morel’s note
[
Mor18
]
. The proof of the final result is preceded by a detailed discussion about Nori motives over function fields, which leads to the auxiliary results
1.15
and
1.17
. In our opinion, these results are of independent interest and represent a useful complement to
[
IM19
, § 6.5]
.

1.1.
Review of the motivic gluing functors

We start by recalling the relevant results from
[
IM19
, § 3.5]
. To begin with, fix a variety
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
and a regular function
f\in\mathscr{O}(S)
with vanishing locus
Z
; let
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
denote the corresponding closed immersion, and let
j:U\hookrightarrow S
denote the complementary open immersion.
Since
j
is affine by construction, by
[
IM19
, § 2.4]
there exist canonical exact functors

j_{!},\;j_{*}:\mathscr{M}(U)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)

which mimic the formal properties of the underlying functors on perverse sheaves, namely: they are left and right adjoint to the inverse image functor
j^{*}:\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(U)
, respectively; they are fully faithful; they are intertwined under the motivic Verdier duality functors of
[
IM19
, § 2.8]
.

As shown in
[
IM19
, § 3.5]
, the classical gluing functors for perverse sheaves defined in
[
Bei87G
]
can be canonically lifted to exact functors

\displaystyle{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}:\mathscr{M}(U)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(Z),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}:\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(Z),

\displaystyle\quad{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}:\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}:\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S).

In the setting of perverse sheaves, the functors
{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}
and
{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}
are the classical unipotent nearby cycles and vanishing cycles functors (shifted by
-1
, so that they become
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structures), while the functor
{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}
is Beilinson’s maximal extension functor (or rather, the composite of the latter with
j^{*}
). The functor
\Omega_{f}
first appeared in Saito’s work on mixed Hodge modules
[
Sai90
]
.

Remark 1.1
.

In
[
IM19
]
the functor
{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}:\mathscr{M}(U)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(Z)
is denoted
{{}^{p}\mathsf{Log}_{f}}
, since it comes from Ayoub’s logarithmic specialization system constructed in
[
Ayo07b
, § 3.6]
.

The functors
{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}
and
{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}
fit into short exact sequences of functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)

(13)

0\rightarrow j_{!}j^{*}M\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\rightarrow i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)(-1)\rightarrow 0

and

(14)

0\rightarrow i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\rightarrow j_{*}j^{*}M\rightarrow 0

which are intertwined under motivic Verdier duality; thus the composite
j_{!}j^{*}M\hookrightarrow{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\twoheadrightarrow j_{*}j^{*}M
coincides with the composite
j_{!}j^{*}M\xrightarrow{\epsilon}M\xrightarrow{\eta}j_{*}j^{*}M
.
There are two further relevant short exact sequences of functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)

(15)

0\rightarrow i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)\rightarrow M\rightarrow 0

and

(16)

0\rightarrow j_{!}j^{*}M\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)\rightarrow i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)\rightarrow 0.

The functors
{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}
and
{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}
are also related by a canonical natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)

{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)

which allows one to combine the exact sequences (
14
) and (
15
) into a commutative diagram of the form

(17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}j^{*}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

All these fact were noted in
[
IM19
, § 3.5]
. Our first task is to deduce further information about the mutual relations among the gluing functors.

In the proof of the following results, we often need to use the fully faithfulness of the exact functor
i_{*}:\mathscr{M}(Z)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)
proven in
[
IM19
, Thm. 4.1]
.

Lemma 1.2
.

With the above notation, there exist a canonical natural isomorphism between functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)

(18)

{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)=\ker\left\{{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus M\xrightarrow{-}j_{*}j^{*}M\right\}

and a canonical natural isomorphism between functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(Z)

(19)

{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)=i^{*}H^{0}[j_{!}j^{*}M\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus M\xrightarrow{-}j_{*}j^{*}M].

Proof.

Regard the commutative diagram (
17
) as a double complex. Since both rows are exact, we deduce that its total complex is exact as well. Hence the total complex of the truncated double complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}j^{*}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

is exact as well. This yields the natural isomorphism (
18
). Moreover, since the arrow
j_{!}j^{*}M\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)
in the short exact sequence (
13
) is a monomorphism, the canonical arrow

\textup{im}\left\{j_{!}j^{*}M\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus M\right\}\rightarrow\textup{im}\left\{j_{!}j^{*}M\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\right\}

is an isomorphism. This yields a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)=

\displaystyle\textup{coker}\left\{j_{!}j^{*}M\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)\right\}

by (
16
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{coker}\left\{j_{!}j^{*}M\xrightarrow{+}\ker\left\{{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus M\xrightarrow{-}j_{*}j^{*}M\right\}\right\}

by (
18
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{0}[j_{!}j^{*}M\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus M\xrightarrow{-}j_{*}j^{*}M].

Thus we obtain the natural isomorphism (
19
) as the composite

{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)=i^{*}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)=i^{*}H^{0}[j_{!}j^{*}M\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus M\xrightarrow{-}j_{*}j^{*}M],

where the first passage follows from the fully faithfulness of
i_{*}
.
∎

The following consequence plays a fundamental role in the construction of motivic inverse images under closed immersions described in
[
IM19
, § 4.1]
. We spell it out since it is relevant for our constructions as well.

Corollary 1.3
.

With the above notation, there exists a canonical isomorphism of functors
\mathscr{M}(Z)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(Z)

(20)

{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(i_{*}M)=M.

In other words, the functor
{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}
is canonically left-inverse to
i_{*}
.

Proof.

Combining any of the two short exact sequences (
13
) and (
14
) with the vanishing
j^{*}\circ i_{*}=0
, we deduce the vanishing
{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}\circ i_{*}=0
. Hence we obtain the sought-after isomorphism (
20
) as the composite

\displaystyle{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(i_{*}M)=

\displaystyle i^{*}H^{0}[j_{!}j^{*}i_{*}M\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(i_{*}M)\oplus i_{*}M\xrightarrow{-}j_{*}j^{*}i_{*}M]

by (
19
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i^{*}H^{0}[0\rightarrow 0\oplus i_{*}M\oplus 0]

as
j^{*}\circ i_{*}=0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i^{*}i_{*}M=M,

where the last passage follows again from the fully faithfulness of
i_{*}
.
∎

With these results at our disposal, we can lift the classical nilpotent monodromy operator
N_{f}
, together with its canonical factorization (see
[
Mor18
, §§ 2, 6]
) to the setting of perverse motives.

Construction 1.4
.

Keep the above notation. Consider the composite natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)

(21)

i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\hookrightarrow{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\twoheadrightarrow i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)(-1),

where the two arrows come from the short exact sequences (
13
) and (
14
), respectively. Since the functor
i_{*}:\mathscr{M}(Z)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)
is fully faithful, there is a unique natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(Z)

(22)

N_{f}:{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)(-1)

whose image under
i_{*}
coincides with (
21
). We look for a canonical factorization of
N_{f}
through the functor
{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}
.
To this end, note that the arrow
i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\hookrightarrow{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)
factors through
{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)=\ker\left\{{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus M\xrightarrow{-}j_{*}j^{*}M\right\}
, because we have

\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}(S)}(i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M),j_{*}j^{*}M)=\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}(U)}(j^{*}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M),j^{*}M)=0

as
j^{*}\circ i_{*}=0
. Hence we obtain a canonical natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(Z)

(23)

\textup{can}_{f}:{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)

by declaring that its image under the fully faithful functor
i_{*}:\mathscr{M}(Z)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)
be the induced arrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\left\{{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus M\xrightarrow{-}j_{*}j^{*}M\right\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}[j_{!}j^{*}M\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus M\xrightarrow{-}j_{*}j^{*}M]}

Dually, note that the arrow
{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\twoheadrightarrow i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M)(-1)
is trivial on
\textup{im}\left\{j_{!}j^{*}M\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus M\right\}
, because we have

\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}(S)}(j_{!}j^{*}M,i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M))=\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}(U)}(j^{*}M,j^{*}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M))=0

as
j^{*}\circ i_{*}=0
. Hence we obtain a canonical natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(Z)

(24)

\textup{var}_{f}:{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)\rightarrow i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)(-1)

by declaring that its image under the fully faithful functor
i_{*}:\mathscr{M}(Z)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)
be the induced arrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}[j_{!}j^{*}M\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus M\xrightarrow{-}j_{*}j^{*}M]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{coker}\left\{j_{!}j^{*}M\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus M\right\}}

By construction, we have the equality of natural transformations

(25)

\textup{var}_{f}\circ\textup{can}_{f}=N_{f}:{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)(-1).

Before stating the main result about gluing of perverse motives, it is convenient to define one last short exact sequence of gluing functors.

Construction 1.5
.

Keep the above notation. We have a canonical natural isomorphism of functors
\mathscr{M}(U)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(Z)

(26)

{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(j_{!}M)={{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M)

given by the composite

\displaystyle{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(j_{!}M)=

\displaystyle i^{*}H^{0}[j_{!}j^{*}(j_{!}M)\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(j_{!}M)\oplus j_{!}M\xrightarrow{-}j_{*}j^{*}(j_{!}M)]

by (
19
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i^{*}H^{0}[j_{!}M\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(j_{!}M)\oplus j_{!}M\xrightarrow{-}j_{*}j^{*}(j_{!}M)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i^{*}\ker\left\{{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(j_{!}M)\rightarrow j_{*}j^{*}(j_{!}M)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i^{*}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}j_{!}M)

by (
14
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M),

where the last passage follows from the fully faithfulness of
i_{*}
and of
j_{!}
.
Hence, composing the short exact sequence (
13
) with the exact functor
j_{!}
on the right and with the exact functor
{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}
on the left, and using the isomorphisms (
20
) and (
26
) together with the fully faithfulness of
j_{!}
, we obtain a canonical short exact sequence of functors
\mathscr{M}(U)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(Z)

(27)

0\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M)\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(j_{!}M)\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M)(-1)\rightarrow 0.

1.2.
Motivic gluing formalism

We are ready to lift Beilinson’s gluing construction to the motivic setting. We keep the notation introduced in the previous subsection: we work over a fixed
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
and we consider a global regular function
f\in\mathscr{O}(S)
with vanishing locus
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
and non-vanishing locus
j:U\hookrightarrow S
.

Notation 1.6
.

We introduce the category of
motivic gluing data

\mathscr{M}(S,f)
defined as follows:

•

objects are quadruples
(M_{U},M_{Z};u,v)
consisting of

–

two objects
M_{U}\in\mathscr{M}(U)
,
M_{Z}\in\mathscr{M}(Z)
,

–

two morphisms
u:{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})\rightarrow M_{Z}
and
v:M_{Z}\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})(-1)
satisfying the relation

v\circ u=N_{f}(M_{U}):{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})(-1);

•

a morphism
\alpha:(M_{U},M_{Z};u,v)\rightarrow(M^{\prime}_{U},M^{\prime}_{Z};u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
is the datum of two morphisms
\alpha_{U}:M_{U}\rightarrow M^{\prime}_{U}
and
\alpha_{Z}:M_{Z}\rightarrow M^{\prime}_{Z}
such that the diagram in
\mathscr{M}(Z)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M^{\prime}_{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M^{\prime}_{U})(-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(\alpha_{U})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(\alpha_{U})(-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}

is commutative.

The following result is the motivic lifting of
[
Bei87G
, Prop. 3.1]
. The proof that we present is merely an adaptation of the detailed argument given in
[
Mor18
, Thm. 8.1]
.

Proposition 1.7
.

Keep the above notation. Then the functors

\delta_{S,f}:\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S,f),\qquad\gamma_{S,f}:\mathscr{M}(S,f)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)

defined by the formulae

\delta_{S,f}(M):=(j^{*}M,{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M);\textup{can}_{f},\textup{var}_{f})

and

\gamma_{S,f}(M_{U},M_{Z};u,v):=H^{0}[i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(j_{!}M_{U})\oplus i_{*}M_{Z}\xrightarrow{-}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})(-1)]

are canonically mutually quasi-inverse equivalences.

Proof.

The fact that
\delta_{S,f}
is well-defined follows from the relation (
25
).
Let us construct a canonical isomorphism
\delta_{S,f}\circ\gamma_{S,f}\simeq\textup{id}_{\mathscr{M}(S,f)}
. Given an object
(M_{U},M_{Z};u,v)\in\mathscr{M}(S,f)
, write the object
\delta_{S,f}\gamma_{S,f}(M_{U},M_{Z};u,v)\in\mathscr{M}(S,f)
as a quadruple
(M^{\prime}_{U},M^{\prime}_{Z};u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
. We have a canonical isomorphism

\displaystyle M^{\prime}_{U}:=

\displaystyle j^{*}\gamma_{S,f}(M_{U},M_{Z};u,v)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle j^{*}H^{0}[i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(j_{!}M_{U})\oplus i_{*}M_{Z}\xrightarrow{-}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})(-1)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{0}[j^{*}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})\xrightarrow{+}j^{*}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(j_{!}M_{U})\oplus j^{*}i_{*}M_{Z}\xrightarrow{-}j^{*}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})(-1)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{0}[0\rightarrow j^{*}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(j_{!}M_{U})\oplus 0\rightarrow 0]

as
j^{*}\circ i_{*}=0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle j^{*}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(j_{!}M_{U})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle j^{*}j_{!}M_{U}

by (
13
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M_{U},

where the last passage follows from the fully faithfulness of
j_{!}
.
Similarly, we have a canonical isomorphism

\displaystyle M^{\prime}_{Z}:=

\displaystyle{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}\gamma_{S,f}(M_{U},M_{Z};u,v)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}H^{0}[i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(j_{!}M_{U})\oplus M_{Z}\xrightarrow{-}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})(-1)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{0}[{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(j_{!}M_{U})\oplus{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}i_{*}M_{Z}\xrightarrow{-}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})(-1)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{0}[{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(j_{!}M_{U})\oplus M_{Z}\xrightarrow{-}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(M_{U})(-1)]

by (
20
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M_{Z}.

by (
27
)

The fact that these two isomorphisms glue to an isomorphism in
\mathscr{M}(S,f)
follows formally from the construction; we omit the details.

Conversely, let us construct a canonical isomorphism
\gamma_{S,f}\circ\delta_{S,f}\simeq\textup{id}_{\mathscr{M}(S)}
. Given an object
M\in\mathscr{M}(S)
, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{S,f}\gamma_{S,f}(M):=

\displaystyle H^{0}[i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)\xrightarrow{-}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)(-1)]=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{coker}\left\{i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\rightarrow O_{f}(M)\right\},

where, for notational convenience, we have set

O_{f}(M):=\ker\left\{{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)\xrightarrow{-}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)(-1)\right\}.

Applying the Snake Lemma to the commutative diagram of functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)
with exact rows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{f}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-}

and using the short exact sequences (
13
) and (
16
), we obtain the vanishing

\ker\left\{{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)\rightarrow O_{f}(M)\right\}=0=\textup{coker}\left\{{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)\rightarrow O_{f}(M)\right\},

from which it follows that the canonical arrow
{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)\rightarrow O_{f}(M)
is an isomorphism. Hence we obtain a canonical isomorphism

\delta_{S,f}\gamma_{S,f}(M)=\textup{coker}\left\{i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\rightarrow O_{f}(M)\right\}=\textup{coker}\left\{i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}(M)\right\}=M

where the last passage follows from the short exact sequence (
15
).
∎

As a consequence of the motivic gluing formalism, we can now state an abstract comparison criterion that we will later apply to obtain the sought-after alternative description of perverse motives.

For sake of clarity, it is convenient to introduce a suitable notion of cofinality for inclusions of fibered categories over a cofiltered small category:

Definition 1.8
.

Let
\mathscr{U}
be a cofiltered small category. Given a
\mathscr{U}
-fibered category
\mathscr{C}
and a
\mathscr{U}
-fibered subcategory
\mathscr{C}^{0}
, we say that the inclusion of
\mathscr{U}
-fibered categories
\mathscr{C}^{0}\subset\mathscr{C}
is
weakly cofinal
if the canonical functor

2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathscr{U}^{op}}\mathscr{C}^{0}(U)\rightarrow 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathscr{U}^{op}}\mathscr{C}(U)

is an equivalence.

Recall that, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, we let
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
denote the poset of smooth Zariski-dense open subsets of
S
. We can state our comparison criterion as follows:

Proposition 1.9
.

Suppose that we are given, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, a (not necessarily full) abelian subcategory
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
of
\mathscr{M}(S)
, in such a way that the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

As
S
varies in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the categories
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
are stable under the following exact functors:

(a)

for every affine open immersion
j:U\hookrightarrow S
, the inverse image functor
j^{*}
as well as the direct image functors
j_{!}
and
j_{*}
;

(b)

for every closed immersion
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
, the direct image functor
i_{*}
;

(c)

for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the Tate twist functor
(-)(-1)
;

(d)

for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
and every
f\in\mathscr{O}(S)
, the functors
{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}
,
{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}
,
{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}
and
{{}^{p}\Omega_{f}}
.

(ii)

For every
S\in\mathsf{Aff}_{k}
and every
f\in\mathscr{O}(S)
, the natural transformations in the exact sequences (
13
), (
14
), (
15
) and (
16
) restrict to natural transformations of functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
.

(iii)

For every
S\in\mathsf{Aff}_{k}
, the natural inclusion of
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
-fibered categories
\mathscr{M}^{0}\subset\mathscr{M}
is weakly cofinal in the sense of
1.8
.

Then, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the inclusion
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\subset\mathscr{M}(S)
is in fact an equivalence.

Proof.

The proof consists of two main steps: in the first place we reduce the proof to the case of affine varieties, while in the second step we prove the thesis in the affine case. As the reader will see, the first step is a standard Zariski-descent argument which only uses the stability of the categories
\mathscr{M}(S)
under inverse images and direct images along affine open immersions; on the other hand, the second step exploits the full power of the gluing formalism.

(Step 1)

Assume for a moment that the inclusion
\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)\subset\mathscr{M}(U)
is known to be an equivalence for every
U\in\mathsf{Aff}_{k}
, and let us show that the same conclusion holds for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
. Fix one variety
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, and choose a finite affine open covering
\left\{u_{r}:U_{r}\hookrightarrow S\right\}_{r\in I}
; for every
r,s\in I
, form the Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{r}\cap U_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{s}^{(r)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}^{(s)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{s}}

and let
u_{r,s}
denote the composite open immersion
U_{r}\cap U_{s}\hookrightarrow S
. Note that each
u_{r}
, each
u_{r}^{(s)}
and each
u_{r,s}
is affine, because
S
is separated over
k
by definition. Consider the usual exact sequence of exact functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)

\bigoplus_{r,s\in I}u_{r,s,!}u_{r,s}^{*}M\rightarrow\bigoplus_{r\in I}u_{r,!}u_{r}^{*}M\rightarrow M\rightarrow 0.

Note that the functors
\bigoplus_{r,s\in I}u_{r,s,!}u_{r,s}^{*}
and
\bigoplus_{r\in I}u_{r,!}u_{r}^{*}
take values in the essential image of
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
by condition (i) in the statement, since, by our assumption, the functors
u_{r,s}^{*}
and
u_{r}^{*}
take values in the essential image of
\mathscr{M}^{0}(U_{r}\cap U_{s})
and
\mathscr{M}^{0}(U_{r})
, respectively. We claim that the natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)

\bigoplus_{r,s\in I}u_{r,s,!}u_{r,s}^{*}M\rightarrow\bigoplus_{r\in I}u_{r,!}u_{r}^{*}M

takes values in
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
as well; this will immediately imply that the inclusion of abelian categories
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\subset\mathscr{M}(S)
is both essentially surjective and fully faithful, thus an equivalence.
To prove the claim, it suffices to recall that, for every
r,s\in I
, the arrow
u_{r,s,!}u_{r,s}^{*}M\rightarrow u_{r,!}u_{r}^{*}M
is nothing but the composite

u_{r,s,!}u_{r,s}^{*}M=u_{r,!}u_{s,!}^{(r)}u_{s}^{(r),*}u_{r}^{*}M\xrightarrow{\epsilon}u_{r,!}u_{r}^{*}M,

and to note the second arrow belongs to
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
by condition (i), since the arrow
u_{s,!}^{(r)}u_{s}^{(r),*}u_{r}^{*}M\xrightarrow{\epsilon}u_{r}^{*}M
belongs to
\mathscr{M}^{0}(U_{r})
by our assumption.

(Step 2)

We show that, for every
S\in\mathsf{Aff}_{k}
, the inclusion
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\subset\mathscr{M}(S)
is an equivalence. We argue by Noetherian induction on
S
, starting from the case where
\dim(S)=0
. The base case follows directly from hypothesis (iii), since in this case the only object of
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
is
S
itself. For the inductive step, assume that
\dim(S)>0
and that the result is known to hold for all proper closed subvarieties
Z
of
S
(this is legitimate, since every such
Z
is itself affine).

Let us first show that the inclusion
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\subset\mathscr{M}(S)
is essentially surjective. Given an object
M\in\mathscr{M}(S)
, choose any regular function
f\in\mathscr{O}(S)
with smooth Zariski-dense non-vanishing locus
D(f)
such that the object
M|_{D(f)}\in\mathscr{M}(D(f))
belongs to
\mathscr{M}^{0}(D(f))
: the existence of such a function follows from condition (iii) in the statement (using the fact that, as
f
varies in
\mathscr{O}(S)
, the distinguished open subsets
D(f)
form a basis for the Zariski topology of the affine variety
S
). Let
j:D(f)\hookrightarrow S
and
i:V(f)\hookrightarrow S
denote the corresponding open and closed immersions. By
1.7
, we have a canonical isomorphism in
\mathscr{M}(S)

M=\gamma_{S,f}\delta_{S,f}(M):=H^{0}[i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\xrightarrow{+}{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\oplus i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)\xrightarrow{-}i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)(-1)].

We claim that the complex on the right is isomorphic to a complex in
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
; this will imply that
M
belongs to the essential image of the abelian subcategory
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
inside
\mathscr{M}(S)
. In order to prove the claim, it suffices to note that:

–

The objects
i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)
,
{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)
,
i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)(-1)
belong to the essential image of
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
by condition (i), since
j^{*}M
lies in the essential image of
\mathscr{M}^{0}(D(f))
by construction.

–

The object
i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)
belongs to
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
by condition (i), since the inclusion
\mathscr{M}^{0}(V(f))\subset\mathscr{M}(V(f))
is essentially surjective by inductive hypothesis.

–

The arrows
i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\rightarrow{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)
and
{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M)\rightarrow i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)(-1)
belong to
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
by condition (ii).

–

The arrows
i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)\rightarrow i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)
and
i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M)\rightarrow i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M)(-1)
, being in the image of
i_{*}
, belong to
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
by condition (i), since the inclusion
\mathscr{M}^{0}(V(f))\subset\mathscr{M}(V(f))
is fully faithful by inductive hypothesis.

This shows the essential surjectivity.

Let us now show that the inclusion
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\subset\mathscr{M}(S)
is full. Given a morphism
\alpha:M_{1}\rightarrow M_{2}
in
\mathscr{M}(S)
, choose any regular function
f\in\mathscr{O}(S)
with smooth, Zariski-dense non-vanishing locus
D(f)
such that the objects
M_{1}|_{D(f)},M_{2}|_{D(f)}\in\mathscr{M}(D(f))
belong to the essential image of
\mathscr{M}^{0}(D(f))
and the morphism
\alpha|_{D(f)}:M_{1}|_{D(f)}\rightarrow M_{2}|_{D(f)}
also lies in
\mathscr{M}^{0}(D(f))
: as in the previous case, the existence of such a function follows from condition (iii) in the statement. As before, let
j:D(f)\hookrightarrow S
and
i:V(f)\hookrightarrow S
denote the corresponding open and closed immersions. Under the canonical isomorphisms
M_{r}=\gamma_{S,f}\delta_{S,f}(M_{r})
,
r=1,2
, constructed in the proof of
1.7
, the morphism
\alpha
is induced by the morphism of complexes in
\mathscr{M}(S)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M_{1})\oplus i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M_{1})(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(M_{2})\oplus i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}M_{2})(-1).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(\alpha)\oplus i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(\alpha)(-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-}

By the same argument as in the previous paragraph, both complexes are isomorphic to complexes in
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
. We claim that the three vertical arrows lie in
\mathscr{M}(S)
as well; this will imply that the morphism
\alpha
belongs to the abelian subcategory
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
of
\mathscr{M}(S)
. In order to prove the claim, it suffices to note that:

–

The arrows
i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}\alpha)
,
{{}^{p}\Xi_{f}}(\alpha)
and
i_{*}{{}^{p}\Psi_{f}}(j^{*}\alpha)(-1)
lie in
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
by condition (i), since the arrow
j^{*}\alpha
lies in
\mathscr{M}^{0}(D(f))
by construction.

–

The arrow
i_{*}{{}^{p}\Phi_{f}}(\alpha)
, being in the essential image of the fully faithful functor
i_{*}
, lies in
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
by condition (i), since the inclusion
\mathscr{M}^{0}(V(f))\subset\mathscr{M}(V(f))
is full by inductive hypothesis.

This shows the fully faithfulness.

The proof is now complete.
∎

At first sight, the amount of conditions to check in order to apply
1.9
looks massive. However, the stability conditions at points (i) and (ii) are automatically satisfied as long as the categories
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
are defined by
t
-exactness conditions. In fact, all the actual work in the proof of
1.12
below goes into checking the weak cofinality property required in condition (iii).

1.3.
Application to perverse motives

In the final part of this section, we apply the criterion of
1.9
to obtain the promised alternative description of perverse motives. Let us first fix the relevant notation:

Notation 1.10
.

Let
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
.

•

We introduce the full additive subcategory

\mathscr{D}^{0}(S):=\left\{A\in\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\;|\;\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(A)\in\mathsf{Perv}(S)\right\}\subset\mathscr{D}(S).

•

We let
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
denote the universal abelian factorization of the additive functor

\beta_{S}^{0}:=\beta_{S}|_{\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}:\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S).

We implicitly regard it as a possibly non-full abelian subcategory of
\mathscr{M}(S)
via the canonical faithful exact functor

\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)

obtained by applying
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 2.5]
to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S}}
id

•

For simplicity, we still write

–

\pi_{S}
for the quotient functor
\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}^{0}(S))\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
, and also for the composite functor
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}^{0}(S))\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
,

–

\iota_{S}
for the induced faithful exact functor
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S)
.

This convention will not create any ambiguity.

Even if the definition of the additive categories
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)
(and, therefore, also that of the abelian categories
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
) depends a priori on the field embedding
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we purposely omit any mention of it from the notation. This choice is justified by the following result:

Lemma 1.11
.

Let
\sigma^{\prime}:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be another complex embedding of
k
; for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, write
\mathsf{Bti}_{S,\sigma^{\prime}}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(S^{\sigma^{\prime}},\mathbb{Q})
for the associated Betti realization functor. Then the additive subcategory

\mathscr{D}^{0}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(S):=\left\{A\in\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\;|\;\mathsf{Bti}_{S,\sigma^{\prime}}^{*}(A)\in\mathsf{Perv}(S^{\sigma^{\prime}})\right\}\subset\mathscr{D}(S)

coincides with the category
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)
defined above.

Proof.

We have to show that, for every object
A\in\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})
, the equivalence

\mathsf{Bti}_{S,\sigma}^{*}(A)\in\mathsf{Perv}(S)\iff\mathsf{Bti}_{S,\sigma^{\prime}}(A)\in\mathsf{Perv}(S)_{\ell}

holds. We claim that, in fact, for every fixed
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the equivalence

{{}^{p}H^{n}}(\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(A))=0\iff{{}^{p}H^{n}}(R_{\ell,S}(A))=0

holds; the claim clearly implies the thesis. The claim can be proved by induction on
\dim(S)
using the method of the proof of
[
IM19
, Prop. 6.11]
. We omit the details.
∎

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of the following result:

Theorem 1.12
.

For every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the canonical inclusion
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\subset\mathscr{M}(S)
is in fact an equivalence.

The proof of this result, given at the end of the present section, is essentially a direct application of
1.9
. However, this requires some careful preparation: firstly, we need to make sense of perverse Nori motives over function fields of
k
-varieties by a colimit construction; secondly, we need to reduce ourselves to the case of function fields over finitely generated subfields of
k
, so that we are allowed to embed these function fields into
\mathbb{C}
; thirdly, in such a situation, we need to interpret the categories of perverse Nori motives over function fields as categories of motives in Nori’s sense, so that we are allowed to exploit Nori’s geometric results over the function fields in question.

In order to achieve all these steps, our original approach was to exploit the alternative definition of perverse motives via the
\ell
-adic realization, as done in
[
IM19
, § 6.5]
: by the comparison result
[
IM19
, Prop. 6.11]
, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
the abelian category
\mathscr{M}(S)
can be recovered as the universal abelian factorization associated to the homological functor

(28)

\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{R_{\ell,S}}D^{b}_{c,\ell}(S,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})\xrightarrow{{{}^{p}H^{0}}}\mathsf{Perv}(S)_{\ell}.

Here,
D^{b}_{c,\ell}(S,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
denotes the constructible
\ell
-adic derived category (as defined in
[
Eke90
]
or equivalently in
[
BS15
]
) and
\mathsf{Perv}(S)_{\ell}
denotes the heart of the self-dual perverse
t
-structure on
D^{b}_{c,\ell}(S,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
(as defined, for example, in
[
Mor19
]
). The triangulated functor

R_{\ell,S}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c,\ell}(S,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})

is the
\ell
-adic realization constructed by Ayoub in
[
Ayo14E
]
; as
S
varies in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, these functors assemble into a morphism of monoidal stable homotopy
2
-functors.
Compared to the Betti realization, the
\ell
-adic realization has the key advantage of being absolute and independent of any auxiliary choices (such as the choice of a complex embedding
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
).

Remark 1.13
.

In fact, as far as we can see, the proof of
[
IM19
, Prop. 6.11]
tacitly passes through the
\ell
-adic derived categories
D^{b}_{c,\ell}(S_{\bar{k}},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
over the base-changed varieties
S_{\bar{k}}:=S\times_{\textup{Spec}(k)}\textup{Spec}(\bar{k})
.
Indeed, the proof of
[
IM19
, Prop. 6.11]
in the case where
S=\textup{Spec}(k)
uses the existence of Huber’s mixed realizations from
[
Hub00
]
, which yields a comparison isomorphism between the Betti and
\ell
-adic realizations of
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(k,\mathbb{Q})
extending the classical comparison isomorphism between Betti and
\ell
-adic cohomology.
Recall, however, that the latter takes the form

H^{n}(X^{\sigma},\mathbb{Q})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}=H^{n}_{\ell}(X_{\bar{k}},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})

for
X\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
. Therefore, the existence of the comparison isomorphism alone does not imply directly a comparison between the Betti realization and the absolute
\ell
-adic realization.
In order to get the desired comparison, one has to use the fact that the inverse image functors

D^{b}_{c,\ell}(S,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c,\ell}(S_{\bar{k}},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})

are conservative and commute with the six operations. Indeed, this fact implies formally that the universal abelian factorization associated to the homological functor (
28
) does not change if one composes further with the exact inverse image functor
\mathsf{Perv}(S)_{\ell}\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S_{\bar{k}})_{\ell}
, because the latter is faithful.

In terms of the absolute
\ell
-adic realization, both the definition of Nori motives over function fields and the reduction to finitely generated fields alluded to above can be easily obtained from the continuity property
[
IM19
, Prop. 6.9]
.
However, the precise form of Nori’s geometric results that we want to use in the proof of
1.12
is only available with respect to the Betti realization.
This would force us to use the comparison result
[
IM19
, Prop. 6.11]
twice: the first time to pass from the Betti to the
\ell
-adic version, the second time to go back from the
\ell
-adic to the Betti version.

In order to avoid such an artificial-sounding argument, here we follow a different approach which does not rely on the
\ell
-adic realization at all. Instead, we define abelian categories of Nori motives over function fields of
k
-varieties by a direct colimit presentation, and we study them from the perspective of the Betti realization.
The crucial result is
1.15
below, where these abelian categories are identified as categories of motives in Nori’s sense, at least when the function field in question admits a complex embedding extending the original embedding of
k
.
This is, in our opinion, a very pleasant result, which provides a clear geometric explanation behind the seemingly miraculous independence results of
[
IM19
, Prop. 6.11]
.
Together with
1.17
, this allows us to reduce the proof of
1.12
to the finitely generated case as promised.

In the first place, we need to recall how Ivorra and Morel’s definition of perverse motives can be extended to function fields of
k
-varieties; we need a similar extension for the categories introduced in
3.1
.

Construction 1.14
.

Let
L
be a finitely generated extension field of
k
, and fix one
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
with function field
L:=k(S)
: this means that
S
is irreducible and the sheaf of
\mathscr{O}_{S}
-modules

U\mapsto\textup{Frac}(\mathscr{O}_{S}(U))

is identified with the constant sheaf with value
L
over the Zariski site of
S
.
We can write

\textup{Spec}(L)=\varprojlim_{U\in\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)}U

as the inverse limit of all affine open subsets of
S
in the category of
k
-schemes. By the continuity property of triangulated étale motives proved in
[
Ayo07a
]
, the canonical functor

2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(U,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\textup{Spec}(L),\mathbb{Q})

induced by the inverse image functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(U,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\textup{Spec}(L),\mathbb{Q})
is an equivalence.
Therefore, by
[
Ter23U
, Lemma 5.12(2)]
, the filtered
2
-colimit of abelian categories

(29)

2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)}\mathscr{M}(U)

can be canonically identified with the universal abelian factorization of the additive functor

\beta_{L,\sigma}:\mathscr{D}(\textup{Spec}(L))=2-\varinjlim_{U\subset S}\mathscr{D}(U)\xrightarrow{\beta_{U}}2-\varinjlim_{U\subset S}\mathsf{Perv}(U),

where, in analogy to the notation
\mathscr{D}(U)
for
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(U,\mathbb{Q})
employed for
U\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, we write
\mathscr{D}(\textup{Spec}(L))
for the underlying additive category of
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\textup{Spec}(L),\mathbb{Q})
.
Let us suggestively write
\mathscr{M}(\textup{Spec}(L))
for the abelian category (
29
); note that, in the case where the extension
L/k
is finite (so that the
k
-scheme
\textup{Spec}(L)
belongs to
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
), this agrees with the definition of perverse motives over
\textup{Spec}(L)
given in
[
IM19
]
.
Clearly, in the definition of the
2
-colimit (
29
), one can replace the poset
\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)
by any cofinal subposet: for instance, we can use the poset
