\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)\cap\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)
consisting of all smooth affine open subsets of
S
.
It is also easy to see that the definition of
\mathscr{M}(\textup{Spec}(L))
is independent of the chosen model
S
of
L
; we leave the details to the interested reader.

Keeping the above notation, let us also introduce the additive subcategory

\mathscr{D}^{0}(\textup{Spec}(L)):=2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathscr{D}^{0}(U)\subset\mathscr{D}(\textup{Spec}(L)).

Again, this definition does not depend on the chosen model
S
of
L
and does not change if one replaces the poset
\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)
by any cofinal subposet such as
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)\cap\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)
.
Moreover, let
\mathscr{M}^{0}(\textup{Spec}(L))
denote the universal abelian factorization of the additive functor

\beta_{L,\sigma}^{0}:=\beta_{L,\Sigma}|_{\mathscr{D}^{0}(\textup{Spec}(L))}:\mathscr{D}^{0}(\textup{Spec}(L))=2-\varinjlim_{U\subset S}\mathscr{D}^{0}(U)\xrightarrow{\beta_{U}}2-\varinjlim_{U\subset S}\mathsf{Perv}(U),

which is independent of the chosen presentation as well; if the extension
L/k
is finite, this is compatible with the original definition given in
3.1
. Applying
[
Ter23U
, Lemma 5.12]
as above, we see that
\mathscr{M}^{0}(\textup{Spec}(L))
can be canonically identified with the filtered
2
-colimit of abelian categories

2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U),

or with the analogous
2
-colimit over the cofinal subposet
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)\cap\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)
.

A priori, the categories of perverse Nori motives over function fields just introduced are not categories of motives over a field in Nori’s sense; this might give rise to some confusion.
As a concrete example, suppose that the field extension
L/k
is finite, so that the
k
-scheme
\textup{Spec}(L)
belongs to
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
; fix a field embedding
\Sigma:L\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extending the original embedding
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
Then we have two distinct ways to define an abelian category of perverse Nori motives out of
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\textup{Spec}(L),\mathbb{Q})
: on the one side, as the category
\mathscr{M}(\textup{Spec}(L))
obtained via
\sigma
(by considering
\textup{Spec}(L)
as a
k
-variety); on the other side, as the category
\mathscr{M}(L)
obtained via
\Sigma
(by considering
L
as the base field).
It is natural to expect these two recipes to define the same abelian category.

In the following proposition, we check that this in indeed the case; in fact, the same independence result holds for arbitrary finitely generated field extensions of
k
.

Proposition 1.15
.

Let
L/k
be a finitely generated field extension, and assume that there exists a field embedding
\Sigma:L\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extending the original embedding
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
Then the two exact functors

\beta_{\textup{Spec}(L),\sigma}^{+}:\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}(L))\rightarrow\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}},\qquad\beta_{L,\Sigma}^{+}:\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}(L))\rightarrow\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}

(resp.
\beta_{\textup{Spec}(L),\sigma}^{0,+}:\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}^{0}(L))\rightarrow\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}},\qquad\beta_{L,\Sigma}^{0,+}:\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}^{0}(L))\rightarrow\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}
)

have the same kernel. In particular, the category
\mathscr{M}(\textup{Spec}(L))
(resp.
\mathscr{M}^{0}(\textup{Spec}(L))
) is canonically equivalent to
\mathscr{M}(L)
(resp.
\mathscr{M}^{0}(L)
).

Proof.

For sake of simplicity, we only write down the argument for the categories
\mathscr{M}(\textup{Spec}(L))
and
\mathscr{M}(L)
; the argument for the categories
\mathscr{M}^{0}(\textup{Spec}(L))
and
\mathscr{M}^{0}(L)
can be obtained by easily adapting the former, and we leave it to the interested reader.

In the first place, we describe the key geometric construction behind the proof. Fix one variety
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
with function field
L
, and write

\textup{Spec}(L)=\varprojlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)}U

as the limit in the category of
k
-schemes over all smooth non-empty open subsets of
S
. For every
U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
, the canonical morphism of
k
-schemes
\eta_{U}:\textup{Spec}(L)\rightarrow U
is the inclusion of the generic point; the induced monomorphism of sheaves of
k
-algebras

\mathscr{O}_{U}\xrightarrow{\eta_{U}^{*}}L\xrightarrow{\Sigma}\mathbb{C}

determines a complex point
z_{U,\Sigma}\in U^{\sigma}
which, by construction, does not lie on any proper algebraic
k
-subvariety of
U
. In fact, for every inclusion
V\subset U
in
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
, the induced inclusion of complex-analytic spaces
V^{\sigma}\subset U^{\sigma}
identifies
z_{V,\Sigma}
with
z_{U,\Sigma}
.
By construction, for every
U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(U,\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(L,\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}(U,\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{U}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{U,\sigma}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{L,\Sigma}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{U,\Sigma}^{*}}

is commutative up to natural isomorphism. In fact, we have a canonical natural isomorphism

z_{U,\Sigma}^{*}\circ\mathsf{Bti}_{U,\sigma}^{*}=\mathsf{Bti}_{L,\Sigma}^{*}\circ\eta_{U}^{*}

as functors on the entire category of étale motives
\mathbf{DA}^{\textup{ét}}(U,\mathbb{Q})
: this extends the identification of complex-analytic spaces

X^{\sigma}\times_{U^{\sigma}}z_{U,\Sigma}=(X\times_{U}\textup{Spec}(L))^{\Sigma},

which holds functorially in
X\in\mathsf{Sm}/U
; this fact was observed in the proof of
[
Ayo14H2
, Prop. 2.20]
.
From the compatibility of the complex points
z_{U,\Sigma}
under restriction, we deduce that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(U,\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(L,\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}D^{b}_{c}(U,\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{Bti}_{U,\sigma}^{*})_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{L,\Sigma}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z_{U,\Sigma}^{*})_{U}}

is commutative up to canonical natural isomorphism as well. Applying the perverse truncation functors, we deduce a commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(U,\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(L,\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}D^{b}_{c}(U,\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathsf{Perv}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{Bti}_{U,\sigma}^{*})_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{L,\Sigma}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z_{U}^{*})_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{p}H^{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z_{U,\Sigma}^{\dagger})_{U}}

where the lower-left horizontal arrow is the equivalence induced by the inclusions
\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(U)\subset\mathsf{Perv}(U)
for
U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
, while the lower-right horizontal arrow is defined by the exact shifted inverse image functors

(30)

z_{U,\Sigma}^{\dagger}:=z_{U,\Sigma}^{*}[-\dim(U)]:\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(U)\rightarrow\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Passing to the abelian hulls, we obtain a commutative diagram of the form

(31)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{A}(2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(U,\mathbb{Q}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(L,\mathbb{Q}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\textup{Spec}(L),\sigma}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{L,\Sigma}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z_{U,\Sigma}^{\dagger})_{U}}

Proving the thesis amounts to showing that the equivalence in the upper horizontal arrow identifies the kernels of the two vertical exact functors.

Let us first explain how to prove this in the simple case where the field extension
L/k
is geometrically integral. In this case, each
U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
is geometrically connected, and so the associated complex-analytic space
U^{\sigma}
is connected as well. This implies that the exact functors (
30
) are all faithful. Therefore the same holds for the lower horizontal arrow in (
31
). But then it follows formally that the two vertical exact functors in (
31
) have the same kernel; compare with the proof of
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 1.10]
.

If the field extension
L/k
is not assumed to be geometrically integral, the argument is similar but slightly more involved: the issue is that the exact functors (
30
) are no longer necessarily faithful, since the complex-analytic spaces
U^{\sigma}
are no longer necessarily connected.
To remedy this issue, the rough idea is to consider multiple complex embeddings of
L
at once so that te corresponding points
z_{U,\Sigma}
hit all connected components of
U^{\sigma}
. In order to make this idea work, we need to describe the construction of the points
z_{U,\Sigma}
in more explicit geometric terms. To this end, choose a transcendence basis
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
for
L/k
with the property that we can write
L=k(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})[x]/P(x)
where
P(x)\in k(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})[x]
is a monic polynomial in
x
with coefficients in
k[t_{1},\dots,t_{n}]
defining a smooth subvariety inside the affine space
\mathbf{A}^{n+1}_{k}
with coordinates
t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x
.
To achieve this, it suffices to apply the strong form of Noether’s Normalization Lemma to some chosen affine
U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
(this is legitimate, since the base field
k
is infinite). In this way, we get an identification

U=\textup{Spec}(k[t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x]/P(x))

as closed subvarieties of
\mathbf{A}^{n+1}_{k}
. Note that, by construction,
P(x)
is irreducible in
k(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})[x]
.

For sake of brevity, write
L_{0}:=k(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
; write also
\Sigma_{0}:=\Sigma|_{L_{0}}:L_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, and let
\Sigma_{1},\dots,\Sigma_{\deg(P)}
denote the
\deg(P)
distinct complex embeddings of
L
extending
\Sigma_{0}
(among which figures the original embedding
\Sigma
).
Moreover, write
P^{\sigma}(x)
for the image of
P(x)
in
\mathbb{C}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})[x]
via
\sigma
, and let

P^{\sigma}(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{r}Q_{i}(x)

be its factorization into irreducible polynomials.
Note that, since the coefficients of
P^{\sigma}(X)
belong to the unique factorization domain
\mathbb{C}[t_{1},\dots,t_{n}]
, the same holds for the coefficients of each factor
Q_{i}(x)
. Thus each
Q_{i}(x)
defines a connected closed subvariety
U_{i}
of
\mathbf{A}^{n+1}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and the complex-analytic space
U^{\sigma}
breaks into connected components as

U^{\sigma}=U_{1}\amalg\dots\amalg U_{\deg(P)}.

This implies, in particular, that each complex variety
U_{i}
is smooth, since so is
U^{\sigma}
.
Moreover, write
P^{\Sigma_{0}}(x)
for the image of
P(x)
in
\mathbb{C}[x]
via
\Sigma_{0}
, and consider the factorization

P^{\Sigma_{0}}(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\bar{Q}_{i}(x),

where
\bar{Q}_{i}(x)
denotes the image of
Q_{i}(x)
in
\mathbb{C}[x]
via the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra homomorphism

\mathbb{C}[t_{1},\dots,t_{n}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\quad t_{i}\mapsto\Sigma_{0}(t_{i}).

The complex embeddings
\Sigma_{1},\dots,\Sigma_{\deg(P)}
extending
\Sigma_{0}
are in natural bijection with the complex roots of
P^{\Sigma_{0}}(x)
: giving such an embedding
\Sigma^{\prime}
amounts to choosing a factor
Q_{i}(x)
of
P^{\sigma}(x)
and specifying a complex root of
\bar{Q}_{i}(x)
; by construction, the corresponding complex point
z_{U,\Sigma^{\prime}}\in U^{\sigma}
belongs to the connected component
U_{i}
. Thus the complex points

z_{U,\Sigma_{1}},\dots,z_{U,\Sigma_{\deg(P)}}

jointly hit every connected component of
U^{\sigma}
. Similarly, for each dense open subset
V\subset U
, the points
z_{U,\Sigma_{i}}
restrict to a family of complex points
z_{V,\Sigma_{i}}\in V^{\sigma}
which jointly hit every connected component of
V^{\sigma}
.

Finally, we are ready to prove the thesis. Consider the commutative diagram

(32)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{A}(2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(U,\mathbb{Q}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(L,\mathbb{Q}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=1}^{\deg(P)}\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\textup{Spec}(L),\sigma}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta_{L,\Sigma_{i}}^{+})_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z_{U,\Sigma_{i}}^{\dagger})_{U,i}}

obtained by tying together the individual diagrams of shape (
31
) for the embeddings
\Sigma_{1},\dots,\Sigma_{\deg(P)}
. For every dense open subset
V\subset U
, the collection of exact shifted inverse image functors

z_{V,\Sigma_{1}}^{\dagger},\dots,z_{V,\Sigma_{\deg(P)}}^{\dagger}:\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(V)\rightarrow\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}

is jointly faithful, precisely because the points
z_{V,\Sigma_{i}}
hit every connected component of
V^{\sigma}
. Therefore the the lower horizontal arrow in (
32
) is a faithful exact functor as well. As in the geometrically connected case, this implies formally that the two vertical exact functors in (
32
) have the same kernel.
In order to conclude, it now suffices to show that the right-most vertical arrow in the two diagrams (
31
) and (
32
) have the same kernel. Equivalently, we need to show that the various exact functors

\beta_{L,\Sigma_{1}}^{+},\dots,\beta_{L,\Sigma_{\deg(P)}}^{+}:\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(L,\mathbb{Q}))\rightarrow\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}

all have the same kernel. This follows from the fact that the induced exact functors

\beta_{L,\Sigma_{1}}^{+},\dots,\beta_{L,\Sigma_{\deg(P)}}^{+}:\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(L,\mathbb{Q}))\rightarrow\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}\xrightarrow{-\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}L}\textup{vect}_{L}

are all isomorphic to each other: indeed, the homological functors

\beta_{L,\Sigma_{1}},\dots,\beta_{L,\Sigma_{\deg(P)}}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(L,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}\xrightarrow{-\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}L}\textup{vect}_{L}

are all isomorphic to the algebraic de Rham realization of Voevodsky motives via Huber’s construction of mixed realizations from
[
Hub00
]
.
∎

Remark 1.16
.

Note that both the proof of
1.11
above and the final part of the proof of
1.15
use crucially the existence of comparison isomorphisms relating the various Betti cohomology theories associated to different complex embeddings of the base fields up to extension of scalars. To the author’s knowledge, the only way to obtain such a comparison is via some other cohomology theory, defined independently of any such embedding, which compares to all Betti cohomology theories at once. As far as the argument is concerned, it is essentially irrelevant which particular auxiliary cohomology is chosen.

In the proof of
1.15
, we chose to use de Rham cohomology instead of
\ell
-adic cohomology since both the definition and the comparison with Betti cohomology are much deeper results for the latter than for the former.
The proof of
1.11
is based on the same argument as
[
IM19
, Prop. 6.11]
, where the authors use
\ell
-adic cohomology and not de Rham cohomology. Note, however, that if one is only interested in the comparison results of
[
IM19
, Prop. 6.11]
for different Betti realizations, one can use the de Rham realization in place of the
\ell
-adic realization, since the latter is actually used as a comparison tool only over
k
-varieties of dimension
0
.

Note that, in general, it might be simply impossible to extend the original embedding
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
to a given function field
L/k
(for instance if
k=\mathbb{C}
and
\sigma=\textup{id}_{\mathbb{C}}
). In order to exploit
1.15
to study the abelian category
\mathscr{M}(\textup{Spec}(L))
as a genuine category of Nori motives over
L
, we need to make sure that we can always reduce to the case where such an embedding extension is possible.

In fact, as we explain now, we can always reduce to the case where
L
(or equivalently,
k
) is finitely generated over
\mathbb{Q}
. The next result is a consequence of
1.15
, applied not directly to the original field extension
L/k
but rather to the induced extensions between suitable finitely generated subfields thereof.

Corollary 1.17
.

Let
L/k
be a finitely generated field extension. Let
\mathscr{S}_{L}
denote the filtered poset of all subfields of
L
which are finitely generated over
\mathbb{Q}
; for each
F\in\mathscr{S}_{L}
, choose an embedding
\Sigma_{F}:F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extending the restriction
\sigma|_{F\cap k}:F\cap k\subset k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

Then, as
F
varies in
\mathscr{S}_{L}
, the abelian categories
\mathscr{M}(F)
(resp.
\mathscr{M}^{0}(F)
) defined via the chosen embeddings
\Sigma_{F}
canonically assemble into an abelian fibered category over
\mathscr{S}_{L}^{op}
, and we have a canonical equivalence

\mathscr{M}(L)=2-\varinjlim_{F\in\mathscr{S}_{L}}\mathscr{M}(F)

\textup{(resp. $\mathscr{M}^{0}(L)=2-\varinjlim_{F\in\mathscr{S}_{L}}\mathscr{M}^{0}(F)$)}.

Proof.

Again, we only give the argument for the categories
\mathscr{M}(L)
and
\mathscr{M}(F)
; the argument for the categories
\mathscr{M}^{0}(L)
and
\mathscr{M}^{0}(F)
can be obtained by easily adapting the former and is left to the interested reader.

For any fixed
F\in\mathscr{S}_{L}
, and any choice of two complex embeddings
\Sigma_{F}^{(1)},\Sigma_{F}^{(2)}:F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, the abelian categories
\mathscr{M}(F_{1},\Sigma_{F}^{(1)})
and
\mathscr{M}(F_{2},\Sigma_{F}^{(2)})
coincide, as a consequence of Huber’s construction of mixed realizations; this was already noted in the proof of
[
IM19
, Prop. 6.11]
.
Therefore, for every inclusion
F_{1}\subset F_{2}
in
\mathscr{S}_{L}
, we have a well-defined exact functor

\mathscr{M}(F_{1}):=\mathscr{M}(F_{1},\Sigma_{F_{1}})=\mathscr{M}(F_{1},\Sigma_{F_{2}}|_{F_{1}})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(\textup{Spec}(F_{2}),\Sigma_{F_{2}}|_{F_{1}})=\mathscr{M}(F_{2},\Sigma_{F_{2}})=:\mathscr{M}(F_{2}),

where the last equivalence is given by
1.15
.
It is easy to see that these functors are compatible with composition of inclusions in
\mathscr{S}_{L}
, thereby defining an abelian fibered category as stated.

The proof of the last assertion is not difficult but a bit lengthy, since it requires us to describe everything in more explicit geometric terms, similarly to what done in the proof of
1.15
.
To begin with, fix some
S\in\mathsf{Aff}_{k}
with function field
L
, as done at the beginning of
1.14
; for the present argument it is not relevant whether
S
is smooth or not. Applying the strong form of Noether’s Normalization Lemma, we can write

S=\textup{Spec}(k[t_{1},\dots,t_{n}][x]/P(x))

where
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
form a transcendence basis for
L/k
and
P(x)\in k[t_{1},\dots,t_{n}][x]
is an irreducible monic polynomial in
x
.
From now on, we regard
S
as a closed subvariety of the affine space
\mathbf{A}^{n+1}_{k}
with coordinates
t_{1},\dots,t_{n},x
, and we tacitly identify the function field
L:=k(S)
with
k(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})[x]/P(x)
.

For notational convenience, let
k_{0}
denote the smallest subfield of
k
such that
P(x)\in k_{0}[t_{1},\dots,t_{n}][x]
; by construction,
k_{0}
is finitely generated over
\mathbb{Q}
.
Consider the filtered poset
\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}
consisting of all subfields of
k
which are finitely generated over
\mathbb{Q}
and contain
k_{0}
, and let
\mathscr{S}_{L/k_{0}}\subset\mathscr{S}_{L}
denote the cofinal subposet consisting of fields of the form
k^{\prime}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})/P(x)
with
k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}
; we thus have an obvious map of posets
\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{L/k_{0}}
, and we write the image of a typical element
k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}
under it as
F^{\prime}
.

For each
k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}
, let
\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}_{k^{\prime}}(S)\subset\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)
denote the subposet consisting of all affine open subsets of
S
which are defined over
k^{\prime}
: by definition, an open subset
U\in\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)
belongs to
\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}_{k^{\prime}}(S)
if and only if the closed subvariety
S\setminus U\subset\mathbf{A}^{n+1}_{k}
is defined over
k^{\prime}
, which means that the associated ideal
\mathbb{I}(S\setminus U)\subset k[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
satisfies the equality

\mathbb{I}(S\setminus U)=(\mathbb{I}(S\setminus U)\cap k^{\prime}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}])\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k.

Note that the definition of
\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}_{k^{\prime}}(S)
depends on the chosen presentation of
S
as a closed subvariety of
\mathbf{A}^{n+1}_{k}
(for instance, it is not stable under
k
-automorphisms of
\mathbf{A}^{n+1}_{k}
).
Nevertheless, since every closed subvariety of
\mathbf{A}^{n+1}_{k}
is defined over some field finitely generated over
\mathbb{Q}
, we have the filtered union

(33)

\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)=\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k^{\prime}/k_{0}}}\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}_{k^{\prime}}(S).

For each
k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}
, we let
S_{(k^{\prime})}
denote the closed subvariety of
\mathbf{A}^{n+1}_{k^{\prime}}
defined by the polynomial
P(x)\in k^{\prime}[t_{1},\dots,t_{n}][x]
.
Similarly, for every
U\in\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}_{k^{\prime}}(S)
, we let
U_{(k^{\prime})}
denote the corresponding affine
k^{\prime}
-variety: its closed complement
S_{(k^{\prime})}\setminus U_{(k^{\prime})}
is the closed subvariety of
\mathbf{A}^{n}_{k^{\prime}}
with associated ideal
\mathbb{I}(S\setminus U)\cap k^{\prime}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
.
Note that, by construction, every
U\in\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)
can be written as a filtered inverse limit with affine transition maps

U=\varprojlim_{k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}^{op}}U_{(k^{\prime})}

in the category of schemes. Therefore, by
[
Ayo14E
, Cor. 3.22]
, we have a canonical equivalence

2-\varinjlim_{k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k^{\prime}/k_{0}}^{op}}\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(U_{(k^{\prime})},\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(U,\mathbb{Q}).

Note also that, for
k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}
and
U\in\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}_{k^{\prime}}(S)
, we have a canonical identification of complex-analytic spaces

U_{(k^{\prime})}^{\sigma|_{k^{\prime}}}=U^{\sigma},

and the resulting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(U_{(k^{\prime})},\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(U,\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}(U_{(k^{\prime})}^{\sigma|_{k^{\prime}}},\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}(U^{\sigma},\mathbb{Q})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{U_{(k^{\prime})},\sigma|_{k^{\prime}}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{U,\sigma}^{*}}

is commutative up to natural isomorphism. In fact, we have a canonical natural isomorphism

\mathsf{Bti}_{U_{(k^{\prime})},\sigma_{k^{\prime}}}^{*}=\mathsf{Bti}_{U,\sigma}^{*}

as functors on the entire category of étale motives
\mathbf{DA}^{\textup{ét}}(U_{(k^{\prime})},\mathbb{Q})
: this extends the identification of complex-analytic spaces

X^{\sigma_{k^{\prime}}}=(X\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k)^{\sigma},

which holds functorially in
X\in\mathsf{Sm}/{U_{(k^{\prime})}}
. Passing to the colimit over
\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}^{op}
, and applying
[
Ter23U
, Lemma 5.12]
, this yields a canonical equivalence

(34)

2-\varinjlim_{k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}}\mathscr{M}(U_{(k^{\prime})},\sigma|_{k^{\prime}})=\mathscr{M}(U,\sigma),

which is clearly compatible with restrictions under inclusions in
\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)
. Lastly, note that, for every
k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}
, the obvious inclusion of posets
\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}_{k^{\prime}}(S)\subset\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S_{(k^{\prime})})
is cofinal.

Putting all these observation together, we obtain the chain of equivalences

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}(L)=

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}(\textup{Spec}(L),\sigma)

by
1.15

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathscr{M}(U,\sigma)

by definition

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2-\varinjlim_{k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}}2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}_{k^{\prime}}(S)}\mathscr{M}(U,\sigma)

by (
33
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2-\varinjlim_{k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}}2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}_{k^{\prime}}(S)}\mathscr{M}(U_{(k^{\prime})},\sigma|_{k^{\prime}})

by (
34
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2-\varinjlim_{k\in\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}}2-\varinjlim_{V\in\mathsf{Aff}\mathsf{Op}(S_{(k^{\prime})})}\mathscr{M}(V,\sigma|_{k^{\prime}})

by cofinality

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2-\varinjlim_{k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}}\mathscr{M}(\textup{Spec}(F^{\prime}),\sigma|_{k^{\prime}})

by definition

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2-\varinjlim_{k^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{k/k_{0}}}\mathscr{M}(F^{\prime},\Sigma_{F^{\prime}})

by
1.15

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2-\varinjlim_{F\in\mathscr{S}_{L}}\mathscr{M}(F,\Sigma_{F})

\displaystyle\textup{by cofinality}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
1.12
.

We claim that, as
S
varies in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the abelian subcategories
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
of
\mathscr{M}(S)
satisfy the conditions in the statement of
1.9
. This will imply the thesis.

The validity of condition (i) follows from the fact that all the exact functors involved are constructed via the method described in
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 2.5]
. Similarly, the validity of condition (ii) follows from the fact that all the natural transformations involved are constructed via the method described in
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 3.4]
.

Let us prove the validity of condition (iii): we have to show that, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the induced faithful exact functor

(35)

2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)\hookrightarrow 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathscr{M}(U)

is an equivalence.
Note that
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
-fibered categories
\mathscr{M}
and
\mathscr{M}^{0}
are stacks for the Zariski topology: for
\mathscr{M}
this result is proved in
[
IM19
, Prop. 2.7]
, while for
\mathscr{M}^{0}
the same result follows using condition (i).
As a consequence, in order to prove that (
35
) is an equivalence for a general
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, it suffices to treat each irreducible component of
S
separately; in other words, without loss of generality we may assume
S
irreducible.

In this situation, let
L:=k(S)
denote the function field of
S
. Then, by the discussion of
1.14
, the functor (
35
) can be identified with the canonical inclusion

\mathscr{M}^{0}(\textup{Spec}(L))\hookrightarrow\mathscr{M}(\textup{Spec}(L))

and, by
1.17
, the latter can be identified with the functor

2-\varinjlim_{F\in\mathscr{S}_{L}^{op}}\mathscr{M}^{0}(F)\hookrightarrow 2-\varinjlim_{F\in\mathscr{S}_{L}^{op}}\mathscr{M}(F)

induced by the inclusions of categories of Nori motives

(36)

\mathscr{M}^{0}(F)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{M}(F)

over every finitely generated subfield
F\subset L
; here, the category
\mathscr{M}(F)
is defined in terms of a complex embedding
\Sigma_{F}:F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extending
\sigma|_{F\cap k}
. Therefore, in order to conclude, it suffices to show that the functors (
36
) are all equivalences.
So fix a field
F
finitely generated over
\mathbb{Q}
.
By
[
BVHP20
, Prop. 3.8]
, in order to prove that (
36
) is an equivalence, it suffices to show that, up to natural isomorphism, the Betti realization

\mathsf{Bti}_{F,\Sigma_{F}}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(F,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}(\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}})

factors through the functor

\iota^{0}_{F,\Sigma_{F}}:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(F))\rightarrow D^{b}(\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}).

The latter claim was established by Nori; see
[
CG17
, Prop. 7.1]
or
[
Har16
, Thm. 7.4.17]
for a detailed proof.
∎

2.
Construction of the external tensor structure

In this section we start constructing and studying the tensor structure on perverse motives. The final outcome of our effort will be the following theorem:

Theorem 2.1
.

The stable homotopy
2
-functor
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
on
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
is canonically unitary symmetric monoidal in the sense of
[
Ayo07a
, Defn. 2.3.1]
. Moreover, the morphism of stable homotopy
2
-functors

\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))

is canonically unitary symmetric monoidal in the sense of
[
Ayo10
, Defn. 3.2]
.

The complete proof of this result will be obtained only at the very end of
Section

4
, as a combination of several partial results that we will collect along the way. More precisely, the results of the present section prove everything except for the existence of the motivic unit constraint and its compatibility with the classical unit constraint on
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
; this is the subject of
Section

4
and requires the technical discussion developed in
Section

3
.

After discussing some general preliminaries about multi-linear functors and natural transformations thereof, the central part of the present section is devoted to constructing the single external tensor product functors on perverse motives as well as the monoidality isomorphisms for inverse image functors. In the last part, we will construct the associativity and commutativity constraints and show that they are compatible in the expected way.

2.1.
Categorical preliminaries

Several constructions in Ivorra and Morel’s paper
[
IM19
]
are based on a technical recognition principle for functors and natural transformations in the constructible derived categories: given two varieties
T,S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
and a triangulated functor
F:D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(T,\mathbb{Q})
which is
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structures, one wants to have a criterion guaranteeing that
F
coincides with the trivial derived functor of its restriction to the perverse hearts

F:\mathsf{Perv}(S)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(T)

under Beilinson’s equivalences. More abstractly, given two abelian categories
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
and a triangulated functor
F:D^{b}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{B})
which is
t
-exact for the obvious
t
-structures, one wants to have a criterion to decide whether
F
coincides with the trivial derived functor of its restriction to the hearts

F:\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}.

This is not always the case, due to the technical defects of triangulated categories. However, by
[
Vol10
, Thm. 1]
, in order for this to hold it suffices that
F
admit a dg-enhancement; a similar result holds for triangulated natural transformations between
t
-exact triangulated functors. This is summarized in
[
IM19
, Prop. 4.4]
.

We are going to apply the same recognition principle to the case of multi-linear functors and natural transformations thereof. Before stating the precise result, let us spell out what we mean by multi-triangulated functors and natural transformations thereof.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\mathscr{D}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{D}_{n}
and
\mathscr{E}
be triangulated categories.

(1)

A
multi-triangulated functor
from
\mathscr{D}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathscr{D}_{n}
to
\mathscr{E}
is a functor

F:\mathscr{D}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathscr{D}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}

which is functorially triangulated separately in each variable in such a way that, for every choice of
i,j\in\left\{1,\dots,n\right\}
with
i<j
, the diagram of functors
\mathscr{D}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathscr{D}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{i}^{\bullet}[1],\dots,A_{j}^{\bullet}[1],\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{i}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{j}^{\bullet}[1],\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet})[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{i}^{\bullet}[1],\dots,A_{j}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet})[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{i}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{j}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet})[2]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

is anti-commutative.

(2)

A
multi-triangulated natural transformation
between
F
and
G
is a natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{D}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathscr{D}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}

\alpha:F\rightarrow G

which is triangulated separately in each variable in the sense that, for every choice of
i\in\left\{1,\dots,n\right\}
, the diagram of functors
\mathscr{D}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathscr{D}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{i}^{\bullet}[1],\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{i}^{\bullet}[1],\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{i}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet})[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{i}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet})[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

is commutative.

In order to motivate the definition, we quickly describe the fundamental example given by derived functors of multi-exact functors. In order to introduce it, we need some ad hoc notation about multi-dimensional complexes:

Notation 2.3
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be an abelian category. For every integer
n\geq 1
, we use the following notation:

•

We regard
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
as a poset with the order relation defined by

(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\leq(i^{\prime}_{1},\dots,i^{\prime}_{n})\iff i_{r}\leq i^{\prime}_{r}\;\;\forall r=1,\dots,n.

•

We let
\square^{b}_{n}(\mathscr{A})
denote the category of
n
-dimensional bounded complexes with values in
\mathscr{A}
: it is the full subcategory of the functor category
\mathbf{Fun}(\mathbb{Z}^{n},\mathscr{A})
consisting of those functors
A^{\square}=(A^{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}
such that:

(i)

For every
(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and every
r=1,\dots,n
, the composite morphism

A^{i_{1},\dots,i_{r},\dots,i_{n}}\rightarrow A^{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}+1,\dots,i_{n}}\rightarrow A^{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}+2,\dots,i_{n}}

vanishes.

(ii)

There exists
N\geq 0
(depending on
A^{\bullet}
) such that
A^{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}=0
whenever
|i_{1}|+\dots+|i_{n}|>N
.

•

We let

\textup{Tot}^{\square}:\square^{b}_{n}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\square^{b}_{1}(\mathscr{A})=C^{b}(\mathscr{A})

denote the total complex functor with respect to a fixed choice of signs.

We are now able to state the fundamental example concisely as follows:

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\mathscr{A}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{A}_{n}
and
\mathscr{B}
be abelian categories, and let
F:\mathscr{A}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathscr{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
be a multi-exact functor. Then the formula

F(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet}):=\textup{Tot}^{\square}(F(A_{n}^{i_{1}},\dots,A_{n}^{i_{n}})_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})

canonically defines a multi-triangulated functor

F:D^{b}(\mathscr{A}_{1})\times\dots\times D^{b}(\mathscr{A}_{n})\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{B}).

Proof.

If
n=2
, the classical computation in the case where
F
is the tensor product functor in the category of vector spaces over some field carries over to the general framework described in the statement. The same computation also works for
n>2
. We omit the details.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\mathscr{A}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{A}_{n}
and
\mathscr{B}
be abelian categories, and let
F,G:\mathscr{A}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathscr{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
be two multi-exact functors. Suppose that we are given a natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{A}_{1}\times\dots\mathscr{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}

\alpha:F(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})\rightarrow G(A_{1},\dots,A_{n}).

Then the formula

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet}):}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Tot}^{\square}(F(A_{1}^{i_{1}},\dots,A_{n}^{i_{n}})_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Tot}^{\square}(G(A_{1}^{i_{1}},\dots,A_{n}^{i_{n}})_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\kappa(A_{1}^{i_{1}},\dots,A_{n}^{i_{n}})_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}})}

defines a multi-triangulated natural transformation between functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{A}_{1})\times\dots\times D^{b}(\mathscr{A}_{n})\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{B})

\alpha:F(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet})\rightarrow G(A_{1}^{\bullet},\dots,A_{n}^{\bullet})

Moreover, this construction is compatible with identities of functors as well as with composition of natural transformations; in particular, it preserves invertibility of natural transformations.

Proof.

The standard computation in the case where
F
and
G
are suitable combinations of tensor product functors in the category of vector spaces over some field carries over to the general setting described in the statement. We omit the details.
∎

Finally, we can state the aforementioned generalization of
[
Vol10
, Thm. 1]
to the multi-linear setting as follows:

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
\mathscr{A}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{A}_{n}
and
\mathscr{B}
be abelian categories. The following statements hold:

(1)

Let
F:D^{b}(\mathscr{A}_{1})\times\dots D^{b}(\mathscr{A}_{n})\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{B})
be a multi-triangulated functor which is
t
-exact in each variable for the obvious
t
-structures, and suppose that it can be enhanced to a dg-quasifunctor.

Then
F
is canonically isomorphic to the functor obtained from the induced multi-exact functor

F:\mathscr{A}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathscr{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}

via the construction of
2.4
.

(2)

Let
F,G:D^{b}(\mathscr{A}_{1})\times\dots\times D^{b}(\mathscr{A}_{n})\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{B})
be two multi-triangulated functors which are
t
-exact in each variable for the obvious
t
-structures, and suppose that they can be enhanced to dg-quasifunctors; fix one such enhancement. Moreover, let
\alpha:F\rightarrow G
be a multi-triangulated natural transformation, and suppose that it can be enhanced to a morphism of dg-quasifunctors.

Then
\alpha
coincides with the canonical natural transformation obtained from the induced natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{A}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathscr{A}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}

\alpha:F\rightarrow G

via the construction of
2.5
.

Proof.

For sake of simplicity, we only sketch the argument in the case
n=2
.

(1)

For every fixed object
A_{1}\in\mathscr{A}_{1}
, the induced triangulated functor

F(A_{1},-):D^{b}(\mathscr{A}_{2})\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{B})

it
t
-exact by hypothesis and inherits a dg-enhancement from
F
. Applying
[
Vol10
, Thm. 1]
to it, we deduce that it coincides with the trivial derived functor of the induced exact functor

F(A_{1},-):\mathscr{A}_{2}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}.

Now, arguing by dévissage in the first variable, one obtains the analogous conclusion for
F
.

(2)

For every fixed object
A_{1}\in\mathscr{A}_{1}
, the induced natural transformation of triangulated functors
\mathscr{A}_{2}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}

\alpha(A_{1},-):F(A_{1},-)\rightarrow G(A_{1},-)

is triangulated by hypothesis and inherits a dg-enhancement from
\alpha
. Applying
[
Vol10
, Thm. 1]
to it, we deduce that it coincides with the canonical natural transformation induced by the natural transformation between functors
\mathscr{A}_{2}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}

\alpha(A_{1},-):F(A_{1},-)\rightarrow G(A_{1},-).

Again, arguing by dévissage in the first variable, one obtains the analogous conclusion for
\alpha
.

∎

Remark 2.7
.

In particular, since the construction of
2.5
is compatible with composition of natural transformations, the result of
2.6
(2) can be used to show the commutativity of natural diagrams of
t
-exact multi-triangulated functors by checking the commutativity of the induced diagram of multi-exact functors on the hearts of the various
t
-structures.

In the setting of perverse motives,
2.6
(1) asserts that, given varieties
S_{1},\dots,S_{n},T\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, every dg-enhanced multi-triangulated functor
F:D^{b}_{c}(S_{1},\mathbb{Q})\times\dots\times D^{b}_{c}(S_{n},\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(T,\mathbb{Q})
which is
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structures separately in each variable is canonically isomorphic to the multi-derived functor of its restriction

F:\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1})\times\dots\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{n})\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(T)

under Beilinson’s equivalences;
2.6
(2) asserts that the same holds for natural transformations between
t
-exact multi-triangulated functors.

All the multi-triangulated functors between constructible derived categories that we will need to consider are suitable combinations of three types of functors: shifted inverse images under smooth equidimensional morphisms, direct images under closed immersions, and external tensor product functors.

2.2.
External tensor structure

We can now start constructing the monoidal structure on perverse motives. As explained in the introduction of the paper, the lack of
t
-exactness of the usual internal tensor product functors forces one to work systematically with the external tensor product functors. The legitimacy of this approach is completely justified by the results of
[
Ter23T
]
, where we show that the entire theory of monoidal fibered categories can be canonically expressed in terms of the external tensor product: there exists a canonical dictionary relating the structure and properties of the internal tensor product to the structure and properties of the external tensor product. In the rest of this section we systematically adopt the language of
external tensor structures
introduced in
[
Ter23T
]
, which allows us to express our main constructions in a concise and precise form.

In fact, all the results of this section are proved for the abelian categories
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
defined in
3.1
; the corresponding results for the categories
\mathscr{M}(S)
will be simply deduced by transport of structure via the equivalences of
1.12
. This operation is legitimate because, as pointed out at the beginning of the proof of
1.12
, the categories
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
are stable under all the exact functors listed in the statement of
1.9
, and the structure of stable homotopy
2
-functor of
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
obtained in
[
IM19
]
is constructed using only these functors (together with the motivic Verdier duality functors
\mathbb{D}_{S}
, which also preserve the subcategories
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
).
Mostly for psychological reasons, we will use the more precise notation "
\mathscr{M}^{0}
" in place of "
\mathscr{M}
" whenever developing construction or proofs where it is necessary to use the alternative description of perverse motives. However, as in the case of
2.1
above, we will adopt the usual notation "
\mathscr{M}
" in the formulation of our main results.

Our first task is to construct the single external tensor product functors together with external monoidality isomorphisms witnessing their compatibility with inverse image functors of perverse motives, in such a way that everything is compatible with the corresponding structure on the underlying categories of perverse sheaves. As always in the theory of Nori motives, the bulk of the construction is based on the general lifting principles for universal abelian factorizations; in this case, we will apply the specific lifting results for external tensor structures on abelian fibered categories as summarized in
[
Ter23U
, § 6]
. Even if general inverse image functors of perverse motives are definitely not exact, the results of
[
Ter23F
]
can be used to reduce the sought-after structure to a minimal amount of data involving only inverse images under smooth morphisms and direct images under closed immersions, which are always exact on perverse motives (up to dividing into connected components and shifting appropriately).
Our first intermediate result towards
2.1
can be stated as follows:

Proposition 2.8
.

The triangulated
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
carries a canonical triangulated external tensor structure in the sense of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 2.6]
. Moreover, the morphism of
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered categories
\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
carries a canonical external tensor structure in the sense of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 8.5]
.

The basic input from the theory of triangulated motives is the existence of a canonical external tensor structure on the
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered categories
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
and
D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
related by a canonical external tensor structure on the Betti realization

\mathsf{Bti}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q}).

Under the canonical dictionary of
[
Ter23T
]
, they correspond to the usual monoidal structures constructed as part of the six functor formalism in
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
]
and
[
Ayo10
]
.

Notation 2.9
.

From now on, we adopt the following notation:

•

For every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, we use the following notation:

–

We let
\theta=\theta_{f}:f^{*}\circ\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathsf{Bti}_{T}\circ f^{*}
denote the
\mathsf{Bti}^{*}
-transition isomorphism along
f
constructed in
[
Ayo10
]
.

–

We let
\tilde{\theta}=\tilde{\theta}_{f}:f^{*}\circ\iota_{S}\xrightarrow{\sim}\iota_{T}\circ f^{*}
denote the
\iota
-transition isomorphism along
f
constructed in
[
IM19
, § 2.1]
.

•

For every closed immersion
z:Z\hookrightarrow S
in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, we use the following notation:

–

We let
\bar{\theta}=\bar{\theta}_{z}:\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}\circ z_{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}z_{*}\circ\mathsf{Bti}_{Z}^{*}
denote the natural isomorphism obtained as in
[
Ter23F
, § 1]
from
\theta_{z}
.

–

We let
\bar{\tilde{\theta}}=\bar{\tilde{\theta}}_{z}:\iota_{S}\circ z_{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}z_{*}\circ\iota_{Z}
denote the natural isomorphism obtained as in
[
Ter23F
, § 1]
from
\tilde{\theta}_{z}
.

•

We use the symbol
(\boxtimes,m;a,c)
for the canonical symmetric associative triangulated external tensor structures on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
and
D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
.

•

We let
\rho
denote the canonical triangulated external tensor structure on the morphism of triangulated
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered categories
\mathsf{Bti}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
.

The proof of
2.8
crucially exploits the general factorization techniques introduced in
[
Ter23F
]
: these are applied to the setting of fibered categories over
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
with respect to the subcategories
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
and
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl}
collecting smooth morphisms and the closed immersions, respectively (see
[
Ter23F
, Ex. 2.2]
).

In order to simplify the notation in some parts of the proofs, it is convenient to adopt the following notational convention:

Notation 2.10
.

Let
\mathbb{H}
be a triangulated
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category which is localic in the sense of
[
Ter23F
, Defn. 3.4]
.

•

Let
f:T\rightarrow S
be a morphism in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
such that the number
e:=\dim(\mathscr{O}_{T,t})-\dim(\mathscr{O}_{S,f(t)})
is independent of
t\in T
. We define the shifted inverse image functor

f^{\dagger}:=f^{*}[e]:\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T).

The two cases of interest for us are the following:

(i)

when
f
is a smooth morphism of constant relative dimension
e
;

(ii)

when
f
is a regular closed immersion of constant codimension
e
.

•

More generally, if
f:T\rightarrow S
is a morphism whose restriction to each connected component of
S
has the property described in the previous point, we define the shifted inverse image functor
f^{\dagger}
by extending the previous definition in the obvious way.

This notation is compatible with composition and interacts with connection isomorphisms and
R
-transition isomorphisms in the expected way.

Proof of
2.8
.

In the first place, fix two varieties
S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
and consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}}^{0}\times\beta_{S_{2}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}\times S_{2}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}

The natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S_{1},\mathbb{Q})\times\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S_{2},\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(S_{1}\times S_{2},\mathbb{Q})

\mathsf{Bti}_{S_{1}}^{*}(A_{1})\boxtimes\mathsf{Bti}_{S_{2}}^{*}(A_{2})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathsf{Bti}^{*}_{S_{1}\times S_{2}}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2})

restricts to a natural isomorphism between the two composite functors
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2})
. Applying
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 3.3]
, we obtain a canonical bi-exact functor

(37)

-\boxtimes-=-\boxtimes_{S_{1},S_{2}}-:\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2})

together with a canonical natural isomorphism of functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2})

(38)

\tilde{\rho}=\tilde{\rho}_{S_{1},S_{2}}:\iota_{S_{1}}(M_{1})\boxtimes\iota_{S_{2}}(M_{2})\xrightarrow{\sim}\iota_{S_{1}\times S_{2}}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2}).

By
2.4
and
2.5
, these extend to a bi-triangulated functor

(39)

-\boxtimes-:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{2}))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2}))

and a bi-triangulated natural isomorphism of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{2}))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2}))

(40)

\tilde{\rho}=\tilde{\rho}_{S_{1},S_{2}}:\iota_{S_{1}}(M_{1}^{\bullet})\boxtimes\iota_{S_{2}}(M_{2}^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\sim}\iota_{S_{1}\times S_{2}}(M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes M_{2}^{\bullet}).

In order to turn the collection of functors (
39
) into an external tensor structure on
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
, we have to construct the external monoidality isomorphisms with respect to inverse image functors in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
compatibly with composition. Once this is done, in order to check that the collection of natural transformations (
40
) defines an external tensor structure on the morphism
\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
, we have to show that they are compatible with the external monoidality isomorphisms on the two sides. If this holds, then the conservativity of
\iota
implies the validity of the projection formulae on
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
.

Therefore, as a consequence of the discussion in
[
Ter23T
, §§ 3, 5]
, we see that the sought-after external tensor structures on
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
and on
\iota
are completely determined by the underlying external tensor cores, as defined in
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 5.2, Defn. 5.6]
, and so we are reduced to the task of constructing the latter. To this end, we apply the results of
[
Ter23U
, § 6]
to the underlying triangulated
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
-fibered and
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl,op}
-fibered categories as follows:

•

Regard
\mathsf{Bti}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
as a morphism of
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
-fibered categories with respect to the shifted inverse image functors
p^{\dagger}
introduced in
2.10
. Using the additivity of relative dimensions with respect to direct products of morphisms, we see that the canonical external tensor structures on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
,
D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
and
\mathsf{Bti}^{*}
induce external tensor structures on the
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
-fibered categories considered. By restriction, these in turn induce analogous external tensor structures on the
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
-fibered categories
\mathscr{D}^{0}(\cdot)
and
\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot)
as well as on the morphism of
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
-fibered categories
\beta^{0}:\mathscr{D}^{0}(\cdot)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot)
. Applying
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 6.4]
, we obtain canonical external tensor structures on the abelian
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
-fibered category
\mathscr{M}^{0}(\cdot)
as well as on the morphism of abelian
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
-fibered categories
\iota:\mathscr{M}^{0}(\cdot)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot)
. Finally, applying
2.5
and shifting the inverse image functors back, the latter define triangulated external tensor structures on the triangulated
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
as well as on the triangulated morphism
\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
.

•

Regard
\mathsf{Bti}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
as a morphism of
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl,op}
-fibered categories with respect to the direct image functors
z_{*}
. Using
[
Ter23F
, Lemma 1.9, Lemma 1.13]
, we see that the canonical external tensor structures on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
,
D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
and
\mathsf{Bti}^{*}
induce external tensor structures on the
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl,op}
-fibered categories considered. By restriction, these in turn induce analogous external tensor structures on the
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl,op}
-fibered categories
\mathscr{D}^{0}(\cdot)
and
\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot)
as well as on the morphism of
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl,op}
-fibered categories
\beta^{0}:\mathscr{D}^{0}(\cdot)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot)
. Applying
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 6.4]
, we obtain canonical external tensor structures on the abelian
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl,op}
-fibered category
\mathscr{M}^{0}(\cdot)
as well as on the morphism of abelian
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl,op}
-fibered categories
\iota:\mathscr{M}^{0}(\cdot)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot)
. Finally, applying
2.5
and using again
[
Ter23F
, Lemma 1.9, Lemma 1.13]
, the latter define triangulated external tensor structures on the triangulated
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl,op}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
as well as on the triangulated morphism
\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
.

•

We have to show that the constructions of the previous two points satisfy the compatibility condition of
[
Ter23F
, Defn. 5.2]
and thus define an external tensor core on the
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
; since the validity of the conditions of
[
Ter23F
, Defn. 8.5]
has been already justified in the previous two points, we will be able to conclude that these two constructions also define an external tensor core on the morphism of
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered categories
\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
.

Let us then check the two conditions (C’-ETC) and (T-ETC) of
[
Ter23F
, Defn. 5.2]
are satisfied:

(C’-ETC)

Given two Cartesian squares in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}

where
p_{i}
(and hence

) is smooth while

(and hence

) is a closed immersion, we have to show that the diagram of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Z_{1}))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Z_{2}))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(P_{1}\penalty\ \times\penalty\ P_{2}))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}^{*}z_{1,*}M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes p_{2}^{*}z_{2,*}M_{2}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{*}(z_{1,*}M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes z_{2,*}M_{2}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{*}(z_{1}\times z_{2})_{*}(M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes M_{2}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{\prime}_{1,*}{p^{\prime}}_{1}^{*}M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes z^{\prime}_{2,*}{p^{\prime}}_{2}^{*}M_{2}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z^{\prime}_{1}\times z^{\prime}_{2})_{*}(p_{1}^{*}M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes p_{2}^{*}M_{2}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z^{\prime}_{1}\times z^{\prime}_{2})_{*}(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{*}(M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes M_{2}^{\bullet})}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\tilde{m}}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\tilde{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}

is commutative. After shifting the inverse image functors under smooth morphisms as in
2.10
, all the triangulated functors involved become
t
-exact for the obvious
t
-structures. Since all of them are obviously dg-enhanced, we are allowed to invoke
2.5
(see
2.7
) which reduces us to showing that the diagram of exact functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(Z_{1})\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(Z_{2})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(P_{1}\times P_{2})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}^{\dagger}z_{1,*}M_{1}\boxtimes p_{2}^{\dagger}z_{2,*}M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{\dagger}(z_{1,*}M_{1}\boxtimes z_{2,*}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{\dagger}(z_{1}\times z_{2})_{*}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{\prime}_{1,*}{p^{\prime}}_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes z^{\prime}_{2,*}{p^{\prime}}_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z^{\prime}_{1}\times z^{\prime}_{2})_{*}(p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z^{\prime}_{1}\times z^{\prime}_{2})_{*}(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{\dagger}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\tilde{m}}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\tilde{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}

is commutative. Since all the functors and natural transformations figuring in the latter diagram are obtained via the lifting principles for universal abelian factorizations, using
[
Ter23U
, Rem. 4.7(1)(2)]
we see that the sought-after commutativity reduces to the commutativity of the analogous diagram of functors on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
. After shifting back, the latter in turn reduces to the validity of axiom (C’-ETC) on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
, which we already know.

where
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
are smooth while
h_{i}
is a closed immersion (necessarily regular), we have to show that the diagram of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{2}))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Q_{1}\times Q_{2}))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}^{*}M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes q_{2}^{*}M_{2}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{1}\times q_{2})^{*}(M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes M_{2}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}^{*}p_{1}^{*}M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes h_{2}^{*}p_{2}^{*}M_{2}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}\times h_{2})^{*}(p_{1}^{*}M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes p_{2}^{*}M_{2}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}\times h_{2})^{*}(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{*}(M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes M_{2}^{\bullet})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}

is commutative. As before, we may assume that
p_{1},p_{2}
and
q_{1},q_{2}
are equidimensional. In this case, after shifting appropriately so that all the functors involved become
t
-exact for the obvious
t
-structures, we are reduced to showing that the diagram of exact functors

\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(Q_{1}\times Q_{2})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes q_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{1}\times q_{2})^{\dagger}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}^{\dagger}p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes h_{2}^{\dagger}p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}(p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{\dagger}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}

is commutative. Unfortunately, this cannot be deduced directly from general properties of universal abelian factorizations, since the inverse images under closed immersions on perverse motives constructed in
[
IM19
, § 4.1]
are not defined via the lifting principles of universal abelian factorizations. In any case, in order to prove the above diagram is commutative, it suffices to show that its image under the faithful exact functor
\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}(q_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes q_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}((q_{1}\times q_{2})^{\dagger}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}(h_{1}^{\dagger}p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes h_{2}^{\dagger}p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}((h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}(p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}((h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{\dagger}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}

is commutative, and this can be checked directly. Indeed, by inserting, on the left side of the latter diagram, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}(q_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes q_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}}(q_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1})\boxtimes\iota_{Q_{2}}(q_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{1}}(M_{1})\boxtimes q_{2}^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{2}}(M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}^{\dagger}p_{1}^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{1}}(M_{1})\boxtimes h_{2}^{\dagger}p_{2}^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{2}}(M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}(h_{1}^{\dagger}p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes h_{2}^{\dagger}p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}}(h_{1}^{\dagger}p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1})\boxtimes\iota_{Q_{2}}(h_{2}^{\dagger}p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}^{\dagger}\iota_{P_{1}}(p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1})\boxtimes h_{2}^{\dagger}\iota_{P_{2}}(p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}

and, on its right side, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{1}\times q_{2})^{\dagger}(\iota_{S_{1}}(M_{1})\boxtimes\iota_{S_{2}}(M_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{1}\times q_{2})^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{1}\times S_{2}}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}((q_{1}\times q_{2})^{\dagger}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}((h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{\dagger}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{\dagger}(\iota_{S_{1}}(M_{1})\boxtimes\iota_{S_{2}}(M_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{1}\times S_{2}}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}\iota_{P_{1}\times P_{2}}((p_{1}\times p_{2})^{\dagger}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

both of which are commutative by naturality and by axiom (mor-
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fib) of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 8.5]
, we are reduced to considering the outer part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}(q_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes q_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}((q_{1}\times q_{2})^{\dagger}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}}(q_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1})\boxtimes\iota_{Q_{2}}(q_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{1}\times q_{2})^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{1}\times S_{2}}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{1}}(M_{1})\boxtimes q_{2}^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{2}}(M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{1}\times q_{2})^{\dagger}(\iota_{S_{1}}(M_{1})\boxtimes\iota_{S_{2}}(M_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}^{\dagger}p_{1}^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{1}}(M_{1})\boxtimes h_{2}^{\dagger}p_{2}^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{2}}(M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}(p_{1}^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{1}}(M_{1})\boxtimes p_{2}^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{2}}(M_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{\dagger}(\iota_{S_{1}}(M_{1})\boxtimes\iota_{S_{2}}(M_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}^{\dagger}\iota_{P_{1}}(p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1})\boxtimes h_{2}^{\dagger}\iota_{P_{2}}(p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}(\iota_{P_{1}}(p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1})\boxtimes\iota_{P_{2}}(p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{\dagger}\iota_{S_{1}\times S_{2}}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}}(h_{1}^{\dagger}p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1})\boxtimes\iota_{Q_{2}}(h_{2}^{\dagger}p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}\iota_{P_{1}\times P_{2}}(p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}\iota_{P_{1}\times P_{2}}((p_{1}\times p_{2})^{\dagger}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}(h_{1}^{\dagger}p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes h_{2}^{\dagger}p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}((h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}(p_{1}^{\dagger}M_{1}\boxtimes p_{2}^{\dagger}M_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}((h_{1}\times h_{2})^{\dagger}(p_{1}\times p_{2})^{\dagger}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}

Here, the two four-term pieces are commutative by naturality, the three six-term pieces are commutative by axiom (mor-ETS) of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 3.6]
, and the central five-term piece (after shifting back) is commutative by the validity of axiom (T-ETC) on
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
.

This concludes the proof.

∎

2.3.
External associativity and commutativity constraints

We go on by equipping the external tensor core of
2.8
with compatible external associativity and commutativity constraints; for a general reference about these notions, see
[
Ter23T
, §§ 3–5]
and
[
Ter23F
, § 4]
. For sake of clarity, we first construct the two constraints separately and only check their compatibility in a second moment.

Lemma 2.11
.

The external tensor structure on
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
carries a canonical external associativity constraint in the sense of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 3.2]
, with respect to which the morphism of
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered categories
\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
is associative in the sense of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 8.13]
.

Proof.

In the first place, fix three varieties
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
and consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{2})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{2})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Downarrow}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{2})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{2}\times S_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{2})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{2}\times S_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{3}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-\boxtimes-)\times\textup{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}}^{0}\times\beta_{S_{2}}^{0}\times\beta^{0}_{S_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}\times S_{2}}^{0}\times\beta_{S_{3}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{3}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-\boxtimes-)\times\textup{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{id}\times(-\boxtimes-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}}^{0}\times\beta_{S_{2}}^{0}\times\beta_{S_{3}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}}^{0}\times\beta_{S_{2}\times S_{3}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{3}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{id}\times(-\boxtimes-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}

The associativity isomorphism of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{2})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{3})\rightarrow\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{3})

(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2})\boxtimes A_{3}\xrightarrow{\sim}A_{1}\boxtimes(A_{2}\boxtimes A_{3})

and the associativity isomorphisms of functors
\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{2})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{3})\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{3})

(K_{1}\boxtimes K_{2})\boxtimes K_{3}\xrightarrow{\sim}K_{1}\boxtimes(K_{2}\boxtimes K_{3})

satisfy the compatibility condition of
[
Ter23U
, Defn. 6.7(1)]
, because the same holds for the corresponding natural isomorphisms on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
and on
D^{b}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
- this is just a way of rephrasing the associativity of
\mathsf{Bti}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
. Therefore, applying
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 3.6]
, we obtain a canonical natural isomorphism between multi-exact functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{2})\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{3})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{3})

(41)

(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})\boxtimes M_{3}\xrightarrow{\sim}M_{1}\boxtimes(M_{2}\boxtimes M_{3})

compatible with the corresponding associativity isomorphism on
\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot)
. By
2.5
, this extends to a multi-triangulated natural isomorphism between functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{2}))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{3}))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{3}))

(42)

(M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes M_{2}^{\bullet})\boxtimes M_{3}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\sim}M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes(M_{2}^{\bullet}\boxtimes M_{3}^{\bullet})

again compatible with the corresponding associativity isomorphism on
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
.

As
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}
vary in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the natural isomorphisms (
42
) satisfy condition (
a
ETS-1) of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 3.2]
: to see this it suffices to check that the natural isomorphisms (
41
) satisfy the same condition, which in turn follows from the validity of axiom (
a
ETS-1) on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
.

By
[
Ter23F
, Lemma 5.5(1)]
, in order to conclude that the natural isomorphisms (
42
) define an associativity constraint for the
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
, it suffices to show that they define associativity constraints for the underlying
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
-fibered and
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl,op}
-fibered categories. By construction, the latter task reduces to checking that the natural isomorphisms (
41
) define associativity constraints for the abelian
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
-fibered and
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl,op}
-fibered category
\mathscr{M}(\cdot)
, which is indeed the case by
[
Ter23U
, Lemma 6.8(1)]
.

The associativity of the morphism
\iota
amounts to the compatibility between the natural isomorphisms (
42
) and the corresponding natural isomorphisms on
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
that we have already mentioned above.
∎

Lemma 2.12
.

The external tensor structure on
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
carries a canonical external commutativity constraint in the sense of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 4.2]
, with respect to which the morphism of
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered categories
\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
is symmetric in the sense of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 8.17]
.

Proof.

In the first place, fix two varieties
S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
and consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Downarrow}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{2})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{2}\times S_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{2})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{2}\times S_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}}^{0}\times\beta_{S_{2}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}\times S_{2}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leftrightarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}}^{0}\times\beta_{S_{2}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{2}}^{0}\times\beta_{S_{1}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{2}\times S_{1}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}\times S_{2}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leftrightarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}}

The commutativity isomorphisms of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{2}\times S_{1})

A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2}\xrightarrow{\sim}\tau^{*}(A_{2}\boxtimes A_{1})

and the commutativity isomorphism of functors
\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2})

K_{1}\boxtimes K_{2}\xrightarrow{\sim}\tau^{*}(K_{2}\boxtimes K_{1})

satisfy the compatibility condition of
[
Ter23U
, Defn. 6.7(2)]
, because the same holds for the corresponding natural isomorphisms on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
and on
D^{b}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
- this is just a way of rephrasing the symmetry of
\mathsf{Bti}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
. Therefore, applying
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 3.6]
, we obtain a canonical natural isomorphism between multi-exact functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2})

(43)

M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2}\xrightarrow{\sim}\tau^{*}(M_{2}\boxtimes M_{1})

compatible with the corresponding commutativity isomorphism on
\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot)
. By
2.5
, this extends to a multi-triangulated natural isomorphism between functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{2}))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2}))

(44)

M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes M_{2}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\sim}\tau^{*}(M_{2}^{\bullet}\boxtimes M_{1}^{\bullet})

again compatible with the corresponding commutativity isomorphism on
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
.

As
S_{1},S_{2}
vary in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the natural isomorphisms (
44
) satisfy condition (
c
ETS-1) of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 4.2]
: to see this it suffices to check that the natural isomorphisms (
43
) satisfy the same condition, which in turn follows from the validity of axiom (
c
ETS-1) on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
.

By
[
Ter23F
, Lemma 5.5(2)]
, in order to conclude that the natural isomorphisms (
44
) define a commutativity constraint for the
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
, it suffices to show that they define commutativity constraints for the underlying
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
-fibered and
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl,op}
-fibered categories. By construction, the latter task reduces to checking that the natural isomorphisms (
43
) define commutativity constraints for the abelian
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
-fibered and
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl,op}
-fibered category
\mathscr{M}(\cdot)
, which is indeed the case by
[
Ter23U
, Lemma 6.8(2)]
.

The symmetry of the morphism
\iota
amounts to the compatibility between the natural isomorphisms (
44
) and the corresponding natural isomorphisms on
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
that we have already mentioned above.
∎

Lemma 2.13
.

The external associativity constraint of
2.11
and the external commutativity constraint of
2.12
are compatible in the sense of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 6.3]
.

Proof.

By construction, it suffices to show that the associativity and commutativity constraints on the abelian
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{sm}
and
\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{cl,op}
-fibered category
\mathscr{M}(\cdot)
defined by the natural isomorphisms (
41
) and (
43
) are compatible. By
[
Ter23U
, Lemma 6.10]
, this reduces to checking that the external associativity and commutativity constraints on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
