are compatible, which is indeed the case.
∎

3.
Tensoring with distinguished motivic local systems

Before completing the construction of the tensor structure on perverse motives, we need to analyze the tensor product functors defined in the previous section more closely with the aim of deducing more information about the motivic internal tensor product.

Recall that, using the canonical dictionary of
[
Ter23T
]
, the symmetric associative external tensor structure on
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
obtained in
Section

2
can be translated canonically into an internal tensor structure: explicitly, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
the internal tensor product on
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
is defined via the classical formula

(45)

M_{1}^{\bullet}\otimes M_{2}^{\bullet}:=\Delta_{S}^{*}(M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes M_{2}^{\bullet}),

where
\Delta_{S}:S\hookrightarrow S\times S
denotes the diagonal embedding.
Hence we can canonically view
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
as a (non-unitary, for the moment) symmetric monoidal
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category. The two parts of the promised monoidal structure that it remains to obtain are the motivic unit constraint and the motivic internal homomorphisms. In both cases, trying to work with the external tensor product does not seem to lead one very far: we definitely need to describe our next constructions in terms of the internal tensor product. The problem is that, at this stage, the internal tensor product functors on perverse motives remains quite obscure, since these are deduced from the external tensor product functors rather than obtained directly from the lifting principles of universal abelian factorizations.

The main goal of this section is to provide a more direct description of the internal tensor product functor (
45
) in the case where the base
S
is smooth and one of the two complexes involved has a certain distinguished form: this is
3.6
, the technical proof of which occupies most part of the present section. Roughly speaking, we will show that, over any
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, a suitable restriction of the internal tensor product functor on
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
can also be obtained directly from the lifting principles of universal abelian factorizations. This result will play a crucial role both in
Section

4
, where we construct the motivic unit constraint, and in
Section

5
, where we construct internal homomorphisms of perverse motives.

3.1.
Distinguished motivic local systems

In order to formalize our idea, we need to introduce another abelian subcategory
\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)
of
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
obtained as the universal abelian factorization of a further additive subquiver
\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)
of
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)
. Recall that we let
\mathsf{Loc}(S)
denote the usual
\mathbb{Q}
-linear abelian category of local systems over
S
.

Notation 3.1
.

For every
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, we use the following notation:

•

We define an abelian subcategory
\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
of
\mathsf{Perv}(S)
by setting

\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)=\left\{K\in\mathsf{Perv}(S)\;|\;K[-\dim(S)]\in\mathsf{Loc}(S)\right\}

if
S
is connected, and extending the definition in the obvious way for general smooth
S
.

•

We introduce the full additive subcategory

\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S):=\left\{A\in\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\;|\;\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(A)\in\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\right\}\subset\mathscr{D}^{0}(S),

and we let

\beta_{S}^{0,loc}:\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)\rightarrow\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)

denote the restriction of
\beta_{S}^{0}
to
\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)
.

•

We let
\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)
denote the universal abelian factorization of the additive functor

\beta_{S}^{0,loc}:=\beta_{S}|_{\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)}:\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S).

We implicitly regard it as a (not necessarily full) abelian subcategory of
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
via the canonical faithful exact functor

\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)

obtained by applying
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 2.5]
to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S}^{0,loc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S}^{0}}

•

For simplicity, we still write

–

\pi_{S}
for the quotient functor
\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S))\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)
, and also for the composite functor
\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)\rightarrow\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S))\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)
,

–

\iota_{S}
for the induced faithful exact functor
\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\subset\mathsf{Perv}(S)
.

This convention will not create any ambiguity.

Remark 3.2
.

By construction, for every smooth (equidimensional) morphism
p:P\rightarrow S
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, the functor
p^{\dagger}:\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{D}^{0}(P)
introduced in
2.10
sends
\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)
to
\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(P)
, and therefore the corresponding exact functor
p^{\dagger}:\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(P)
sends
\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)
to
\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(P)
.
For every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, we can restrict the picture to the cofiltered category
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
introduced before
1.9
: as
U
varies in
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
, the categories
\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(U)
canonically assemble into an abelian
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
-fibered subcategory
\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(\cdot)
of
\mathscr{M}^{0}(\cdot)
.

In view of the applications to
Section

5
, it is important to note the following fact:

Lemma 3.3
.

For every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
-fibered category
\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}
is weakly cofinal in
\mathscr{M}^{0}
in the sense of
1.8
.

Proof.

By
[
Ter23U
, Cor. 5.15]
, it suffices to show that the
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
-fibered subcategory
\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}
of
\mathscr{D}^{0}
is weakly cofinal. Since
\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}
is a full
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
-fibered subcategory of
\mathscr{D}^{0}
, it suffices to check the condition on objects. To this end, using the definition of
\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}
, it suffices to show that the inclusion of
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
-fibered categories
\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(\cdot)\subset\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot)
is weakly cofinal, which is obvious.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

The existence of a perverse motivic
t
-structure on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})
would imply the fullness of the inclusion
\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)\subset\mathscr{M}(S)
. Unfortunately, we have no idea how to show this unconditionally; in any case,
3.3
suffices for all our applications here.

We now describe the auxiliary construction on which the main results of the present section are based:

Construction 3.5
.

Fix
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
. We define a shifted tensor product functor

(46)

-\otimes^{\dagger}-:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\times\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})

by the formula

A_{1}\otimes^{\dagger}A_{2}:=(A_{1}\otimes A_{2})[-\dim(S)]

in the case where
S
is connected, and extending it in the obvious way for general
S
.
Similarly, we define a shifted tensor product functor

(47)

-\otimes^{\dagger}-:D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))\times D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))

by the formula

K^{\bullet}_{1}\otimes^{\dagger}K^{\bullet}_{2}:=(K^{\bullet}_{1}\otimes K^{\bullet}_{2})[-\dim(S)]

in the case where
S
is connected, and extending it in the obvious way for general
S
. Note that the functor (
47
) sends
\mathsf{Perv}(S)\times\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
to
\mathsf{Perv}(S)
, and therefore the functor (
46
) sends
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)
to
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)
. Applying
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 4.3]
to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S)\times\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes^{\dagger}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S}^{0}\times\beta_{S}^{0,loc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes^{\dagger}-}

we obtain a bi-exact functor

(48)

-\otimes^{\dagger}-:\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)

together with a canonical natural isomorphism of functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S)

\tilde{\rho}^{\dagger}=\tilde{\rho}^{\dagger}_{S}:\iota_{S}(M)\otimes^{\dagger}\iota_{S}(L)\xrightarrow{\sim}\iota_{S}(M\otimes^{\dagger}L).

It is a priori unclear whether the functor (
48
) is related to (the restriction of) the internal tensor product of perverse motives defined via the formula (
45
). It is a pleasant surprise that the two functors are indeed related in the way one would expect:

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
be connected of dimension
d
. Then there exists a canonical isomorphism of functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)

(49)

M\otimes^{\dagger}L\xrightarrow{\sim}(M\otimes L)[-d]

rendering the diagram of functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S)

(50)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(M\otimes^{\dagger}L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}((M\otimes L)[-d])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(M\otimes L)[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(M)\otimes^{\dagger}\iota_{S}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{S}(M)\otimes\iota_{S}(L))[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}}

commutative.

Most of the rest of the section is devoted to the technical proof of this result. Before discussing the details, let us briefly explain what is the general strategy behind the proof and why implementing it correctly turns out to be quite subtle.
Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S\times S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S)\times\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S\times S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S}^{0}\times\beta_{S}^{0,loc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S\times S}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d]}

where both squares are commutative up to canonical natural isomorphism. By construction, the vertical and horizontal composites recover (the restrictions of) the shifted tensor product functors (
46
) and (
47
), respectively; in particular they take values in
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)
and
\mathsf{Perv}(S)
, respectively.
If the functor

(51)

\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d]:D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S\times S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))

was
t
-exact for the obvious
t
-structures, then the corresponding functor
\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d]:\mathscr{D}(S\times S)\rightarrow\mathscr{D}(S)
would send
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S\times S)
to
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)
. Hence one could refine the previous diagram to a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S\times S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S)\times\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S\times S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S}^{0}\times\beta_{S}^{0,loc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S\times S}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{0}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d]}

and one could try to deduce that the functor (
48
) is naturally isomorphic to the composite

\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)\xrightarrow{-\boxtimes-}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S\times S)\xrightarrow{\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d]}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)

by using the general functoriality of universal abelian factorizations from
[
Ter23U
]
. If this was possible, then the conclusion of
3.6
would follow trivially from the fact that lifting exact functors to universal abelian factorizations is compatible with composition.

Needless to say, the functor (
51
) is not
t
-exact, hence the argument sketched here simply does not work. On the other hand, it is well-known that the composite functor

\Delta_{S,*}\circ\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d]:D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S\times S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S\times S))

can be modeled as an exact functor
C^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S\times S))\rightarrow C^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S\times S))
by means of a suitable Čech complex construction. For instance, in the simple case where
S
is the affine line
\mathbf{A}^{1}_{k}
, one can use the total complex associated to the exact functor
C^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\mathbf{A}^{2}_{k}))\rightarrow C^{[0,1]}(C^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\mathbf{A}^{2}_{k})))

(52)

K^{\bullet}\rightsquigarrow[j_{!}j^{*}K^{\bullet}\rightarrow K^{\bullet}],

where
j
denotes the affine open immersion of the complement of the diagonal in
\mathbf{A}^{2}_{k}
. The key advantage is that the complex in (
52
) lifts canonically to the level of perverse motives.
In general, the model for
\Delta_{S,*}\circ\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d]
depends on the choice of a finite affine covering of the open complement of
\Delta_{S}(S)
in
S\times S
, but the derived functor of the latter is essentially independent of such a choice. In fact, this idea is exploited in the proof of
[
IM19
, Prop. 4.2]
to construct the inverse image functor
\Delta_{S}^{*}
- or, more generally, inverse image functors along closed immersions - in the setting of perverse motives. We are going to prove
3.6
by a similar method.

3.2.
Some Čech complex computations

For the reader’s convenience, we start by recalling the Čech complex construction just mentioned in the setting of perverse sheaves and perverse motives.

Construction 3.7
.

Let
s:S\hookrightarrow Y
be a closed immersion in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, and let
u:U\hookrightarrow Y
denote the complementary open immersion.
Suppose that we are given a finite affine open covering
\mathscr{U}=\left\{U_{i}\subset U\right\}_{i=1}^{d}
of
U
. For every subset
I\subset\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
, set

U_{I}:=\bigcap_{i\in I}U_{i}

and let
j_{I}:U_{I}\hookrightarrow Y
denote the corresponding open immersion (with the agreement that
U_{\emptyset}=Y
and
j_{\emptyset}=\textup{id}_{Y}
). Note that each
j_{I}
is affine, because
Y
is separated over
k
by definition. For every choice of subsets
I,I^{\prime}\subset\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
satisfying
I^{\prime}\subset I
, there exists a canonical natural transformation of functors
\mathsf{Perv}(Y)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(Y)

\alpha^{\mathscr{U}}_{I,I^{\prime}}:j_{I,!}j_{I}^{*}K\rightarrow j_{I^{\prime},!}j_{I^{\prime}}^{*}K

induced by the co-unit along the inclusion of
U_{I}
into
U_{I^{\prime}}
. For each
r=0,\dots,d
, set

U^{(r)}_{\mathscr{U}}:=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}\\
\#I=r\end{subarray}}U_{I}

and let
J_{r}:U^{(r)}\rightarrow Y
denote the canonical morphism. We introduce the Čech complex functor

\check{C}^{\bullet}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:\mathsf{Perv}(Y)\rightarrow C^{[-d,0]}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y)),\quad K\rightsquigarrow[J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}K\to\dots\to J_{1,!}J_{1}^{*}K\to K],

where, for each
r=1,\dots,d
, the arrow

J_{r,!}J_{r}^{*}K=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}\\
\#I=r\end{subarray}}j_{I,!}j_{I}^{*}K\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}I^{\prime}\subset\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}\\
\#I^{\prime}=r-1\end{subarray}}j_{I^{\prime},!}j_{I^{\prime}}^{*}K=J_{r-1,!}J_{r-1}^{*}K

is induced by the usual alternating sum of the arrows
\alpha^{\mathscr{U}}_{I,I^{\prime}}
. We extend the Čech complex functor to an exact functor

\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:C^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))\xrightarrow{\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}^{\bullet}}C^{[-d,0]}(C^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y)))\xrightarrow{\textup{Tot}^{\bullet}}C^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))

and we consider its trivial derived functor

(53)

\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y)).

The argument used in the proof of
[
IM19
, Prop. 4.2]
implies that the functor (
53
) is canonically left adjoint to the inclusion
D^{b}_{S}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))\hookrightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))
, hence canonically isomorphic to (the co-restriction of) the functor
s_{*}s^{*}:D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))
.

The construction carries over to the setting of perverse motives: one can define a Čech complex functor

\check{C}^{\bullet}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y)\rightarrow C^{[-d,0]}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y)),\quad M\rightsquigarrow[J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}M\to\dots\to J_{1,!}J_{1}^{*}M\to M]

and use it to obtain a triangulated functor

\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))

which is canonically left adjoint to the inclusion
D^{b}_{S}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))\hookrightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))
, hence canonically isomorphic to (the co-restriction of) the functor
s_{*}s^{*}:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))
.
Note that we have a canonical natural isomorphism between exact functors
C^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))\rightarrow C^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))

\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(\iota_{Y}(M^{\bullet}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\iota_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(M^{\bullet}))

defined as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(\iota_{Y}(M^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(M^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Tot}^{\bullet}\check{C}^{\bullet}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(\iota_{Y}(M^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Tot}^{\bullet}\iota_{Y}(\check{C}^{\bullet}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(M^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}(\textup{Tot}^{\bullet}\check{C}^{\bullet}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(M^{\bullet}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the left-most horizontal arrow is induced by the family of natural isomorphisms of functors
C^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))\rightarrow C^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))

\check{C}^{r}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(\iota_{Y}(M^{\bullet})):=J_{r,!}J_{r}^{*}\iota_{Y}(M^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\sim}J_{r,!}\iota_{U_{\mathscr{U}}^{(r)}}(J_{r}^{*}M^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\sim}\iota_{Y}(J_{r,!}J_{r}^{*}M^{\bullet})=:\iota_{Y}(\check{C}^{r}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(M^{\bullet}))

as
r=0,\dots,d
varies. In the following, we consider the induced natural isomorphism of triangulated functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))

\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(\iota_{Y}(M^{\bullet}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\iota_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(M^{\bullet})).

We need to provide an analogous Čech complex construction on the level of triangulated étale motives. This is possible since the stable homotopy
2
-functor
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
canonically underlies a stable homotopy algebraic derivator in the sense of
[
Ayo07a
, § 2.4.2]
.

Construction 3.8
.

Keep the notation and assumptions of
3.7
.
For every choice of subsets
I,I^{\prime}\subset\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
satisfying
I^{\prime}\subset I
, consider the canonical natural transformation of functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})

\alpha^{\mathscr{U}}_{I,I^{\prime}}:j_{I,!}j_{I}^{*}A\rightarrow j_{I^{\prime},!}j_{I^{\prime}}^{*}A

induced by the co-unit along the inclusion of
U_{I}
into
U_{I^{\prime}}
. By construction, the diagram of functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{I,!}j_{I}^{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{I^{\prime},!}j_{I^{\prime}}^{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{I,!}\mathsf{Bti}_{U_{I}}^{*}(j_{I}^{*}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{I^{\prime},!}\mathsf{Bti}_{U_{I^{\prime}}}^{*}(j_{I^{\prime}}^{*}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(j_{I,!}j_{I}^{*}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(j_{I^{\prime},!}j_{I^{\prime}}^{*}A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\mathscr{U}}_{I,I^{\prime}}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\mathscr{U}}_{I,I^{\prime}}}

is commutative under Beilinson’s equivalence
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))\xrightarrow{\sim}D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})
.
Let
[-1,0]
denote the partially ordered set
\left\{-1<0\right\}
; for every integer
n\geq 0
we regard the product category
[-1,0]^{n}
as a small diagram (with the convention that
[-1,0]^{0}
is just the singleton category). We are particularly interested in the diagram
[-1,0]^{d}
: in this case, for every subset
I\subset\left\{0,\dots,d\right\}
, we let
\underline{I}
denote the object
(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{d})\in[-1,0]^{d}
whose
i
-th coordinate
\epsilon_{i}
equals
-1
if
i\in I
and
0
otherwise. Note that the assignment
I\rightsquigarrow\underline{I}
defines an anti-isomorphism between the powerset of
\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
(regarded as a poset with the usual inclusion ordering) and the category
[-1,0]^{d}
.
Given two subsets
I,I^{\prime}\subset\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
satisfying
I^{\prime}\subset I
, we let
e_{I,I^{\prime}}:\underline{I}\rightarrow\underline{I}^{\prime}
denote the corresponding morphism in
[-1,0]^{d}
.
Following the idea of
[
IM19
, Lemma 3.4, Lemma 3.6]
, we can construct a triangulated functor

\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}((Y,[-1,0]^{d}),\mathbb{Q})

providing a coherent lifting of the
[-1,0]^{d}
-indexed diagram of functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})

\underline{I}(A):=j_{I,!}j_{I}^{*}A,\qquad e_{I,I^{\prime}}(A):=\alpha_{I,I^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{U}}(A):j_{I,!}j_{I}^{*}A\rightarrow j_{I^{\prime},!}j_{I^{\prime}}^{*}A.

Concretely, one can consider the two obvious morphisms of diagram of
k
-varieties

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underline{I}\mapsto U_{I})_{\underline{I}\in[-1,0]^{d}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underline{I}\mapsto Y)_{\underline{I}\in[-1,0]^{d}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}

and define the functor
\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}
by the formula

\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:=\xi_{!}\circ\nu^{*}.

Now, for every integer
n\geq 1
, let

\textup{Cof}_{n}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}((Y,[-1,0]^{n}),\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}((Y,[-1,0]^{n-1}),\mathbb{Q})

denote the co-fiber with respect to the last coordinate of
[-1,0]^{n}=[-1,0]^{n-1}\times[-1,0]
. We let

\textup{Tot}^{\square}:=\textup{Cof}_{1}\circ\dots\circ\textup{Cof}_{d}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}((Y,[-1,0]^{d}),\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})

denote the associated derived total complex functor, and we set

\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:=\textup{Tot}^{\square}\circ\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q}).

Similarly, we can define a functor

\sigma_{\leq(-1,\dots,-1)}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}((Y,[-1,0]^{d}),\mathbb{Q})

providing a coherent lifting of the
[-1,0]^{d}
-indexed diagram of functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})

\underline{I}(A):=\begin{cases}J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}A,&I=\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}\\
0,&I\neq\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}.\end{cases}

Note that there is an obvious natural transformation of functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}((Y,[-1,0]^{d}),\mathbb{Q})

(54)

\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(A)\rightarrow\sigma_{\leq(-1,\dots,-1)}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(A).

One can repeat the entire construction replacing
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
with any stable homotopy algebraic derivator in the sense of
[
Ayo07a
, § 2.4.2]
. As a consequence of
[
Ayo10
, Thm. 1.8, Lemme 1.10]
, the stable homotopy
2
-functor
D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
canonically underlies a stable homotopy algebraic derivator, and the Betti realization

\mathsf{Bti}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})

can be canonically extended over diagrams of
k
-varieties. Thus we can define a functor

\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}((Y,[-1,0]^{d}),\mathbb{Q})

providing a coherent lifting of the
[-1,0]^{d}
-indexed diagram of functors
D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})

\underline{I}(K^{\bullet}):=j_{I,!}j_{I}^{*}K^{\bullet},\qquad e_{I,I^{\prime}}(A):=\alpha_{I,I^{\prime}}^{\mathscr{U}}(K^{\bullet}):j_{I,!}j_{I}^{*}K^{\bullet}\rightarrow j_{I^{\prime},!}j_{I^{\prime}}^{*}K^{\bullet},

as well as a functor

\sigma_{\leq(-1,\dots,-1)}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}((Y,[-1,0]^{d}),\mathbb{Q})

providing a coherent lifting of the
[-1,0]^{d}
-indexed diagram of functors
D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})

\underline{I}(K^{\bullet}):=\begin{cases}J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}K^{\bullet},&I=\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}\\
0,&I\neq\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}.\end{cases}

As in the previous case, they are related by a canonical natural transformation of functors
D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}((Y,[-1,0]^{d}),\mathbb{Q})

\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(K^{\bullet})\rightarrow\sigma_{\leq(-1,\dots,-1)}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(K^{\bullet}).

Note that, moreover, there exist canonical natural isomorphisms of functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}((Y,[-1,0]^{d}),\mathbb{Q})

\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(A)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathsf{Bti}_{(Y,[-1,0]^{d})}^{*}(\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(A))

and

\sigma_{\leq(-1,\dots,-1)}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(A)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathsf{Bti}_{(Y,[-1,0]^{d})}^{*}(\sigma_{\leq(-1,\dots,-1)}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(A))

rendering the diagram of functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}((Y,[-1,0]^{d}),\mathbb{Q})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{(Y,[-1,0]^{d})}^{*}(\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\leq(-1,\dots,-1)}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{(Y,[-1,0]^{d})}^{*}(\sigma_{\leq(-1,\dots,-1)}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(A))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commutative. We can also define a total complex functor

\textup{Tot}^{\square}:=\textup{Cof}_{1}\circ\dots\circ\textup{Cof}_{d}:D^{b}_{c}((Y,[-1,0]^{d}),\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q}).

By construction, the composite

\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:=\textup{Tot}^{\square}\circ\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}:D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})

recovers the functor (
53
) under Beilinson’s equivalence
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))\xrightarrow{\sim}D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})
. Therefore we obtain a canonical natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})

\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}(A)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(A))

by taking the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(A)).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Tot}^{\square}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Tot}^{\square}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y,[-1,0]^{d}}^{*}(\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(\textup{Tot}^{\square}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(A))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

By construction, the diagram of functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})

(55)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}s^{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(s^{*}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(s_{*}s^{*}A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}}

is commutative.

We need to compare the total Čech complex functor on the level of perverse motives with the analogous functor on triangulated motives. This turns out to be quite subtle, since the two functors are constructed in different ways. In order to proceed, it is convenient to introduce yet a further subdiagram of
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)
:

Notation 3.9
.

Given a closed immersion
s:S\hookrightarrow Y
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, we introduce the full additive subcategory

\mathscr{D}^{0,s}(Y):=\left\{B\in\mathscr{D}^{0}(Y)\;|\;s^{\dagger}B\in\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\right\}\subset\mathscr{D}^{0}(Y).

We can state the promised result about Čech complex functors as follows:

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
s:S\hookrightarrow Y
be a closed immersion of constant codimension
d
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
. Suppose that the open complement
U
of
S
in
Y
admits a finite affine open covering
\mathscr{U}
with exactly
d
elements. Then there exist a canonical natural isomorphism of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0,s}(Y)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y)

(56)

\pi_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](B))\xrightarrow{\sim}\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](\pi_{Y}(B))

such that the diagram of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0,s}(Y)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(Y)

(57)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]\pi_{Y}(B))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

is commutative.

Proof.

First of all, let us check that the composite functor

\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]\circ\pi_{Y}:\mathscr{D}^{0,s}(Y)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y)\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))

really takes values in
\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y)
- or, equivalently, that its composition with the conservative
t
-exact functor
\iota_{Y}:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))
takes values in
\mathsf{Perv}(Y)
. To this end, for every object
B\in\mathscr{D}^{0,s}(Y)
we compute

\displaystyle\iota_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](\pi_{Y}(B)))=

\displaystyle\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(B))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](\beta_{Y}(B))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{*}s^{*}[-d]\beta_{Y}(B)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{*}s^{*}[-d]\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B)

as
B\in\mathscr{D}^{0}(Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(s^{*}[-d]B)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{*}\beta_{S}(s^{*}[-d]B)

\displaystyle\textup{as $B\in\mathscr{D}^{0,s}(Y)$}.

The latter object belongs indeed to
\mathsf{Perv}(Y)
in view of the definition of
\beta_{S}
and by the
t
-exactness of
s_{*}:D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})
.

In order to define the sought-after natural isomorphism (
56
), we start by constructing a natural transformation between exact functors
\mathscr{D}^{0}(Y)\rightarrow C^{[-d,0]}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))

\pi_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](B))[d]\rightarrow\check{C}^{\bullet}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(\pi_{Y}(B)).

With the notation of
3.7
and
3.8
, this amounts to giving a natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0}(Y)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y)

(58)

\pi_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](B))\rightarrow J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}\pi_{Y}(B)

such that the composite

(59)

\pi_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](B))\rightarrow J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}\pi_{Y}(B)\rightarrow J_{d-1,!}J_{d-1}^{*}\pi_{Y}(B)=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}\\
\#I=d-1\end{subarray}}j_{I,!}j_{I}^{*}\pi_{Y}(B)

vanishes. We define (
58
) by taking the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}\pi_{Y}(B),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}(\textup{Tot}^{\square}[-d]\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}(\textup{Tot}^{\square}[-d]\sigma_{\leq(-1,\dots,-1)}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}(J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}B)}

where the first horizontal arrow is induced by (
54
).
The vanishing of (
59
) follows from the existence of a commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}(J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}\\
\#I=d-1\end{subarray}}\pi_{Y}(j_{I,!}j_{I}^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}\pi_{Y}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}\\
\#I=d-1\end{subarray}}j_{I,!}j_{I}^{*}\pi_{Y}(B)}

where the upper horizontal composite vanishes by construction.
We deduce a natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0}(Y)\rightarrow D^{[0,d]}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))

(60)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](\pi_{Y}(B)).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Tot}^{\bullet}\pi_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Tot}^{\bullet}\check{C}^{\bullet}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](\pi_{Y}(B))}

In an analogous way, we obtain a natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0}(Y)\rightarrow D^{[-d,0]}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))

(61)

\beta_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](B))\rightarrow\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](\beta_{Y}(B))

making the diagram of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0}(Y)\rightarrow D^{[0,d]}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))

(62)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](\beta_{Y}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](\pi_{Y}(B)))}

\scriptstyle\sim

commutative. We claim that the value of (
60
) on a given object
B\in\mathscr{D}^{0}(Y)
is an isomorphism if and only if
B\in\mathscr{D}^{0,s}(Y)
. Using the commutativity of the diagram (
62
) and the conservativity of
\iota_{Y}:D^{[0,d]}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y))\rightarrow D^{[0,d]}(\mathsf{Perv}(Y))
, we see that this is equivalent to showing that the value of (
61
) on a given object
B\in\mathscr{D}^{0}(Y)
is an isomorphism if and only if
B\in\mathscr{D}^{0,s}(Y)
. To this end, taking the image under
{{}^{p}H^{0}}
of the commutative diagram (
55
), the arrow (
61
) can be identified with the canonical arrow in
\mathsf{Perv}(Y)

(63)

{{}^{p}H^{0}}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(s_{*}s^{\dagger}B)={{}^{p}H^{0}}s_{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(s^{\dagger}B)=s_{*}{{}^{p}H^{0}}\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(s^{\dagger}B)\rightarrow s_{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(s^{\dagger}B).

Here, the latter arrow in the composite can be also written as

s_{*}{{}^{p}H^{0}}s^{\dagger}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B)\rightarrow s_{*}s^{\dagger}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B)

and is induced by the left-
t
-exactness of the functor
s^{\dagger}=s^{*}[-d]:D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
(which follows precisely from the fact that
U
admits an affine open covering with
d
elements). Using the conservativity of
s_{*}:D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})
, we see that the arrow (
63
) is invertible if and only if
\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(s^{\dagger}B)\in\mathsf{Perv}(S)
.

We define the natural isomorphism (
56
) by taking the restriction of (
60
) to
\mathscr{D}^{0,s}(Y)
. By construction, the diagram (
57
) coincides with the outer part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]\beta_{Y}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]\pi_{Y}(B))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the two triangles are commutative by definition and the bottom piece is already known to be commutative. Hence, in order to conclude the proof it suffices to show that the top piece is commutative as well. Unwinding the various definitions, the commutativity of the upper piece follows from the commutativity of the outer part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(\textup{Tot}^{\square}[-d]\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Tot}^{\square}[-d]\mathsf{Bti}_{(Y,[-1,0]^{d})}^{*}(\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Tot}^{\square}[-d]\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(\textup{Tot}^{\square}[-d]\sigma_{\leq(-1,\dots,-1)}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Tot}^{\square}[-d]\mathsf{Bti}_{(Y,[-1,0]^{d})}^{*}(\sigma_{\leq(-1,\dots,-1)}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Tot}^{\square}[-d]\sigma_{\leq(-1,\dots,-1)}\check{C}^{\square}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}(\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{Y}(J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{d,!}J_{d}^{*}\beta_{Y}(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

which in turn follows from the commutativity of the four inner pieces, which holds by construction. This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
s:S\hookrightarrow Y
be a closed immersion of constant codimension
d
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
. Then there exists a natural isomorphism of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0,s}(Y)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)

(64)

\zeta_{s}:\pi_{S}(s^{\dagger}B)\xrightarrow{\sim}s^{\dagger}\pi_{Y}(B)

such that the diagram of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0,s}(Y)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S)

(65)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\dagger}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\dagger}\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}\pi_{S}(s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(s^{\dagger}\pi_{V}(B))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{s}}

is commutative.

Proof.

Note that, for every closed immersion
s:S\hookrightarrow Y
as in the statement, the faithfulness and conservativity of the functor
\iota_{S}:\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S)
implies that a natural transformation as in (
64
) rendering the diagram (
65
) commutative is necessarily uniquely determined and invertible, provided it exists.

We start with a preliminary observation. Let
s:S\hookrightarrow Y
be as in the statement; let
v:V\hookrightarrow Y
be an open immersion, and form the Cartesian square in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Suppose for a moment that the thesis is known to hold both for
s
and for
s_{U}
. We claim that, in this situation, the diagram of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0,s}(Y)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}\pi_{S}(s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}s^{\dagger}\pi_{Y}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{U}(u^{*}s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\dagger}v^{*}\pi_{Y}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{U}(s_{U}^{\dagger}v^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{U}^{\dagger}\pi_{V}(v^{*}B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{s_{U}}}

is commutative. In order to show this, it suffices to check the commutativity of its image under the faithful functor
\iota_{U}:\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(U)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}(u^{*}\pi_{S}(s^{\dagger}B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}(u^{*}s^{\dagger}\pi_{Y}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}\pi_{U}(u^{*}s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}(s_{U}^{\dagger}v^{*}\pi_{Y}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}\pi_{U}(s_{U}^{\dagger}v^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}(s_{U}^{\dagger}\pi_{V}(v^{*}B))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{s_{U}}}

that we can decompose as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}(u^{*}\pi_{S}(s^{\dagger}B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}(u^{*}s^{\dagger}\pi_{Y}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}\iota_{S}\pi_{S}(s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}\iota_{S}(s^{\dagger}\pi_{Y}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}s^{\dagger}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}s^{\dagger}\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}\pi_{U}(u^{*}s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{U}^{*}(u^{*}s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{U}^{\dagger}v^{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{U}^{\dagger}v^{*}\iota_{V}\pi_{V}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}(s_{U}^{\dagger}v^{*}\pi_{Y}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{U}^{*}(s_{U}^{\dagger}v^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{U}^{\dagger}\mathsf{Bti}_{V}^{*}(v^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{U}^{\dagger}\iota_{V}(v^{*}\pi_{Y}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{U}^{\dagger}\iota_{V}\pi_{V}(v^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}\pi_{U}(s_{U}^{\dagger}v^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}(s_{U}^{\dagger}\pi_{V}(v^{*}B))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{s_{U}}}

Here, the two five-term pieces involving
\zeta_{s}
and
\zeta_{s_{U}}
are commutative by assumption, the six-term piece in the upper-right part as well as the two five-term pieces in the upper-left part and in the central part are commutative by
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 2.5(2)]
, the central six-term piece is commutative by construction, and the remaining three pieces are commutative by naturality.

As a consequence of this observation we deduce that, in order to show the existence of the natural isomorphism
\zeta_{s}
along a given closed immersion
s:S\hookrightarrow Y
, it suffices to show that, for some finite open covering
\left\{v_{i}:V_{i}\hookrightarrow Y\right\}_{i\in I}
, the isomorphisms
\zeta_{s_{i}}
along all restrictions
s_{i}:s^{-1}(V_{i})\hookrightarrow V_{i}
exist: this follows from the fact that both
\mathscr{M}^{0}(\cdot)
and
\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot)
are stacks for the Zariski topology. Therefore, since both
Y
and
S
are smooth by assumption (hence, in particular, the closed immersion
s
is regular of codimension
d
), we may reduce to the case where
Y
is affine and connected and the open complement
U
of
S
in
Y
admits a finite affine open covering
\mathscr{U}
with exactly
d
elements: for example, for each point of
S
one can take the distinguished open subsets associated to the elements of some regular sequence of length
d
defining
S
inside
Y
locally around the chosen point.

In this situation, fix one such open covering
\mathscr{U}
; we define the sought-after natural isomorphism
\zeta_{s}
by declaring that its image under the fully faithful functor
s_{*}:\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y)
is the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}\pi_{S}(s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}s^{\dagger}\pi_{Y}(B),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}(s_{*}s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d](\pi_{Y}(B))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the bottom-left horizontal arrow is given by
3.10
. In order to check the commutativity of the diagram (
65
), it suffices to check the commutativity of its image under the fully faithful functor
s_{*}:\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(Y)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}s^{\dagger}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}s^{\dagger}\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}\iota_{S}\pi_{S}(s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}\iota_{S}(s^{\dagger}\pi_{Y}(B)),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{s}}

which in turn is equivalent to showing the commutativity of the outer rectangle of the composite diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}s^{\dagger}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}s^{\dagger}\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}\iota_{S}\pi_{S}(s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}\iota_{S}(s^{\dagger}\pi_{Y}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}(s_{*}\pi_{S}(s^{\dagger}B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}(s_{*}s^{\dagger}\pi_{Y}(B)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\tilde{\theta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\tilde{\theta}}}

Expanding the definition of the bottom horizontal arrow in the latter, we obtain the outer part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}s^{\dagger}\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}\iota_{S}\pi_{S}(s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}s^{\dagger}\iota_{Y}(\pi_{Y}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}(s_{*}\pi_{S}(s^{\dagger}B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(s_{*}s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{*}\iota_{S}(s^{\dagger}\pi_{Y}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(s_{*}s^{\dagger}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Y}^{*}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}(s_{*}s^{\dagger}\pi_{Y}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}\pi_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Y}(\check{C}_{Y,\mathscr{U}}[-d]\pi_{Y}(B))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\tilde{\theta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\tilde{\theta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Here, the top-left piece is commutative by
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 2.5(2)]
, the top-central piece and the bottom-right piece are commutative by construction, the bottom-central piece is commutative by
3.10
, while the other two pieces are commutative by naturality.
∎

3.3.
Proof of the main result and applications

We can finally compare the shifted tensor product of
3.5
with the one obtained from the external tensor product, as announced at the beginning of this section:

Proof of
3.6
.

We want to apply the abstract criterion of
[
Ter23U
, Cor. 4.5]
. To this end, we have to exhibit the existence of a natural isomorphism of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)

(66)

\chi_{S}:(\pi_{S}(A)\otimes\pi_{S}(L))[-d]\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_{S}((A\otimes L)[-d])

rendering the diagram of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S)

(67)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{S}\pi_{S}(A)\otimes\iota_{S}\pi_{S}(L))[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(A)\otimes\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(L))[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(A\otimes L)[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}((A\otimes L)[-d])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(\pi_{S}(A)\otimes\pi_{S}(L))[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}\pi_{S}((A\otimes L)[-d])}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{S}}

commutative. We define the natural isomorphism (
66
) as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{S}(A)\otimes\pi_{S}(L))[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi((A\otimes L)[-d]),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S}^{\dagger}(\pi_{S}(A)\boxtimes\pi_{S}(L))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S}^{\dagger}\pi_{S\times S}(A\boxtimes L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{S}(\Delta_{S}^{\dagger}(A\boxtimes L))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{\Delta_{S}}}

where the isomorphism

(68)

\zeta_{\Delta_{S}}:\pi_{S}(\Delta_{S}^{\dagger}(A\boxtimes L))\rightarrow\Delta_{S}^{\dagger}\pi_{S\times S}(A\boxtimes L)

is provided by specializing the natural transformation (
64
) to the object
A\boxtimes L
. The fact that (
68
) is actually invertible follows from
3.11
, since the object
A\boxtimes L
belongs to
\mathscr{D}^{0,\Delta_{S}}(S\times S)
by construction: this is just a rephrasing of the discussion in
3.5
.
In order to check that the diagram (
67
) is commutative, it suffices to express all the arrows in terms of the external tensor product: in this way, we obtain the outer part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d](\iota_{S}\pi_{S}(A)\boxtimes\iota_{S}\pi_{S}(L))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d](\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(A)\boxtimes\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(L))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d]\mathsf{Bti}_{S\times S}^{*}(A\boxtimes L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d]\iota_{S\times S}(\pi_{S}(A)\boxtimes\pi_{S}(L))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d]\iota_{S\times S}\pi_{S\times S}(A\boxtimes L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(\Delta_{S}^{*}(A\boxtimes L)[-d])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(\Delta_{S}^{*}(\pi_{S}(A)\boxtimes\pi_{S}(L)))[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(\Delta_{S}^{*}\pi_{S\times S}(A\boxtimes L))[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d](A\boxtimes L))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d](\pi_{S}(A)\boxtimes\pi_{S}(L)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d]\pi_{S\times S}(A\boxtimes L))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}\pi_{S}(\Delta_{S}^{*}[-d](A\boxtimes L))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{\Delta_{S}}}

where the right-most piece is commutative by
3.11
, the top piece is commutative by
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 4.3(2)]
, while the other pieces are commutative by naturality. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

Of course, the result of
3.6
admits a symmetric version where the roles of the two variables are exchanged: there exists a canonical isomorphism of functors
\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)

L\otimes^{\dagger}M\xrightarrow{\sim}(L\otimes M)[-d]

such that the diagram of functors
\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(L\otimes^{\dagger}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}((L\otimes M)[-d])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(L\otimes M)[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(L)\otimes^{\dagger}\iota_{S}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{S}(L)\otimes\iota_{S}(M))[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\rho}^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

is commutative.

Recall that, for both stable homotopy
2
-functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
and
D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
are endowed with Verdier duality functors defined, over any given
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, by the formula

\mathbb{D}_{S}(A):=\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(A,a_{S}^{!}\mathbf{1}_{\textup{Spec}(k)}),

where
a_{S}:S\rightarrow\textup{Spec}(k)
denotes the structural projection. Since, as
S
varies in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the Betti realization functors
\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}(S,\mathbb{Q})
commute with the six operations on the two sides, they also commute with Verdier duality in a canonical way. The fact that
\mathbb{D}_{S}
defines a duality means that, in both cases, the canonical arrow

A\rightarrow\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathbb{D}_{S}(A))

is invertible. Using this, one can deduce a canonical identification

(69)

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(A,B)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{D}_{S}(A\otimes\mathbb{D}_{S}(B))

in both cases; again, these are compatible with one another under the Betti realization.
Since, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the equivalence

\mathbb{D}_{S}:D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{\sim}D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})^{op}

is
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structures, it restricts to an equivalence

\mathbb{D}_{S}:\mathsf{Perv}(S)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathsf{Perv}(S)^{op}

and so, by
[
IM19
, § 2.8]
, it canonically lifts to an equivalence

\mathbb{D}_{S}:\mathscr{M}(S)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathscr{M}(S)^{op}

that one might still call Verdier duality. Note that these functors are obtained for free without even the need to know that internal homomorphisms of perverse motives actually exist! In fact, one might try to use the above formula (
69
) in the setting of perverse motives as a way of defining internal homomorphisms there. In this way one would obtain, over every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, a bi-triangulated functor

(70)

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(-,-):D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))^{op}\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))

endowed with a canonical natural isomorphism of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))^{op}\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))

\iota_{S}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M_{1}^{\bullet},M_{2}^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\iota_{S}(M_{1}^{\bullet}),\iota_{S}(M_{2}^{\bullet})).

However, with this approach it would remain unclear whether the functor (
70
) defines a right adjoint to the internal tensor product as expected: the issue is that, for a general object
M^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
, none of the two functors

-\otimes M^{\bullet},\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M^{\bullet},-):D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))

is exact - and, even worse, none of them is obtained directly from the lifting principles of universal abelian factorizations. However, the result of
3.6
puts us in the desired situation at least for some choices of
M^{\bullet}
. This is what we want to prove now.

In order to state the final result of this section, recall that, for any additive category
\mathscr{D}
, we let
\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D})
denote the category of finitely presented abelian presheaves on
\mathscr{D}
(i.e. cokernels of representable presheaves) and we let
\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D})
denote Freyd’s abelian hull of
\mathscr{D}
. By construction, every object of
\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D})
can be written as the kernel of an arrow in
\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D})
. Moreover, for every additive functor
\beta:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
into an abelian category
\mathscr{A}
, the corresponding universal abelian factorization
\mathbf{A}(\beta)
is defined as a Serre quotient of
\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D})
, and so the image of
\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D})
in
\mathbf{A}(\beta)
generates the whole of
\mathbf{A}(\beta)
under kernels.

Corollary 3.13
.

Let
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
be connected of dimension
d
, and let
\mathbb{D}_{S}
denote the motivic Verdier duality functor defined in
[
IM19
, § 2.8]
. Let
L\in\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)
, and suppose that
L
lies in the image of
\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S))
under the quotient functor
\pi_{S}:\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S))\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(S)
. Then there is a canonical adjunction

-\otimes L[-d]:\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\rightleftarrows\mathscr{M}^{0}(S):\mathbb{D}_{S}(L[-d]\otimes\mathbb{D}_{S}(-))

compatible with the corresponding adjunction on the underlying categories of perverse sheaves in the sense of
[
Ter23U
, Defn. 3.6]
.

Proof.

As a consequence of
3.6
and
3.12
, it suffices to construct, for every
L
as in the statement, an adjunction

(71)

-\otimes^{\dagger}L:\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\leftrightarrows\mathscr{M}^{0}(S):\mathbb{D}_{S}(L\otimes^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{S}(-))

compatible with the corresponding adjunction on the underlying categories of perverse sheaves in the sense of
[
Ter23U
, Defn. 3.6]
. As a consequence of the faithfulness of the functor
\iota_{S}:\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S)
, we see that such an adjunction (or, more precisely, its associated unit and co-unit transformations) is uniquely determined, provided it exists. We construct the adjoint pair in the statement in two steps of increasing generality:

(Step 1)

Suppose first that
L=\pi_{S}(A)
for some
A\in\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)
. In this case, we obtain the sought-after adjunction by applying
[
Ter23U
, Cor. 3.9]
to the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes^{\dagger}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{0}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{0}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes^{\dagger}\beta_{S}^{0}(A)}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{0}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes^{\dagger}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{0}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{0}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{0}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S}^{0}(A)\otimes^{\dagger}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}_{S}}

Note that, as a consequence of
[
Ter23U
, Rem. 3.5(2)]
, the natural bijections

\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M\otimes^{\dagger}\pi_{S}(A),N)=\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M,\mathbb{D}_{S}(\pi_{S}(A)\otimes^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{S}(N)))

are functorial with respect to
A\in\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)
.

(Step 2)

Let
L\in\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S))
. Choose a presentation of the form
L=\textup{coker}\left\{\pi_{S}(A)\rightarrow\pi_{S}(B)\right\}
for some morphism
A\rightarrow B
in
\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(S)
. Then we obtain a natural bijection

\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M\otimes^{\dagger}L,N)=\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M,\mathbb{D}_{S}(L\otimes^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{S}(N)))

by combining the two chains of bijections

\displaystyle\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M\otimes^{\dagger}L,N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M\otimes^{\dagger}\textup{coker}\left\{\pi_{S}(A)\rightarrow\pi_{S}(B)\right\},N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(\textup{coker}\left\{M\otimes^{\dagger}\pi_{S}(A)\rightarrow M\otimes^{\dagger}\pi_{S}(B)\right\},N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ker\left\{\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M\otimes^{\dagger}\pi_{S}(B),N)\rightarrow\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M\otimes^{\dagger}\pi_{S}(A),N)\right\}

and

\displaystyle\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M,\mathbb{D}_{S}(L\otimes^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{S}(N)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M,\mathbb{D}_{S}(\textup{coker}\left\{\pi_{S}(A)\rightarrow\pi_{S}(B)\right\}\otimes^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{S}(N)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M,\mathbb{D}_{S}(\textup{coker}\left\{\pi_{S}(A)\otimes^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{S}(N)\rightarrow\pi_{S}(B)\otimes^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{S}(N)\right\}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M,\ker\left\{\mathbb{D}_{S}(\pi_{S}(B)\otimes^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{S}(N))\rightarrow\mathbb{D}_{S}(\pi_{S}(A)\otimes^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{S}(N))\right\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ker\left\{\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M,\mathbb{D}_{S}(\pi_{S}(B)\otimes^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{S}(N)))\rightarrow\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)}(M,\mathbb{D}_{S}(\pi_{S}(A)\otimes^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{S}(N)))\right\}

via the previous case. It is clear from the construction that the adjunction obtained in this way is compatible with the corresponding adjunction on perverse sheaves.

∎

In
Section

6
we will explain how to prove that the same result holds in full generality, by combining our own results with certain related recent advances in the theory of Nori motives. We stress, however, that the results just mentioned rely crucially on the construction of internal homomorphisms obtained in
Section

5
, where, in turn, the essential ingredient is provided precisely by the tiny-looking
3.13
.

4.
Construction of the unit constraint

By
2.8
we have a canonical monoidal structure on perverse motives endowed with mutually compatible associativity and commutativity constraints. In order to complete the proof of
2.1
, we need to construct a unit constraint compatible with the rest of the structure. This is achieved in the present section building on the auxiliary results of
Section

3
. The extension techniques developed in
[
Ter23E
]
allow us to complete our construction by working with smooth varieties only: this will often prove convenient in order to avoid technical issues related to singular varieties. Over smooth varieties, we will often make use of shifted inverse image functors and shifted tensor products as defined in
2.10
and
3.5
, respectively.

As usual, we write perverse Nori motives with the symbol "
\mathscr{M}^{0}
" rather than as "
\mathscr{M}
" in all those passages that depend closely on the construction of the tensor product, but not in the statement of the main result:

Proposition 4.1
.

The internal tensor structure on
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
constructed in
2.8
carries a canonical internal unit constraint in the sense of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 5.1]
, with respect to which the morphism of
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered categories
\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
is canonically unitary in the sense of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 8.20]
.

In the first place, we need to construct the unit section for perverse motives.

Notation 4.2
.

We adopt the following notation:

•

We use the symbol
\mathbf{1}=(\mathbf{1}_{S})_{S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}}
to denote the canonical unit sections of the monoidal
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered categories
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
and
D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
.

•

We let
w:\mathsf{Bti}^{*}(\mathbf{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{1}
denote the canonical isomorphism of unit sections defined in
[
Ayo10
]
.

•

We use the symbol
u
to denote the internal unit constraints of
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
and
D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
.

4.1.
Construction of the unit section

In the first place, we need to define the unit section of the
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
.

Proposition 4.3
.

The
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
possesses a canonical unit section
\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}=(\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})_{S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}}
, and the morphism of
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered categories
\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
induces a canonical isomorphism of unit sections

\tilde{w}:\iota(\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{1}.

The basic technical issue is that, if a variety
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
is not smooth, the unit sheaf
\mathbf{1}_{S}\in D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
is not a perverse sheaf (not even up to decomposing
S
into connected components and shifting appropriately); in fact, a priori it is not obvious at all that the unit sheaf belongs to the essential image of the triangulated functor
\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))
. However, the structure of stable homotopy
2
-functor on
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
allows one to easily overcome this issue by working directly over smooth varieties only: this idea can be made rigorous via the embedding method of
[
Ter23E
, § 1]
, which we apply to the setting of fibered categories over
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
with respect to the full subcategory
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
of smooth varieties.

The core of the proof of
4.3
is constructing the isomorphisms witnessing the compatibility of the various motivic unit objects with respect to inverse image functors. Using a factorization method analogous to that of
[
Ter23F
]
, we easily reduce to the cases of smooth morphisms and closed immersions, which we treat separately. While the case of smooth morphisms is quite straightforward, the case of closed immersions is slightly tricky and requires some preliminary discussion.
Our approach consists in reducing to the case of closed immersions of codimension
1
, which can be treated directly by means of suitable localization triangles; it is plausible that the Čech complex constructions described in
Section

3
could lead to an alternative approach, but we have not tried to follow this direction.

Our preliminary results can be summarized as follows:

Lemma 4.4
.

Regard
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
and
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
as triangulated
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
-fibered categories with inverse image functors
f^{\dagger}
as in
2.10
.

Let
K
be a section of the
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
such that, for every connected
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, the object
K_{S}\in D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))
lies in degree
0
. Suppose that we are given, for every
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, and object
K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}\in\mathscr{M}(S)
together with an isomorphism in
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))

(72)

w_{S}:\iota_{S}(K_{S}^{\mathscr{M}})\xrightarrow{\sim}K_{S}.

Then the following statements hold:

(1)

Fix a morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
. Then there exists at most one morphism in
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(T))

(73)

K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{f}:f^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}\rightarrow K^{\mathscr{M}}_{T}

making the diagram in
\mathsf{Perv}(T)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\dagger}\iota_{S}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{T}(f^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{T}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\dagger}K_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{w}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{f}^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{w}_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{f}^{\dagger}}

commute; if this is the case, it is automatically an isomorphism.

(2)

Suppose that, for every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, the natural isomorphism (
73
) exists. Then the natural isomorphisms (
73
) turn the collection of objects
\left\{K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}\right\}_{S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}}
into a section
K
of the
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
, and the morphisms (
72
) define an isomorphism between sections of
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))

(74)

w:\iota(K^{\mathscr{M}})\xrightarrow{\sim}K.

(3)

Suppose that the isomorphism (
73
) is known to exist whenever
f
is either a smooth morphism or a closed immersion of codimension
1
between smooth connected varieties. Then the natural isomorphism (
73
) exists for every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
.

Proof.

(1)

The uniqueness of
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{f}
follows from the fact that
\iota_{T}:\mathscr{M}(T)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(T)
is faithful, while its invertibility follows from the fact that
\iota_{T}
is also conservative (being exact).

(2)

We only need to check that the isomorphisms (
73
) are compatible with composition: given two composable morphisms
f:T\rightarrow S
and
g:S\rightarrow V
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, we have to show that the diagram in
\mathscr{M}(T)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\dagger}g^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathscr{M}}_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{g}^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{f}^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{gf}^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}}

is commutative or, equivalently, that its image under the faithful functor
\iota_{T}:\mathscr{M}(T)\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(T)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{T}(f^{\dagger}g^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{T}(f^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{T}((gf)^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{T}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{T}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{g}^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{f}^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{gf}^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}}

is commutative. But the latter coincides with the outer rectangle of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{T}(f^{\dagger}g^{\dagger}K_{V}^{\mathscr{M}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{T}(f^{\dagger}K_{S}^{\mathscr{M}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\dagger}\iota_{S}(g^{\dagger}K_{V}^{\mathscr{M}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\dagger}\iota_{S}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\dagger}g^{\dagger}\iota_{V}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\dagger}g^{\dagger}K_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\dagger}K_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{\dagger}\iota_{V}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{\dagger}K_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{T}((gf)^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{g}^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{f}^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{g}^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{w}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{g}^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{f}^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{w}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}_{gf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{gf}^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{gf}^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{T}}

where the three five-term pieces are commutative by construction while all the other inner pieces are commutative by naturality.

(3)

As one can easily deduce from the proof of the previous point, for every two given composable morphisms
f:T\rightarrow S
and
g:S\rightarrow V
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
such that the isomorphisms
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{f}
and
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{g}
are already known to exist, the natural isomorphism
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{gf}
exists as well and equals the composite

(gf)^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{V}=f^{\dagger}g^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{V}\xrightarrow{K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{g}}f^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}\xrightarrow{K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{f}}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{T}.

This implies that it suffices to know that the isomorphism
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{f}
exists whenever
f
is a smooth morphism or a closed immersion in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
. Even better, once one knows that
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{p}
exists for every smooth morphism
p
(in particular, for every open immersion), it suffices to show that the isomorphism
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{z}
exists for every closed immersion
z
between smooth connected varieties.

In order to conclude, we now claim that, in order to know the existence of
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{z}
for every closed immersion between smooth varieties
z:Z\hookrightarrow S
, it suffices to know that it exists whenever
z
is a closed immersion of pure codimension
1
. To see this, recall that, given a closed immersion
z:Z\hookrightarrow S
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, it is always possible to find a finite open covering
\left\{S_{i}\subset S\right\}_{i\in I}
such that each closed immersion
z_{i}:z^{-1}(S_{i})\hookrightarrow S_{i}
can be written as a composite of closed immersions of pure codimension
1
. In this situation, for each index
i\in I
form the Cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{Z,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

Arguing as in the proof of the previous point one easily sees that, if the four isomorphism
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}
,
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}
,
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}
,
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}
all exist, then they make the diagram in
\mathscr{M}(Z_{i})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i}^{\dagger}K_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{i}^{\dagger}K_{S_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{Z_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{Z,i}^{\dagger}z^{\dagger}K_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{Z,i}^{\dagger}K_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{u_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{z_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{u_{Z,i}}}

commute. Conversely, since the
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
-fibered categories
\mathscr{M}(\cdot)
and
\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot)
are stacks for the Zariski topology, the existence of the isomorphisms
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{z_{i}}
,
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{u_{i}}
and
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{u_{Z,i}}
for all
i\in I
implies the existence of
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{z}
. This proves the claim and concludes the proof.

∎

Proof of
4.3
.

By
[
Ter23E
, Prop. 1.7, Lemma 2.7]
, it suffices to construct the motivic unit section
\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}
and the isomorphism
w:\iota(\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{1}
over
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
. To this end, we apply the criterion of
4.4
taking
K
to be the shifted unit section of the
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
: for every connected
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
we set
K_{S}:=\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)]
, and we extend the definition to general
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
in the obvious way. In a similar manner, we define the shifted motivic unit objects: for every connected
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
we set
K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}:=\pi_{S}(\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)])
, and we extend the definition to general
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
in the obvious way. Note that we have a canonical isomorphism in
\mathsf{Perv}(S)

(75)

\tilde{w}_{S}:\iota_{S}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})\xrightarrow{\sim}K_{S},

which is defined as the composite

\iota_{S}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}):=\iota_{S}\pi_{S}(\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)])=\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)])=\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)]=:K_{S}

in the case when
S
is connected and by the obvious extension in the general case. Finally, we define the unshifted motivic unit objects by setting
\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{S}:=K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}[-\dim(S)]
when
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
is connected, and extending the definition to general
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
in the obvious way. As above, we have a canonical isomorphism in
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))

(76)

\iota_{S}(\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{1}_{S},

defined as the composite

\iota_{S}(\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})=\iota_{S}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}[-\dim(S)])=\iota_{S}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})[-\dim(S)]\xrightarrow{\sim}K_{S}[-\dim(S)]=\mathbf{1}_{S}

when
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
is connected and by the obvious extension in the general case. Clearly, turning the collection
(\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})_{S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}}
into a section of
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
with respect to the usual inverse image functors
f^{*}
in such a way that the isomorphisms (
76
) define an isomorphism of sections is the same as turning the collection
(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})_{S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}}
into a section of
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
with respect to the shifted inverse image functors
f^{\dagger}
in such a way that the isomorphisms (
75
) define an isomorphism of sections.

By
4.4
(3), in order to achieve the latter task, it suffices to construct the isomorphisms
K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{f}
in the cases where
f
is either a smooth morphism or a closed immersion of codimension
1
. We treat these two cases separately as follows:

•

Let
p:P\rightarrow S
be a smooth morphism in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
. We define the isomorphism in
\mathscr{M}(P)

K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{p}:p^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}\xrightarrow{\sim}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{P}

by taking the composite

p^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}:=p^{\dagger}\pi_{S}(\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)])=\pi_{P}(p^{\dagger}\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)])\xrightarrow{\mathbf{1}_{p}^{\dagger}}\pi_{P}(\mathbf{1}_{P}[\dim(P)])=:K^{\mathscr{M}}_{P}.

The associated diagram in
\mathsf{Perv}(P)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\dagger}\iota_{S}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{P}(p^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{P}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\dagger}K_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{w}_{S}}
