\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{p}^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{w}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{p}^{\dagger}}

which can be more explicitly written in the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\dagger}\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{P}^{*}(p^{\dagger}\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{P}^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{P}[\dim(P)])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\dagger}\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{P}[\dim(P)]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{p}^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{p}^{\dagger}}

is commutative since
w:\mathsf{Bti}^{*}(\mathbf{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{1}
is a morphism of sections.

•

Let
z:Z\hookrightarrow S
be a closed immersion of codimension
1
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
. In order to define the isomorphism in
\mathscr{M}(Z)

\mathbf{1}_{z}^{\mathscr{M},*}:z^{\dagger}K_{S}^{\mathscr{M}}\xrightarrow{\sim}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z}

we describe the morphism in
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))

(77)

K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}\rightarrow z_{*}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z}[1]

corresponding to it under adjunction. To this end, let
u:U\hookrightarrow S
denote the open immersion complementary to
z
. Consider the distinguished triangle in
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})

(78)

z_{*}\mathbf{1}_{Z}[\dim(Z)]\rightarrow u_{!}u^{*}\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)]\rightarrow\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)]\xrightarrow{+}

obtained from the usual localization triangle

u_{!}u^{*}\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)]\rightarrow\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)]\rightarrow z_{*}z^{*}\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)]\xrightarrow{+}

after inserting the isomorphism
\mathbf{1}_{z}^{*}:z^{*}\mathbf{1}_{S}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{1}_{Z}
and rotating backwards. Note that all the terms in (
78
) belong to
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)
by construction. Hence, applying the functor
\pi_{S}:\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)
and using the canonical isomorphisms
\beta_{S}\circ z_{*}=z_{*}\circ\beta_{Z}
and
\beta_{S}\circ u_{!}u^{*}=u_{!}\circ\beta_{U}\circ u^{*}=u_{!}u^{*}\circ\beta_{S}
, we obtain a short exact sequence in
\mathscr{M}(S)

0\rightarrow z_{*}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z}\rightarrow u_{!}u^{*}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}\rightarrow K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}\rightarrow 0.

We define (
77
) to be the second arrow of the distinguished triangle in
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

u_{!}u^{*}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}\rightarrow K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}\rightarrow z_{*}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z}[1]\xrightarrow{+}

obtained by taking the triangle associated to this short exact sequence and rotating forwards.
By construction, showing the commutativity of the associated diagram in
\mathsf{Perv}(Z)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{\dagger}\iota_{S}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Z}(z^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Z}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{\dagger}K_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{w}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\theta}_{z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathscr{M},\dagger}_{z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{w}_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{z}^{\dagger}}

is equivalent to showing the commutativity of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}(z_{*}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z})[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}\iota_{Z}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z})[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}K_{Z}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{w}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\tilde{\theta}}_{z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{w}_{Z}}

Since the latter can be written equivalently in the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{Z}(\mathbf{1}_{Z}[\dim(Z)])[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z_{*}\mathbf{1}_{Z}[\dim(Z)])[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{w}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{z}}

its commutativity is in the end equivalent to that of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}_{Z}^{*}(z^{*}\mathbf{1}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bti}^{*}_{Z}(\mathbf{1}_{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}\mathbf{1}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{z}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{z}^{*}}

which indeed holds since
w:\mathsf{Bti}^{*}(\mathbf{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{1}
is a morphism of sections.

This concludes the proof.
∎

4.2.
Construction of the unit constraint

In order to prove
4.1
, we need to show that the
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
admits an internal unit constraint with respect to the motivic unit section constructed in
4.3
, compatibly with the usual unit constraint on
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
under the morphism of
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered categories
\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
.

As done in the previous subsection, it is useful to invoke the results of
[
Ter23E
, §§ 3, 4]
to reduce the construction to smooth varieties; this time, the main reason is that we want to apply
3.6
, which was formulated and proved for smooth varieties only.

The core of the proof of
4.1
is checking the compatibility of the action of the unit objects with respect to inverse image functors. Using the factorization ideas of
[
Ter23F
]
, we easily reduce to the cases of smooth morphisms and closed immersions, which we treat separately. Again, the case of smooth morphisms does not create any additional difficulties, while the case of closed immersion is a bit more subtle and requires some preliminary work. What we have to do is to rephrase the compatibility conditions considered in a form which only involves
t
-exact functors by replacing inverse images under closed immersions by the corresponding direct images.

For sake of brevity, we formulate our preliminary results only for the right unit action; of course, the same conclusions apply to the left unit action as well.

Lemma 4.5
.

Regard
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
as a
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
-fibered category, and consider the motivic uni section
\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}=(\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})_{S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}}
constructed in
4.3
. Suppose that we are given, for every
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, a natural isomorphism of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))

u_{r}=u_{r,S}:M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}\xrightarrow{\sim}M^{\bullet}.

Then the following statements hold:

(1)

For every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, let
D_{f}
denote the diagram of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(T))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes f^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}M^{\bullet}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{f}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}

Then, in order for
D_{f}
to be commutative for every morphism
f
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, it suffices that it be commutative for every smooth morphism and for every closed immersion.

(2)

Let
z:Z\hookrightarrow S
be a closed immersion in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, and let
D^{\prime}_{z}
denote the diagram
D^{\prime}_{z}
of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes z_{*}z^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes z_{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}(N^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}N^{\bullet}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{z}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}

Then
D_{z}
is commutative if and only if
D^{\prime}_{z}
is commutative.

Proof.

Both assertions follow by a standard application of the factorization method of
[
Ter23F
]
. Since unit constraints are not discussed in
[
Ter23F
]
, for the reader’s convenience we include the argument:

(1)

Given a morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, choose a factorization of it in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
of the form

f:T\xrightarrow{t}P\xrightarrow{p}S

with
t
a closed immersion and
p
a smooth morphism; note that
P
is smooth since so is
S
as in the statement, so this factorization exists in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
. Then, by construction, the diagram
D_{f}
coincides with the outer part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes f^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{*}p^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes t^{*}p^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{*}(p^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes p^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{*}p^{*}(M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{*}p^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes t^{*}\mathbf{1}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{*}(p^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{*}p^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{*}p^{*}M^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}M^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M},*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M},*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M},*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M},*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

where the two central trapezoids are essentially the diagrams
D_{p}
and
D_{z}
. Since all the remaining pieces of the diagram are already known to be commutative, the thesis follows.

(2)

Let
D^{\prime\prime}_{z}
denote the diagram of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(Z))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes z^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}(z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}z_{*}N^{\bullet}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{z}^{\mathscr{M},*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}

We claim that the commutativity of
D_{z}
and of
D^{\prime}_{z}
are both equivalent to the commutativity of
D^{\prime\prime}_{z}
. We prove each equivalence directly as follows:

•

It is clear that the commutativity of
D_{z}
implies that of
D^{\prime\prime}_{z}
. Conversely,
D_{z}
can be written as the outer part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes z^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}(M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}z_{*}z^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes z^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}(z_{*}z^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}z_{*}z^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}z_{*}z^{*}M^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}M^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

where the central square is essentially
D^{\prime\prime}_{z}
and the lateral pieces are already known to be commutative. This shows that
D_{z}
is commutative if and only if so is
D^{\prime\prime}_{z}
.

•

The diagram
D^{\prime}_{z}
can be written as the outer part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes z_{*}z^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}z^{*}(z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes z_{*}z^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}z^{*}(z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes z_{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}(z^{*}z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes z^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\bullet}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}z^{*}z_{*}N^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}z^{*}(z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes z_{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}(z^{*}z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes z^{*}z_{*}z^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}(z^{*}z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\bullet}_{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}z^{*}(z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes z_{*}\mathbf{1}^{\bullet}_{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}(z^{*}z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes z^{*}z_{*}\mathbf{1}^{\bullet}_{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}(N^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\bullet}_{Z})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M},*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M},*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M},*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M},*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}

where all arrows are invertible by construction and all pieces except possibly the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}(z^{*}z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes z^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}z^{*}(z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}(z^{*}z_{*}N^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}z^{*}z_{*}N^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{z}^{\mathscr{M},*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}

are commutative by naturality. Hence
D^{\prime}_{z}
is commutative if and only if the above square is commutative; since the functor
z_{*}:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
is fully faithful, this is the case if and only if
D^{\prime\prime}_{z}
is commutative.

This proves the claim and concludes the proof.

∎

In order to be able to apply
4.5
(2), we need to use the following variant of
3.6
:

Proposition 4.6
.

Let

be a closed immersion in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
. Then the bi-exact functor

z_{*}(-)\otimes z_{*}(-[-\dim(Z)])=(z_{*}(-)\otimes z_{*}(-))[-\dim(Z)]:\mathscr{M}^{0}(Z)\times\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(Z)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)

is canonically isomorphic to the functor obtained by applying

, Prop. 4.3]
to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(Z)\times\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(Z)\times\mathsf{Perv}(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z_{*}(-)\otimes z_{*}(-))[-\dim(Z)]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{0}_{Z}\times\beta^{0,loc}_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{0}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z_{*}(-)\otimes z_{*}(-))[-\dim(Z)]}

Proof.

As the reader can check, the argument used for the proof of

∎

Proof of
4.1
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S).}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S}^{0}}

The shifted right (resp. left) unit isomorphisms of functors
\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{D}^{0}(S)

u_{r}=u_{r,S}:A\otimes^{\dagger}\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)]\xrightarrow{\sim}A,\qquad u_{l}=u_{l,S}:\mathbf{1}_{S}[\dim(S)]\otimes^{\dagger}A\xrightarrow{\sim}A

and the shifted right (resp. left) unit isomorphism of functors

satisfy the compatibility condition of
[

, Defn. 3.1]
, because the same holds for the corresponding natural isomorphisms on

and on

- this is just a way of rephrasing the unitarity of

) when evaluated on the shifted object

Therefore, applying
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 3.4]
, we obtain two canonical natural isomorphisms between functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)

(79)

\tilde{u}_{r}=\tilde{u}_{r,S}:M\otimes^{\dagger}\mathbf{1}_{S}^{\mathscr{M}}[\dim(S)]\xrightarrow{\sim}M,\qquad\tilde{u}_{l}=\tilde{u}_{l,S}:\mathbf{1}_{S}^{\mathscr{M}}[\dim(S)]\otimes^{\dagger}M\xrightarrow{\sim}M

compatible with the corresponding shifted unit isomorphisms on
\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot)
; they coincide (up to a sign
(-1)^{\dim(S)}
) when evaluated on the shifted object
\mathbf{1}_{S}^{\mathscr{M}}[\dim(S)]
. Applying
2.5
, unshifting the unit objects and the tensor product functors, and applying
3.6
, we obtain two unit natural isomorphisms of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

(80)

\tilde{u}_{r}=\tilde{u}_{r,S}:M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{S}^{\mathscr{M}}\xrightarrow{\sim}M^{\bullet},\qquad\tilde{u}_{l}=\tilde{u}_{l,S}:\mathbf{1}_{S}^{\mathscr{M}}\otimes M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\sim}M^{\bullet}

again compatible with the corresponding unit isomorphisms on
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
;
they coincide (with no sign) when evaluated on the unshifted object
\mathbf{1}_{S}^{\mathscr{M}}
, thereby satisfying condition (
u
ITS-1) of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 5.1]
.

It remains to show that the natural isomorphisms (
80
) satisfy condition (
u
ITS-2) of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 5.1]
. For sake of simplicity, we only write the proof for the right unit action; of course, the same argument will apply to the left unit action as well. What we have to show in the case of the right unit action is that, for every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, the diagram
D_{f}
associated to
f
as in the statement of
4.5
is commutative. To this end, applying
4.5
(1) we reduce to considering the cases where
f
is either a smooth morphism or a closed immersion; applying
4.5
(2), we can also rephrase the case of a closed immersion
z
in terms of the commutativity of the alternative diagram
D^{\prime}_{z}
introduced there. Here are the details:

•

Let
p:P\rightarrow S
be a smooth morphism in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
. The diagram of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(P))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes p^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}(M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}M^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{p}^{\mathscr{M},*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{u}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{u}_{r}}

is commutative if and only if so is the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\dagger}M^{\bullet}\otimes^{\dagger}p^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\dagger}(M^{\bullet}\otimes^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\dagger}M^{\bullet}\otimes K^{\mathscr{M}}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\dagger}M^{\bullet}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{p}^{\mathscr{M},*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{u}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{u}_{r}}

Since all the functors involved in the latter diagram are both dg-enhanced and
t
-exact for the obvious
t

2.6
(1) they coincide with the trivial derived functors of the corresponding exact functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(P)
. Hence (see

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\dagger}M\otimes p^{\dagger}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\dagger}M\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\dagger}M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{p}^{\mathscr{M},*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{u}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{u}_{r}}

is commutative. Since all the functors and natural transformations involved in the latter diagram come from the lifting principles of universal abelian factorizations, it suffices to show that the corresponding diagram of functors in
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
is commutative, which follows from the validity of axiom (
u
ITS-2) there.

•

Let
z:Z\hookrightarrow S
be a closed immersion in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
. By
4.5
(2), it suffices to show that the diagram of triangulated functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{*}M^{\bullet}\otimes z_{*}z^{*}\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}}

is commutative. Since all the functors involved in this diagram are dg-enhanced and

, all the functors and natural transformations appearing in the latter diagram come from the lifting principles of universal abelian factorizations. Thus it suffices to show that the corresponding diagram of functors in

∎

4.3.
Compatibility with the associativity and commutativity constraints

In order to make the picture complete, it remains to study the compatibility between the unit constraint of
4.1
and the associativity and commutativity constraints constructed in
2.11
and
2.12
. Since the unit constraint has been defined in the language of internal tensor structures, whereas the associativity and commutativity constraints have been defined in the language of external tensor structures, we need to decide whether to check the two compatibility conditions in the internal or in the external setting. Working in the external setting turns out to be easier, since everything can be more directly related to the lifting principles of universal abelian factorizations. In order to explain why this is the case, we start with the following observation:

Remark 4.7
.

For every
S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Downarrow}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes K_{S_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}\times S_{2}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boxtimes K_{S_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr^{12,\dagger}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{S_{1}\times S_{2}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr^{12,\dagger}_{1}}

Therefore, applying
[
Ter23U
, Prop. 3.4]
, we obtain a canonical natural isomorphism between functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2})

M_{1}\boxtimes K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S_{2}}\xrightarrow{\sim}pr^{12,\dagger}_{1}M_{1}.

As a consequence of
3.6
we see that, for every
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, the motivic right unit isomorphism in (
79
) coincides with the composite isomorphism

M_{1}\otimes^{\dagger}K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}=M_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}:=\Delta_{S}^{*}(M_{1}\boxtimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})=\Delta_{S}^{\dagger}(M_{1}\boxtimes K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Delta_{S}^{\dagger}pr^{12,\dagger}_{1}M_{1}=M_{1}.

Of course, the analogue result holds for the motivic left unit isomorphism.

We can now easily deduce the sought-after compatibility properties as follows:

Lemma 4.8
.

The external unit constraint obtained from
4.1
and the external associativity constraint of
2.11
are compatible in the sense of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 6.7]
.

Proof.

We have to show that the associativity and unit constraints satisfy condition (
au
ETS) from
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 6.7]
. For sake of simplicity, we only check the commutativity of the third family of diagrams mentioned there; the commutativity of the other two families follows by a similar argument. Using the analogue of
[
Ter23E
, Lemma 3.11(2)]
for external tensor structures, we see that it suffices to consider smooth varieties only. In the end, what we have to show is that, for every
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, the diagram of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{2}))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{3}))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes M_{2}^{\bullet})\boxtimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes(M_{2}^{\bullet}\boxtimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S_{3}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr^{123,*}_{23}(M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes M_{2}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes pr^{12,*}_{2}M_{2}^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

is commutative. After shifting the unit objects, using
4.7
, we reduce to showing that the diagram of functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1})\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{3})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})\boxtimes K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\boxtimes(M_{2}\boxtimes K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S_{3}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr^{123,\dagger}_{23}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\boxtimes pr^{12,\dagger}_{2}M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

is commutative. Since all the functors and natural transformations appearing in the latter diagram come from the lifting principles of universal abelian factorizations, it suffices to check that the corresponding diagram on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
is commutative, which (after unshifting the unit objects) follows from the validity of axiom (
au
ETS) there.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

The internal unit constraint of
4.1
and the internal commutativity constraint obtained from
2.12
are compatible in the sense of
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 6.11]
.

Proof.

We have to show that the commutativity and unit constraints satisfy condition (
cu
ETS) from
[
Ter23T
, Defn. 6.11]
. Using the analogue of
[
Ter23E
, Lemma 3.11(3)]
for external tensor structure, we see that it suffices to consider smooth varieties only. In the end, what we have to show is that, for every
S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, the diagram of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S_{1}))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S_{1}\times S_{2}))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}(\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S_{2}}\boxtimes M_{1}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr^{12,*}_{1}M_{1}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}pr^{21,*}_{1}M_{1}^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{l}}

is commutative. After shifting the unit object, using
4.7
, we reduce to showing that the diagram of functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S_{1}\times S_{2})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\boxtimes K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}(K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S_{2}}\boxtimes M_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr^{12,\dagger}_{1}M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}pr^{21,\dagger}_{1}M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{l}}

is commutative. Since all the functors and natural transformations appearing in the latter diagram come from the lifting principles of universal abelian factorizations, it suffices to check that the corresponding diagram on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
is commutative, which (after unshifting the unit object) follows from the validity of axiom (
cu
ETS) there.
∎

Proof of
2.1
.

Combine the results of
2.8
,
2.11
,
2.12
and
2.13
obtained previously with the results of
4.1
,
4.8
and
4.9
obtained in the present section.
∎

5.
Internal homomorphisms

In this section, we complete the construction of the six functor formalism on perverse motives by showing the existence of internal homomorphisms and their compatibility with those on the underlying categories of perverse sheaves. As usual, we state the main result in the language of stable homotopy
2
-functors:

Theorem 5.1
.

The monoidal stable homotopy
2
-functor
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
is closed in the sense of
[
Ayo07a
, Defn. 2.3.50]
.
Moreover, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the canonical natural transformation between functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))^{op}\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))

\iota_{S}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M_{1}^{\bullet},M_{2}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\iota_{S}(M_{1}^{\bullet}),\iota_{S}(M_{2}^{\bullet}))

is invertible.

The strategy of the proof consists of two main steps: on the one hand, an abstract construction of internal homomorphisms as an ind-adjoint of the internal tensor product; on the other side, the adjunction for distinguished motivic local systems obtained in
3.13
. As usual, following the conventions of
Section

2
, we write the categories of perverse motives as "
\mathscr{M}^{0}
" in all constructions and proofs specifically involving the tensor product, and simply as "
\mathscr{M}
" otherwise.

The abstract construction of internal homomorphism is inspired by Arapura’s construction of motivic direct images in
[
Ara13
, § 5.1]
, based on a suitable adjoint functor theorem for derived categories of Grothendieck abelian categories from
[
Fra01
]
. Compared to Arapura’s case, our construction requires a longer preparation in order to extend the internal tensor product to the level of derived ind-categories.

5.1.
Recollections on ind-categories

We start by reviewing some facts about ind-categories of (Noetherian) abelian categories and their derived categories. Recall from
[
SGA4
, Exp. 1, § 8.2]
that, for every small category
\mathscr{C}
, the ind-category
\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{C}
is the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Fun}(\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathbf{Sets})
consisting of all those presheaves which are filtered colimits of representables; the Yoneda embedding
\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Fun}(\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathbf{Sets})
can be refined to a fully faithful functor
\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{C}
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be an essentially small abelian category. Then:

(1)

The category
\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}
is a Grothendieck abelian category, and the canonical embedding
\mathscr{A}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}
is exact.

(2)

If
\mathscr{A}
is Noetherian, then its essential image in
\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}
consists of the subcategory of Noetherian objects inside
\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}
.

(3)

The canonical triangulated functor
D^{b}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A})
is fully faithful and induces an equivalence

D^{b}(\mathscr{A})\xrightarrow{\sim}D^{b}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}):=\left\{A^{\bullet}\in D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A})\;|\;H^{n}(A^{\bullet})=0\;\forall|n|\gg 0,\;H^{n}(A^{\bullet})\in\mathscr{A}\;\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

Proof.

The first statement is proved in
[
KS06
, Thm. 8.6.5]
; the second one is an easy consequence of
[
Hub93
, Lemma 1.3]
; the third one follows from
[
KS06
, Prop. 13.1.12(i), Thm. 15.3.1(i)]
.
∎

We need to extend certain additive functors of abelian categories to the level of ind-categories and to their derived categories. The following result describes this in detail:

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
F:\mathscr{A}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}_{2}
be a functor between abelian categories. Then:

(1)

Up to canonical natural isomorphism, there exists a unique functor

\mathrm{Ind}(F):\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}_{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}_{2}

extending
F
and commuting with arbitrary small filtered colimits.

(2)

If
F
is additive, then so it
\mathrm{Ind}(F)
.

(3)

If
F
is faithful (resp. full) then so is
\mathrm{Ind}(F)
.

(4)

If
F
is left-exact (resp. right-exact), then so is
\mathrm{Ind}(F)
.

Moreover, the assignment
F\mapsto\mathrm{Ind}(F)
is canonically compatible with composition of functors.

Proof.

The first statement is proved in
[
SGA4
, Exp. 1, § 8.6]
and does not require the assumption that
\mathscr{A}_{1}
and
\mathscr{A}_{2}
are abelian; the second statement follows directly from the construction of
\mathrm{Ind}(F)
; the third statement is
[
KS06
, Prop. 6.1.10]
, and the fourth statement is
[
KS06
, Cor. 8.6.8]
. Finally, the compatibility with composition follows directly from the construction.
∎

Similarly, we need to extend certain natural transformations to the level of ind-categories. This can be done as follows:

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
\mathscr{A}_{1}
and
\mathscr{A}_{2}
be two abelian categories. Then:

(1)

Given two functors
F,G:\mathscr{A}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}_{2}
, and a natural transformation
\alpha:F\rightarrow G
, the following hold:

(i)

There exists a unique natural transformation of functors
\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}_{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}_{2}

\mathrm{Ind}(\alpha):\mathrm{Ind}(F)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}(G)

such that, for every object
A=\varinjlim_{i\in I}A_{i}
of
\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}_{1}
, the diagram in
\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}_{2}

(81)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{i\in I}F(A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{i\in I}G(A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ind}(F)(\varinjlim_{i\in I}A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ind}(G)(\varinjlim_{i\in I}A_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{i\in I}\alpha(A_{i})}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ind}(\alpha)(A)}

is commutative.

(ii)

The natural transformation
\mathrm{Ind}(\alpha)
is invertible if and only if
\alpha
is invertible.

(2)

Given three functors
F,G,H:\mathscr{A}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}_{2}
, for every choice of natural transformations
\alpha:F\rightarrow G
and
\beta:G\rightarrow H
, we have the equality

\mathrm{Ind}(\beta\circ\alpha)=\mathrm{Ind}(\beta)\circ\mathrm{Ind}(\alpha).

Proof.

In the first statement, (i) follows from the fact that the commutativity of the diagram (
81
) uniquely determines
\mathrm{Ind}(\alpha)
, while (ii) is a consequence of the second statement of the proposition; the latter follows from the explicit definition of
\mathrm{Ind}(\alpha)
and
\mathrm{Ind}(\beta)
via commutative diagrams of the form (
81
).
∎

The main case of interest for us concerns the unit and co-unit transformations associated to an adjoint pair, in which case we can apply the following criterion:

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
\mathscr{A}_{1}
and
\mathscr{A}_{2}
be abelian categories, and let

F:\mathscr{A}_{1}\leftrightarrows\mathscr{A}_{2}:G

be an adjoint pair of functors. Then the functors
\mathrm{Ind}(F)
and
\mathrm{Ind}(G)
fit into an adjunction

\mathrm{Ind}(F):\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}_{1}\leftrightarrows\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}_{2}:\mathrm{Ind}(G)

compatible with the original one.

Proof.

Indeed, one can use
5.4
to extend the unit and co-unit transformations to the level of ind-categories, as well as to check that the extended natural transformations still satisfy the triangular identities of adjunctions.
∎

We also need to extend some short exact sequences of exact functors to the level of ind-categories. To this end, we note the following result:

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\mathscr{A}_{1}
and
\mathscr{A}_{2}
be essentially small abelian categories. Then, for every short exact sequence of functors
\mathscr{A}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}_{2}

0\rightarrow F^{\prime}\rightarrow F\rightarrow F^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 0,

the induced sequence of functors
\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}_{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}_{2}

0\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}(F^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}(F)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}(F^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow 0

is exact as well.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{A}_{2}
is a Grothendieck abelian category by
5.2
(1), the thesis follows from the fact that filtered colimits of exact sequences in Grothendieck abelian categories are exact by definition.
∎

5.2.
Indization of perverse motives

In order to construct motivic internal homomorphisms, we first need to extend part of the functoriality of the stable homotopy
2
-functor
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
to the assignment
S\mapsto D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))
.

Specifically, we are interested in direct and inverse images under open and closed immersions. By
5.3
, for every closed immersion
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
, the fully faithful exact functor
i_{*}:\mathscr{M}(Z)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)
extends to a fully faithful exact functor

i_{*}:\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S).

Similarly, for every open immersion
j:U\hookrightarrow S
, the exact functor
j^{*}:\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(U)
extends to an exact functor

j^{*}:\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(U).

Moreover, if
j
is affine, the fully faithful exact functors
j_{!},j_{*}:\mathscr{M}(U)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)
extend to fully faithful exact functors

j_{!},j_{*}:\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(U)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S)

and, using
5.5
, we obtain canonical adjunction

j_{!}:\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(U)\leftrightarrows\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S):j^{*},\qquad\qquad j^{*}:\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S)\leftrightarrows\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(U):j_{*}.

The result of
[
IM19
, Prop. 4.2]
can be generalized to the setting of ind-categories as follows:

Proposition 5.7
.

For every closed immersion
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
, the triangulated functor
i_{*}:D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))
admits a canonical left adjoint

i^{*}:D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z))

sending
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
to
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(Z))
.

Following the argument for the proof of
[
IM19
, Prop. 4.2]
, we start by establishing an auxiliary result:

Lemma 5.8
.

Given a closed immersion
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
with complementary open immersion
j:U\hookrightarrow S
, the triangulated functor
i_{*}:D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))

is fully faithful and induces an equivalence

D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z))\xrightarrow{\sim}D_{Z}(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S)):=\ker\left\{j^{*}:D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(U))\right\}.

Proof.

As in the proof of
[
IM19
, Thm. 4.1]
, one reduces formally to the case where
S\in\mathsf{Aff}_{k}
and
Z=V(f)
for some
f\in\mathscr{O}(S)
. In this case, consider the exact functor

\Phi_{f}:\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z).

We claim that the restriction to
D_{Z}(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))
of its trivial derived functor

\Phi_{f}:D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z))

provides a quasi-inverse to
i_{*}
.
The natural isomorphism of functors
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z))

\Phi_{f}(i_{*}M^{\bullet})=M^{\bullet}

is induced by the natural isomorphism (
20
) after passing to the ind-completions and deriving. The natural isomorphisms of functors
D_{Z}(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D_{Z}(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))

i_{*}\Phi_{f}(M^{\bullet})=M^{\bullet}

is obtained from the distinguished triangles of functors
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))

i_{*}\Psi_{f}(j^{*}M^{\bullet})\rightarrow\Omega_{f}(M^{\bullet})\rightarrow M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{+},\qquad j_{!}j^{*}M^{\bullet}\rightarrow\Omega_{f}(M^{\bullet})\rightarrow i_{*}\Phi_{f}(M^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{+}

induced from the short exact sequences of exact functors
\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S)

extending (
15
) and (
16
), taking into account
5.6
above.
∎

Proof of
5.7
.

We have to show that the functor
i_{*}:D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))
admits a left adjoint sending
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
to
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(Z))
. As a consequence of
5.8
, this is equivalent to showing that the inclusion functor
D_{Z}(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))\subset D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))
admits a left adjoint sending
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
to
D^{b}_{Z}(\mathscr{M}(S))
. The latter can be constructed using Čech complexes in the same way as in the proof of
[
IM19
, Prop. 4.2]
; see also
3.7
.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

(1)

For every pair of composable closed immersions
z^{\prime}:Z^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Z
and
z:Z\hookrightarrow S
in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the natural isomorphism of exact functors
\mathscr{M}(Z^{\prime}))\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(Z)

\overline{\textup{conn}}_{z^{\prime},z}:(zz^{\prime})_{*}M\xrightarrow{\sim}z_{*}z^{\prime}_{*}M

induces an analogous isomorphism of the corresponding exact functors
\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S)
via
5.4
, whence an analogous natural isomorphism of their trivial derived functors
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z^{\prime}))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))
. By adjunction, we obtain a canonical isomorphism of functors
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z^{\prime}))

\textup{conn}_{z^{\prime},z}:(zz^{\prime})^{*}M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\sim}{z^{\prime}}^{*}z^{*}M^{\bullet}.

(2)

Consider a Cartesian square in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

where both
z
and
i
are closed immersions. Then the natural transformation of functors
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(V))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z))

z^{*}i_{*}M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\eta}z^{*}i_{*}z_{V,*}z_{V}^{*}M^{\bullet}=z^{*}z_{*}i_{Z,*}z_{V}^{*}M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}i_{Z,*}z_{V}^{*}M^{\bullet}

is invertible. This is equivalent to the invertibility of the unit arrow, which can be shown via the associated localization triangle; the existence of the latter follows directly from the construction of
[
IM19
, Prop. 4.2]
.

(3)

Consider a Cartesian square in
\mathsf{Var}_{k}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

where
z
is a closed immersion and
v
is an affine open immersion. Since the natural transformation of exact functors
\mathscr{M}(Z)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(V)

v^{*}z_{*}M\xrightarrow{\eta}z_{V,*}z_{V}^{*}v^{*}z_{*}M=z_{V,*}v_{Z}^{*}z^{*}z_{*}M\xrightarrow{\epsilon}z_{V,*}v_{Z}^{*}M

is invertible by proper base-change, by
5.4
the same is true for the induced natural transformation of exact functors
\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(V)
, and hence also for the natural transformation of their trivial derived functors
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(V))
. We deduce that the corresponding natural transformation between left adjoints
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(V))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(Z))

v_{Z,!}z_{V}^{*}M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\eta}v_{Z,!}z_{V}^{*}v^{*}v_{!}M^{\bullet}=v_{Z,!}v_{Z}^{*}z^{*}v_{!}M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}z^{*}v_{!}M^{\bullet}

is invertible as well. Moreover, it is clear from the construction that the natural transformation of functors
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(V_{Z}))

z_{V}^{*}v^{*}M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\eta}z_{V}^{*}v^{*}z_{*}z^{*}M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\sim}z_{V}^{*}z_{V,*}v_{Z}^{*}z^{*}M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}v_{Z}^{*}z^{*}M^{\bullet}

is invertible.

(4)

In fact, following
[
IM19
, § 4.2]
, one could turn the assignment
S\mapsto D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}(S))
into a
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category. We omit the details.

5.3.
Abstract construction of internal homomorphisms

After the above preliminaries, we are ready to describe our abstrac construction of internal homomorphisms on the derived ind-categories of perverse motives. Here are the details:

Construction 5.10
.

Fix
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
. For every object
M^{\bullet}\in C^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
, we construct a canonical triangulated functor

(82)

-\otimes M^{\bullet}:D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

extending the functor
-\otimes M^{\bullet}:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
.
Since the latter is, by definition, the composite

D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\xrightarrow{-\boxtimes M^{\bullet}}D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S\times S))\xrightarrow{\Delta_{S}^{*}}D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)),

the task reduces to constructing the two single extensions

(83)

-\boxtimes M^{\bullet}:D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S\times S))

and

(84)

\Delta_{S}^{*}:D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S\times S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

and, since the second extension has already been defined in
5.7
, it remains to construct the first one. To this end, choose
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
M^{\bullet}\in C^{[a,b]}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
. Recall from
[
KS06
, Lemma 15.4.1]
that we have a canonical identification

\mathrm{Ind}C^{[a,b]}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S\times S))=C^{[a,b]}(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S\times S)).

Consider the exact functor

-\boxtimes M^{\bullet}:\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}C^{[a,b]}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S\times S))=C^{[a,b]}(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S\times S))

extending the functor
-\boxtimes M^{\bullet}:\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\rightarrow C^{[a,b]}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S\times S))
. By construction, the induced functor

-\boxtimes M^{\bullet}:D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(C^{[a,b]}(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S\times S)))\xrightarrow{\textup{Tot}^{\bullet}}D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S\times S))

does not depend on the choice of the integers
a,b
and defines the sought-after extension (
83
).
Using the independence from
a,b
of the construction for each single
M^{\bullet}
, one can easily see that the construction of the functor (
82
) is covariantly functorial with respect to
M^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
.

Note that both functors (
83
) and (
84
) commute with small direct sums: for the first one, this follows from the construction (as the functor
\textup{Tot}^{\bullet}
commutes with small direct sums); for the second one, this follows from the fact that it is a left adjoint. Therefore the functor (
82
) commutes with small direct sums as well.
Since, by
[
Fra01
, Thm. 3.1]
the category
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
satisfies Brown representability, we deduce that, for every fixed
B^{\bullet}\in D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
, the functor

D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)),\quad A^{\bullet}\rightsquigarrow\textup{Hom}_{D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))}(A^{\bullet}\otimes M^{\bullet},B^{\bullet})

is representable by an object
\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M^{\bullet},B^{\bullet})\in D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
. By construction, the assignment
B^{\bullet}\rightsquigarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M^{\bullet},B^{\bullet})
is functorial in
B^{\bullet}\in D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
, and so defines a functor

(85)

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M^{\bullet},-):D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

which is a triangulated right adjoint to (
82
). Since, as already noted above, the construction of (
82
) is covariantly functorial in
M^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
, we deduce that the construction of (
85
) is contravariantly functorial in
M^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
.

Note that it is not clear a priori whether (
85
) also sends
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
to itself; in fact, showing this is one of the main points in the proof of
5.1
.

In order to understand the abstract internal homomorphisms functors just defined better, we now study their compatibility with open and closed immersions.

Construction 5.11
.

For every open immersion
j:U\hookrightarrow S
, we have a canonical natural isomorphism of functors
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))

\tilde{m}=\tilde{m}_{j}:j^{*}A^{\bullet}\otimes j^{*}M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\sim}j^{*}(A^{\bullet}\otimes M^{\bullet})

extending the given isomorphism of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
. It is obtained as the composite

j^{*}A^{\bullet}\otimes j^{*}M^{\bullet}:=\Delta_{U}^{*}(j^{*}A^{\bullet}\boxtimes j^{*}M^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\tilde{m}}\Delta_{U}^{*}(j\times j)^{*}(A^{\bullet}\boxtimes M^{\bullet})=j^{*}\Delta_{U}^{*}(A^{\bullet}\boxtimes M^{\bullet})=:j^{*}(A^{\bullet}\otimes M^{\bullet})

where the first arrow is obtained from the natural isomorphism of bi-exact functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)

\tilde{m}_{j,j}:j^{*}A^{\bullet}\boxtimes j^{*}M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\sim}(j\times j)^{*}(A^{\bullet}\boxtimes M^{\bullet})

after passing to the ind-completion in the first variable and then to the derived categories, while the second arrow is described in
5.9
(3).

Similarly, for every closed immersion
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
, we have a canonical natural isomorphism of functors
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(Z))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Z))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

\bar{\tilde{m}}=\bar{\tilde{m}}_{i}:i_{*}A^{\bullet}\otimes i_{*}M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\sim}i_{*}(A^{\bullet}\otimes M^{\bullet})

extending the given isomorphism of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Z))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Z))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
. It is obtained as the composite

i_{*}A^{\bullet}\otimes i_{*}M^{\bullet}=\Delta_{S}^{*}(i_{*}A^{\bullet}\boxtimes i_{*}M^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\bar{\tilde{m}}}\Delta_{S}^{*}(i\times i)_{*}(A^{\bullet}\boxtimes M^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\sim}i_{*}\Delta_{Z}^{*}(A^{\bullet}\boxtimes M^{\bullet})=:i_{*}(A^{\bullet}\otimes M^{\bullet}).

where the first arrow is obtained from the natural isomorphism of bi-exact functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(Z)\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(Z)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S\times S)

\bar{\tilde{m}}_{i,i}:i_{*}A\boxtimes i_{*}M\xrightarrow{\sim}(i\times i)_{*}(A\boxtimes M)

after passing to the ind-completion in the first variable and then to the derived categories, while the second arrow is described in
5.9
(2).

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
j:U\hookrightarrow S
be an affine open immersion, and let
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
denote the complementary closed immersion. Then the natural transformations of functors
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

(86)

j_{!}(j^{*}A^{\bullet}\otimes j^{*}M^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\eta}j_{!}(j^{*}A\otimes j^{*}j_{!}j^{*}M)\xrightarrow{\tilde{m}}j_{!}j^{*}(A\otimes j_{!}j^{*}M)\xrightarrow{\epsilon}A^{\bullet}\otimes j_{!}j^{*}M^{\bullet}

and

(87)

A^{\bullet}\otimes i_{*}i^{*}M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\eta}i_{*}i^{*}A^{\bullet}\otimes i_{*}i^{*}M^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\bar{\tilde{m}}}i_{*}(i^{*}A^{\bullet}\otimes i^{*}M^{\bullet})

are invertible.

Proof.

Showing that the natural transformation (
86
) is invertible is equivalent to showing the invertibility of the co-unit transformation

\epsilon:j_{!}j^{*}(A^{\bullet}\otimes j_{!}j^{*}M^{\bullet})\rightarrow A^{\bullet}\otimes j_{!}j^{*}M^{\bullet}.

The latter can be written as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}(A^{\bullet}\otimes j_{!}j^{*}M^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\bullet}\otimes j_{!}j^{*}M^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}\Delta_{S}^{*}(A^{\bullet}\boxtimes j_{!}j_{*}M^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S}^{*}(A^{\bullet}\boxtimes j_{!}j^{*}M^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}\Delta_{U}^{*}(j\times j)^{*}(A^{\bullet}\boxtimes j_{!}j^{*}M^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S}^{*}(j\times j)_{!}(j\times j)^{*}(A^{\bullet}\boxtimes j_{!}j^{*}M^{\bullet})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}

where the central arrow is invertible by
5.9
(3). Thus it suffices to show the invertibility of the co-unit

\epsilon:(j\times j)_{!}(j\times j)^{*}(A^{\bullet}\boxtimes j_{!}j^{*}M^{\bullet})\rightarrow A^{\bullet}\boxtimes j_{!}j^{*}M^{\bullet},

which follows from the invertibility of the analogous natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\times\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0}(S\times S)
.
The same argument allows us to conclude that the co-unit transformation of functors
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

\epsilon:j_{!}j^{*}A^{\bullet}\otimes j_{!}j^{*}M^{\bullet}\rightarrow j_{!}j^{*}A^{\bullet}\otimes M^{\bullet}

is invertible. By the localization triangle in the second variable, this implies the vanishing of the functor
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

j_{!}j^{*}A^{\bullet}\otimes i_{*}i^{*}M^{\bullet}=0.

By the localization triangle in the first variable, this in turn implies that the unit transformation of functors
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

\eta:A^{\bullet}\otimes i_{*}i^{*}M^{\bullet}\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}A^{\bullet}\otimes i_{*}i^{*}M^{\bullet}

is invertible, which is equivalent to the invertibility of the natural transformation (
87
).
∎

Corollary 5.13
.

Keep the notation and assumptions of
5.12
. Then there exist canonical natural isomorphisms of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))^{op}\times D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(j_{!}j^{*}M^{\bullet},B^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\sim}j_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(j^{*}M^{\bullet},j^{*}B^{\bullet}),\quad\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(i_{*}i^{*}M^{\bullet},B^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\sim}i_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{Z}(i^{*}M^{\bullet},i^{!}B^{\bullet}).

Proof.

For every fixed
M^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
, the natural isomorphisms of functors
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
in the statement are obtained by adjunction from those of
5.12
. Since the latter natural isomorphisms are functorial in
M^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
, so are those in the statement.
∎

5.4.
Proof of the main result

The strategy for the proof of
5.1
is based on two abstract results that we now describe. For sake of clarity we formulate them in the setting of perverse motives and perverse sheaves, but they might be useful in more general settings as well.

In the first place, we need a criterion ensuring that the abstract internal homomorphisms just constructed respect the categories
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
.

Proposition 5.14
.

Suppose that, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
and every
M\in\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
there exists a dense affine open immersion
j:U\hookrightarrow S
such that the functor

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(j^{*}M,-):D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))

sends
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))
to itself. Then, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the functor

(88)

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(-,-):D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))^{op}\times D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

sends
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))^{op}\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
to
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
.

Proof.

We have to show that, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
and every
M^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
, the functor

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M^{\bullet},-):D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

sends
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
to itself. We argue by Noetherian induction on
S
. If
\dim(S)=0
, the result holds by hypothesis if
M^{\bullet}
is concentrated in degree
0
; the general case follows immediately by dévissage, since
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
is a triangulated subcategory of
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
and the functor (
88
) is triangulated in the first variable.

For the inductive step, suppose that
\dim(S)>0
and that the result is known to hold for all proper closed subvarieties of
S
. As in the previous paragraph, we reduce immediately to the case where the complex
M^{\bullet}
is concentrated in degree
0
, so that we may identify it with an object
M\in\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
. In this case, choose a dense affine open immersion
j:U\hookrightarrow S
satisfying the hypothesis in the statement; let
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
denote the complementary closed immersion. For every
B^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
, applying the functor
\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(-,B^{\bullet})
to the localization triangle

j_{!}j^{*}M\rightarrow M\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}M\xrightarrow{+}

we obtain the distinguished triangle in
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(i_{*}i^{*}M,B^{\bullet})\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M,B^{\bullet})\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(j_{!}j^{*}M,B^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{+}.

Since
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
is a triangulated subcategory of
D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
, it suffices to show that the two lateral terms in the latter triangle belong to
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
in order to conclude the same for the term in the middle. By
5.13
we have canonical isomorphisms

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(i_{*}i^{*}M,B^{\bullet})=i_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{Z}(i^{*}M,i^{!}B^{\bullet}),\qquad\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(j_{!}j^{*}M,B^{\bullet})=j_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(j^{*}M,j^{*}B^{\bullet}).

Since we know that
i^{*}M,i^{!}B^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
, applying the inductive hypothesis we deduce that
\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{Z}(i^{*}M,i^{!}B^{\bullet})\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(Z))
, and therefore that
i_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{Z}(i^{*}M,i^{!}B^{\bullet})\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
. Similarly, since we know that
j^{*}B^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))
, applying the hypothesis on
j^{*}M
we deduce that
\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(j^{*}M,j^{*}B^{\bullet})\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))
. This concludes the proof.
∎

In the second place, we need a criterion ensuring that the monoidal morphism
\iota:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(\cdot))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
is compatible with internal homomorphisms.

Proposition 5.15
.

Suppose that the hypotheses (and hence the conclusions) of
5.14
are satisfied. In addition, suppose that, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
and every
M\in\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
, there exists a dense affine open immersion
j:U\hookrightarrow S
such that the canonical natural transformation of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(U))

\iota_{U}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(j^{*}M,B^{\bullet})\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\iota_{U}(j^{*}M),\iota_{U}(B^{\bullet}))

is invertible. Then, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the canonical natural transformation of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))

\iota_{S}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M^{\bullet},B^{\bullet})\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\iota_{S}(M^{\bullet}),\iota_{S}(B^{\bullet}))

is invertible.

As a preparation for the proof of
5.15
, we need to describe the relations among the various natural isomorphisms witnessing the compatibility of internal homomorphisms with open and closed immersions. For simplicity, we state these auxiliary results in the setting of (closed) monoidal stable homotopy
2
-functors of
[
Ayo07a
, § 2.3]
.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
R:\mathbb{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}
be a monoidal morphism of monoidal stable homotopy
2
-functors. Then the following assertions hold:

(1)

For every closed immersion
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
, and every
M\in\mathbb{H}_{1}(Z)
, the diagram of functors
\mathbb{H}_{1}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}(S)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(i_{*}(i^{*}A\otimes M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}R_{Z}(i^{*}A\otimes M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}(R_{Z}(i^{*}A)\otimes R_{Z}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}(i^{*}R_{S}(A)\otimes R_{Z}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(A\otimes i_{*}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(A)\otimes R_{S}(i_{*}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(A)\otimes i_{*}R_{Z}(M)}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}^{-1}}

is commutative.

(2)

For every open immersion
j:U\hookrightarrow S
, and every
M\in\mathbb{H}_{1}(U)
, the diagram of functors
\mathbb{H}_{1}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}(S)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(j_{!}(j^{*}A\otimes M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}R_{U}(j^{*}A\otimes M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(R_{U}(j^{*}A)\otimes R_{U}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(j^{*}R_{S}(A)\otimes R_{U}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(A\otimes j_{!}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(A)\otimes R_{S}(j_{!}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(A)\otimes j_{!}R_{U}(M)}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}}

is commutative.

Proof.

We only prove the second assertion; the first one can be proved in a similar way.
Developing the definitions of the two vertical isomorphisms in the second diagram, we obtain the more explicit diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(j_{!}(j^{*}A\otimes M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}R_{U}(j^{*}A\otimes M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(R_{U}(j^{*}A)\otimes R_{U}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(j_{!}(j^{*}A\otimes j^{*}j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(j^{*}R_{S}(A)\otimes\iota_{U}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(j^{*}R_{S}(A)\otimes j^{*}j_{!}R_{U}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(j_{!}j^{*}(A\otimes j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}(R_{S}(A)\otimes j_{!}R_{U}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(A\otimes j_{!}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(A)\otimes R_{S}(j_{!}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(A)\otimes j_{!}R_{U}(M).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}}

By inserting, on the top side of the latter, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(j_{!}(j^{*}A\otimes M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}R_{U}(j^{*}A\otimes M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(R_{U}(j^{*}A)\otimes R_{U}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(j_{!}(j^{*}A\otimes j^{*}j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}R_{U}(j^{*}A\otimes j^{*}j_{!}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(R_{U}(j^{*}A)\otimes R_{U}(j^{*}j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(j^{*}R_{S}(A)\otimes R_{U}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(A)\otimes R_{U}(j^{*}j_{!}M))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

and, on its bottom side, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}R_{U}(j^{*}(A\otimes j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(j_{!}j^{*}(A\otimes j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}(R_{S}(A\otimes j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}(R_{S}(A)\otimes R_{S}(j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}(R_{S}(A)\otimes j_{!}R_{U}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(A\otimes j_{!}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(A)\otimes R_{S}(j_{!}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(j^{*}R_{S}(A)\otimes j_{!}R_{U}(M))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}}

we are reduced to considering the outer part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(j_{!}(j^{*}A\otimes j^{*}j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}R_{U}(j^{*}A\otimes j^{*}j_{!}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(R_{U}(j^{*}A)\otimes R_{U}(j^{*}j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(j^{*}R_{S}(A)\otimes R_{U}(j^{*}j_{!}M)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(j^{*}R_{S}(A)\otimes R_{U}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}(j_{!}j^{*}(A\otimes j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}R_{U}(j^{*}(A\otimes j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(j^{*}R_{S}(A)\otimes j^{*}R_{S}(j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}(j^{*}R_{S}(A)\otimes j^{*}j_{!}R_{U}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}(R_{S}(A\otimes j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}(R_{S}(A)\otimes R_{S}(j_{!}M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}(R_{S}(A)\otimes j_{!}R_{U}(M))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

where the two lateral pieces are commutative by naturality while the central rectangle is commutative by definition.
∎

Corollary 5.17
.

Let
R:\mathbb{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}
be a monoidal morphism of monoidal stable homotopy
2
-functors. Then the following assertions hold:

(1)

For every closed immersion
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
, the diagram of functors
\mathbb{H}_{1}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}(S)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}i_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{Z}(M,i^{!}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}R_{Z}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{Z}(M,i^{!}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{Z}(R_{Z}(M),R_{Z}(i^{!}B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{Z}(R_{Z}(M),i^{!}R_{S}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(i_{*}M,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(R_{S}(i_{*}M),R_{S}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(i_{*}R_{Z}(M),R_{S}(B))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{!}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}}

\scriptstyle\sim

is commutative.

(2)

For every open immersion
j:U\hookrightarrow S
, and every
K\in\mathbb{H}_{1}(U)
the diagram of functors
\mathbb{H}_{1}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}(S)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}j_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(M,j^{!}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}R_{U}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(M,j^{!}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(R_{U}(M),R_{U}(j^{!}B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(R_{U}(M),j^{!}R_{S}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{S}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(j_{!}M,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(R_{S}(j_{!}M),R_{S}(B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(j_{!}R_{U}(M),R_{S}(B))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{!}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\theta}}

\scriptstyle\sim

is commutative.

Proof.

By adapting the argument in the proof of
[
Ayo07a
, Prop. 2.3.57]
, one sees that the two statements are formal consequences of the corresponding statements of
5.16
. We omit the details.
∎

Proof of
5.15
.

We have to show that, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
and every
M^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
, the natural transformation of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))

\iota_{S}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M^{\bullet},B^{\bullet})\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\iota_{S}(M^{\bullet}),\iota_{S}(B^{\bullet}))

is invertible. We argue by Noetherian induction on
S
. If
\dim(S)=0
, the result holds by hypothesis if
M^{\bullet}
is concentrated in degree
0
; the general case follows immediately by dévissage.

For the inductive step, assume that
\dim(S)>0
and that the result is known to hold for all proper closed subvarieties of
S
. As in the previous paragraph, we may reduce immediately to the case where the complex
M^{\bullet}
is concentrated in degree
0
, so that we may identify it with an object
M\in\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
. In this case, choose a Zariski-dense affine open immersion
j:U\hookrightarrow S
satisfying the hypothesis in the statement; let
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
denote the complementary closed immersion. For every
B^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))
, we obtain the morphism of distinguished triangles in
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(S))

(89)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(i_{*}i^{*}M,B^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M,B^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(j_{!}j^{*}M,B^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\iota_{S}(i_{*}i^{*}M),\iota_{S}(B^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\iota_{S}(M),\iota_{S}(B^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}^{\mathscr{M}}(\iota_{S}(j_{!}j^{*}M),\iota_{S}(B^{\bullet}))}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+1}

In order to show that the central vertical arrow is an isomorphism, it suffices to show that so are the two lateral vertical arrows. But the two arrows in question are the two marked arrows in the diagrams in
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}i_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{Z}(i^{*}M,i^{!}B^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}\iota_{Z}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{Z}(i^{*}M,i^{!}B^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{Z}(\iota_{Z}(i^{*}M),\iota_{Z}(i^{!}B^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{Z}(\iota_{Z}(i^{*}M),i^{!}\iota_{S}(B^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(i_{*}i^{*}M,B^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\iota_{S}(i_{*}i^{*}M),\iota_{S}(B^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(i_{*}\iota_{Z}(i^{*}M),\iota_{S}(B^{\bullet}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

\scriptstyle\sim

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}j_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(j^{*}M,j^{*}B^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}\iota_{U}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(j^{*}M,j^{*}B^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(\iota_{U}(j^{*}M),\iota_{U}(j^{*}B^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(\iota_{U}(j^{*}M),j^{*}\iota_{S}(B^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{S}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(j_{!}j^{*}M,B^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\iota_{S}(j_{!}j^{*}M),\iota_{S}(B^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(j_{!}\iota_{U}(j^{*}M),\iota_{S}(B^{\bullet}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

\scriptstyle\sim

respectively. Now, taking into account
5.9
(4), we see that the result of
5.16
applies to our setting as well, and thus the same holds for
5.17
. Therefore the two natural diagrams above are commutative. Moreover, in each of them, the vertical arrow in the lower-right corner is invertible by
5.13
, while the horizontal arrow in the upper-right corner is invertible by assumption. We deduce that the marked arrow is invertible as well.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
5.1
.

We want to apply the criteria of
5.14
and
5.15
. To this end, fix an object
M\in\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
. Choose a smooth Zariski-dense affine open immersion
j:U\hookrightarrow S
such that
j^{*}M\in\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(U)
: this is possible since the
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
-fibered subcategory
\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}
of
\mathscr{M}^{0}
is weakly cofinal by
3.3
.
Up to writing
U
as the disjoint union of its connected components and treating each component separately, we may assume that
U
is connected of dimension
d\geq 0
. We divide the proof into two main steps: first of all, we check that the hypotheses are satisfied if
j^{*}M
lies in the image of
\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(U))
; then, we explain how to deduce the general case. Here are the details:

(Step 1)

Suppose first that the object
j^{*}M
lies in the image of
\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(U))
under the quotient functor
\pi_{U}:\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(U))\rightarrow\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(U)
.
In this case, by
3.13
, we have a canonical adjunction of exact functors

(90)

F:\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)\rightleftarrows\mathscr{M}^{0}(U):G,

where
F:=-\otimes j^{*}M[-d]
and
G:=\mathbb{D}_{U}(j^{*}M[-d]\otimes\mathbb{D}_{U}(-))
.
In order to deduce the thesis, the main point is to make sure that the induced exact functor
\mathrm{Ind}(F):\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)
is naturally isomorphic to (the co-restriction of) the functor

-\otimes j^{*}M[-d]:\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))

obtained from
5.10
.

In order to establish this claim, we only need to analyze carefully the various constructions involved. Since the functor
\Delta_{U,*}:\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)
is exact and fully faithful by
5.3
, this is equivalent to saying that the composite functor

\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Ind}(F)}\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)\xrightarrow{\Delta_{U,*}}\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)

is naturally isomorphic to (the co-restriction of) the composite functor

\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)\xrightarrow{-\otimes j^{*}M[-d]}D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))\xrightarrow{\Delta_{U,*}}D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)).

By construction, the latter can be written as the composite

\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)\xrightarrow{-\boxtimes j^{*}M[-d]}\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)[-d]\xrightarrow{\Delta_{U,*}\Delta_{U}^{*}}D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)).

Moreover, following the idea of
3.7
, the functor
\Delta_{U,*}\Delta_{U}^{*}:\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)[-d]\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U))
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)[-d]\xrightarrow{\check{C}_{U\times U,\mathscr{U}}^{\bullet}}C^{[-n,d-n]}(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U))\rightarrow

\displaystyle D^{[-n,d-n]}(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U))

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)),

where
\mathscr{U}
denotes some fixed affine open covering of
U\times U\setminus\Delta_{U}(U)
with
n
elements. Now, for every Grothendieck abelian category
\mathscr{A}
, the additive functors
H^{i}:C^{b}(\mathscr{A})\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
commute with filtered colimits. Since the category
\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)
is Grothendieck abelian by
5.2
(1), we deduce that the composite functors

\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)\xrightarrow{-\boxtimes j^{*}M[-d]}\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)[-d]\xrightarrow{\check{C}_{U\times U,\mathscr{U}}^{\bullet}}C^{[-n,d-n]}(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U))\xrightarrow{H^{i}}\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)

vanish for every
i\neq 0
, and that the composite functor

\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)\xrightarrow{-\boxtimes j^{*}M[-d]}\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)[-d]\xrightarrow{\check{C}_{U\times U,\mathscr{U}}^{\bullet}}C^{[-n,d-n]}(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U))\xrightarrow{H^{0}}\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U\times U)

commutes with filtered colimits. Hence the two functors considered in the claim both satisfy the conditions of
5.3
(1). By the essential uniqueness in the statement of
5.3
(1), we conclude that they are naturally isomorphic as claimed.

Now we can consider the adjunction
