\mathrm{Ind}(F):D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))\rightleftarrows D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U)):\mathrm{Ind}(G)

obtained from (
90
) by applying
5.5
and then passing to the derived categories. As a consequence of the claim just proved, the triangulated functor
\mathrm{Ind}(F):D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))
is naturally isomorphic to the functor

-\otimes j^{*}M[-d]:D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))

obtained from
5.10
: indeed, since the latter triangulated functor is dg-enhanced by construction and
t
-exact for the obvious
t
-structures by the claim, by
2.6
(1) it is naturally isomorphic to the trivial derived functor of the induced exact functor between the hearts.
Taking the right adjoints, we deduce that the functor
\mathrm{Ind}(G):D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))
is naturally isomorphic to the triangulated functor
\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(j^{*}M[-d],-)
obtained from
5.10
. But
\mathrm{Ind}(G)
sends
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))
to itself by construction, and the induced natural transformation of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(U))

\iota_{U}G(B^{\bullet})\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(\iota_{U}(j^{*}M)[-d],\iota_{U}(B^{\bullet}))

is invertible as a consequence of Corollary
3.13
. We deduce that the triangulated functor

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(j^{*}M,-)=\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(j^{*}M[-d],-)[-d]:D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))

sends
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))
to itself, and that the induced natural transformation of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(U))

\iota_{U}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(j^{*}M,B^{\bullet})\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(\iota_{U}(j^{*}M),\iota_{U}(B^{\bullet}))

is invertible. This concludes the proof in the case where
j^{*}M
lies in the image of
\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(U))
.

(Step 2)

For a general choice of
M\in\mathscr{M}^{0}(S)
, the object
j^{*}M\in\mathscr{M}^{0,loc}(U)
does not necessarily satisfy the previous condition. However, by construction, every object of
\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(U))
can be written as the kernel of some morphism in
\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(U))
. Hence we can always choose a co-presentation of the form

j^{*}M=\ker\left\{\pi_{U}(C_{1})\rightarrow\pi_{U}(C_{2})\right\}

for some morphism
C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
in
\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(U))
. Write
N_{1}:=\pi_{U}(C_{1})
,
N_{2}:=\pi_{U}(C_{2})
for convenience, and set
N_{3}:=\textup{coker}\left\{\pi_{U}(C_{1})\rightarrow\pi_{U}(C_{2})\right\}
. Since the image of
\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(U))
in
\mathbf{A}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(U))
is closed under cokernels (as every cokernel of presheaves of finite presentation is itself of finite presentation), the object
N_{3}
belongs to the image of
\mathbf{R}(\mathscr{D}^{0,loc}(U))
. By construction, the object
j^{*}M
is canonically isomorphic to the complex
[N_{1}\rightarrow N_{2}\rightarrow N_{3}]
in
D^{[0,3]}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))
. Moreover, by the previous step of the proof, each of the three functors

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(N_{i},-):D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Ind}\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))\qquad(i=1,2,3)

sends
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))
to itself, and each of the three induced natural transformations of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(U))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(U))

\iota_{U}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(N_{i},B^{\bullet})\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{U}(\iota_{U}(N_{i}),\iota_{U}(B^{\bullet}))\qquad(i=1,2,3)

is invertible. Since both of these properties are triangulated with respect to the first variable, we conclude that they hold for
M
as well. This concludes the proof.

∎

Remark 5.18
.

As explained in
[
IM19
, § 6]
, there exists a theory of weights for perverse motives compatible with the analogous notion of weight for perverse sheaves of geometric origin. By construction, the operations
f^{*}
,
f_{*}
,
f_{!}
and
f^{!}
constructed in
[
IM19
]
on the level of perverse motives enjoy the same weight-exactness properties as the corresponding operations on perverse sheaves as described in
[
BBD82
, § 5.1.14]
.

The same observation applies to the tensor product and internal homomorphisms functors constructed here. Namely, given
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
and letting
D^{b}_{\leq w}(\mathscr{M}(S))
(resp.
D^{b}_{\geq w}(\mathscr{M}(S))
) denote the full subcategory of
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
collecting the complexes of weights at most
w
(resp. at least
w
), we have that the internal tensor product functor restricts to

-\otimes-:D^{b}_{\leq w_{1}}(\mathscr{M}(S))\times D^{b}_{\leq w_{2}}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}_{\leq w_{1}+w_{2}}(\mathscr{M}(S)),

while the internal homomorphisms functor restricts to

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(-,-):D^{b}_{\leq w_{1}}(\mathscr{M}(S))\times D^{b}_{\geq w_{2}}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}_{\geq-w_{1}+w_{2}}(\mathscr{M}(S)).

5.5.
Motivic Verdier duality

We close this section with some useful remarks about Verdier duality for perverse motives.
Recall that, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the motivic Verdier duality functor

\mathbb{D}_{S}:\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)^{op}

was constructed in
[
IM19
, § 2.8]
as an application of the general lifting principles of universal abelian factorizations. The functor
\mathbb{D}_{S}
canonically defines an involutive anti-equivalence of
\mathscr{M}(S)
: there exists a canonical natural isomorphism between functors
\mathscr{M}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(S)

M\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathbb{D}_{S}(M))

which is induced by the usual bi-duality isomorphisms on
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
and on
\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot)
.

What is still lacking at the moment is a precise link between the motivic Verdier duality and the internal homomorphisms of perverse motives:
3.13
given a partial result in this direction; however, not much else could be said without an additional ingredient that was only recently obtained in Tubach’s work
[
Tub23
]
.
Namely, by
[
Tub23
, Thm. 4.43]
there exists a canonical unitary symmetric monoidal morphism of stable homotopy
2
-functors

(91)

\mathsf{Nri}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot)),

with the property that the composite morphism

\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{Nri}^{*}}D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))\xrightarrow{\iota}D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))=D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})

recovers the Betti realization
\mathsf{Bti}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(\cdot,\mathbb{Q})
up to canonical equivalence. The proof is an application of Drew and Gallauer’s universality theorem
[
DG22
, Thm. 7.14]
, which requires to construct an
\infty
-categorical enhancement of the six operations on perverse motives in advance. The construction of such an enhancement is based on a motivic adaptation of Nori’s classical argument for the derived category of constructible sheaves from
[
Nor18
]
.

Using the existence of the morphism (
91
), we can prove the following result:

Proposition 5.19
.

Fix a variety
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
with structural morphism
a_{S}:S\rightarrow\textup{Spec}(k)
. Then:

(1)

There exists a canonical natural isomorphism of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))^{op}

\mathbb{D}_{S}(M^{\bullet})=\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M^{\bullet},a_{S}^{!}\mathbf{1}_{\textup{Spec}(k)}).

(2)

There exists a canonical natural isomorphism of functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))^{op}\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M^{\bullet},N^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{D}_{S}(M^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbb{D}_{S}(N^{\bullet})).

Proof.

(1)

Using the conservativity of
\iota_{S}:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))=D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
, one easily sees that the canonical natural transformation of functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})^{op}\times\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})

\mathsf{Nri}_{S}^{*}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(A,B)\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\mathsf{Nri}_{S}^{*}(A),\mathsf{Nri}_{S}^{*}(B))

induced by the monoidality of
\mathsf{Nri}_{S}^{*}
is necessarily invertible, since the same holds true for the Betti realization
\mathsf{Bti}_{S}^{*}:\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
. In particular, we obtain a canonical isomorphism of functors
\mathbf{DA}_{ct}^{\textup{ét}}(S,\mathbb{Q})^{op}\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))

\mathsf{Nri}_{S}^{*}(\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(A,a_{S}^{!}\mathbf{1}_{\textup{Spec}(k)}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\mathsf{Nri}_{S}^{*}(A),a_{S}^{!}\mathbf{1}_{\textup{Spec}(k)})

which, by construction, satisfies the hypotheses of
[
Ter23U
, Cor. 2.9]
. Applying the latter result, we get the thesis.

(2)

We have a canonical natural transformation between functors
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))^{op}\times D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))

(92)

\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M^{\bullet},N^{\bullet})\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M^{\bullet}\otimes\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(N^{\bullet},K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}),K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})

whose value on the pair
(M^{\bullet},N^{\bullet})
is the morphism in
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
corresponding under adjunction to the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(B,a_{S}^{!}\mathbf{1}_{\textup{Spec}(k)})\otimes\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(B,a_{S}^{!}\mathbf{1}_{\textup{Spec}(k)})\otimes A\otimes\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(B,a_{S}^{!}\mathbf{1}_{\textup{Spec}(k)})\otimes\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(A,B)\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(B,a_{S}^{!}\mathbf{1}_{\textup{Spec}(k)})\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{S}^{!}\mathbf{1}_{\textup{Spec}(k)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev}

Since the analogous natural transformation on
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
is known to be invertible, it follows from
5.1
that (
92
) is invertible as well. Combining this with the previous point, we obtain the result.

∎

Remark 5.20
.

Clearly, the natural isomorphism of
5.19
extends the one constructed in
3.13
.

6.
Motivic local systems

In this short final section, we define Tannakian categories of motivic local systems over smooth, geometrically connected varieties and we establish their basic properties. As an application, we show that the derived categories of perverse motives satisfy Gallauer’s axiomatic of
constructible systems
from
[
Gal22
]
and we deduce a topological reconstruction theorem for perverse motives.

6.1.
Tannakian categories of motivic local systems

As explained in
[
HMS17
, § II.9]
, the abelian category
\mathscr{M}(k)
of
\mathbb{Q}
-linear Nori motives over
k
is a neutral Tannakian category over
\mathbb{Q}
with fibre functor given by the canonical faithful exact functor
\iota_{k}:\mathscr{M}(k)\hookrightarrow\textup{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. The Tannaka dual of
\mathscr{M}(k)
is a pro-affine algebraic group
\mathscr{G}_{mot}(k)
called the
motivic Galois group
of
k
.

In the setting of motivic sheaves, a candidate category of motivic local system over general bases was proposed by Arapura in
[
Ara13
, § 6]
within his own theory of constructible motivic sheaves; see also
[
Ara19
, § 2]
. While internal homomorphisms are not available in full generality in Arapura’s theory, one can still show that these categories of motivic local systems are indeed Tannakian by generalizing the approach for Nori motives, where the main ingredients are provided by the Basic Lemma and Poincaré duality for
k
-varieties. A technical defect of Arapura’s construction is that his categories of motivic local systems are not visibly full subcategories inside the corresponding categories of constructible motivic sheaves.

Here we propose an alternative approach that exploits the six functor formalism on perverse motives in its full strength. By definition, our categories of motivic local systems will be full subcategories inside the corresponding categories of perverse motives. The existence of internal homomorphisms on perverse motives allows us to obtain duality as a straightforward consequence of the rigidity of ordinary local systems.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
be smooth and geometrically connected. We introduce the full abelian subcategory

\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S):=\left\{M\in\mathscr{M}(S)\;|\;\iota_{S}(M)\in\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\right\}\subset\mathscr{M}(S),

where
\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\subset\mathsf{Perv}(S)
is the abelian subcategory introduced in
2.10
. We call
(perverse) motivic local systems
over
S
the objects of
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
.

We can prove straightaway the main structural result about motivic local systems: namely, that they form a neutral Tannakian category over
\mathbb{Q}
. See
[
DM82
]
for the general theory of Tannakian categories.

Theorem 6.2
.

For every smooth, geometrically connected
k
-variety
S
of dimension
d
, the abelian category
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
, endowed with the shifted tensor product

(93)

-\otimes^{\dagger}-:\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\times\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S),\quad(M,N)\rightsquigarrow(M[-d]\otimes N[-d])[d],

is a neutral Tannakian category over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

Note that the abelian category
\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
, endowed with the shifted tensor product

-\otimes^{\dagger}-:\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\times\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\rightarrow\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S),\quad(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\rightsquigarrow(\mathscr{F}[-d]\otimes\mathscr{G}[-d])[d]

is canonically a neutral Tannakian category over
\mathbb{Q}
with unit object
K_{S}:=\mathbf{1}_{S}[d]
. Indeed, the shifted tensor product is designed in such a way that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\times\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Loc}(S)\times\mathsf{Loc}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Loc}(S)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes^{\dagger}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-d]\times[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes-}

is commutative. Hence
\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
can be endowed with canonical mutually compatible associativity, commutativity, and unit constraints obtained by transport of structure from those of the neutral Tannakian category
\mathsf{Loc}(S)
. In fact, as the interested reader can easily check, the validity of the various coherence and mutual compatibility conditions for the constraints on
\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
is just a formal consequence of the definitions. Therefore, by the same procedure, one can define mutually compatible associativity, commutativity, and unit constraints on
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
with respect to the shifted tensor product (
93
).
In this way, by construction, the faithful exact functor

(94)

\iota_{S}:\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)

becomes a tensor functor with the monoidal structure inherited from
2.1
. The category
\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
is neutral Tannakian over
\mathbb{Q}
with fibre functor given by the stalk functor at any chosen point; for any such functor, we obtain a fibre functor for
\mathscr{M}Loc_{p}(S)
simply by composing with (
94
).

Note that the motivic shifted unit object
K^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}:=\mathbf{1}_{S}^{\mathscr{M}}[d]
of
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
satisfies

\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)}(K_{S}^{\mathscr{M}},K_{S}^{\mathscr{M}})=\mathbb{Q},

since the same holds for the shifted unit object of
\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
and the functor
\iota_{S}:\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
is faithful and conservative. Thus, in order to conclude that
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
is neutral Tannakian over
\mathbb{Q}
, we only have to prove the existence of strong duals inside it.
To this end, note that, for every object
\mathscr{F}\in\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
, its dual
\mathscr{F}^{\lor}\in\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
is defined by the formula

\mathscr{F}^{\lor}:=\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\mathscr{F}[-d],\mathbf{1}_{S})[d].

Indeed, this formula is designed in such a way that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)^{op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Loc}(S)^{op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Loc}(S)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lor}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lor}

is commutative. Hence, for every object
M\in\mathscr{M}(S)
, it is natural to define its dual
M^{\lor}
by the analogous formula

M^{\lor}:=\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M[-d],\mathbf{1}_{S}^{\mathscr{M}})[d].

As a consequence of
5.1
, the latter formula defines a functor
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)^{op}
and we have a canonical isomorphisms of functors
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\rightarrow\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)^{op}

\iota_{S}(M^{\lor})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\iota_{S}M)^{\lor}.

In order to conclude that the construction
M\rightsquigarrow M^{\lor}
defines the strong dual in
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
, we have to check the following two conditions:

(i)

The canonical natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)^{op}\times\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)

M^{\lor}[-d]\otimes N[-d]:=\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M[-d],\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})\otimes N[-d]\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M[-d],N[-d])

corresponding under adjunction to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M[-d],\mathbf{1}_{S}^{\mathscr{M}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(N[-d]\otimes M[-d],N[-d])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(N[-d],\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M[-d],N[-d])).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N[-d]\otimes-}

is invertible.

(ii)

The canonical natural transformation of functors
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)

M\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M[-d],\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})[d][-d],\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})[d]=:(M^{\lor})^{\lor}

defined as the composite

M=M[-d][d]\rightarrow\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M[-d],\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S}),\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})[d]=\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(\underline{\textup{Hom}}_{S}(M[-d],\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})[d][-d],\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{S})[d]

is invertible.

As usual, the validity of each of these two conditions follows from the validity of the corresponding condition on
\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
and the conservativity of
\iota_{S}:\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\rightarrow\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

The definition of the shifted tensor product in (
93
) does not coincide with the one given in
Section

3
. Of course, the two functors in question are naturally isomorphic via any of the two composites in (the shift of) the diagram of functors
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\times\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)[-d]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M[-d]\otimes N[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M\otimes N[-d])[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M[-d]\otimes N)[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M\otimes N)[-2d].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

However, the latter diagram is
(-1)^{d}
-commutative. Hence, if
d
is odd, the two versions of the shifted tensor product are not canonically isomorphic. The definition of
Section

3
is adapted to the setting of
3.6
, but it is not well-suited for the task of defining compatible associativity, commutativity and unit constraints by shifting those of perverse motives.

Definition 6.4
.

For every smooth connected
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, we let
\mathscr{G}_{mot}(S)
denote the Tannaka dual of the Tannakian category
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)
, and we call it the
motivic Galois group
of
S
.

By construction, the motivic Galois group
\mathscr{G}_{mot}(S)
is a pro-algebraic group over
\mathbb{Q}
. We plan to investigate the structure of these groups systematically in future work. One basic question is to compute the difference between
\mathscr{G}_{mot}(S)
and Nori’s original motivic Galois group
\mathscr{G}_{mot}(k)
. Note that the structural morphism
a_{S}:S\rightarrow\textup{Spec}(k)
induces an exact tensor functor

a_{S}^{\dagger}:=a_{S}^{*}[\dim(S)]:\mathscr{M}(k)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S),

which, under Tannaka duality, corresponds to a morphism of pro-algebraic groups

a_{S,\dagger}:=\mathscr{G}_{mot}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{G}_{mot}(k).

Intuitively, the difference between the two groups should be measured the usual Tannakian category of topological local systems on the complex-analytic space
S^{\sigma}
; more precisely, by the category of local systems of geometric origin. At least over the generic point of
S
, this intuition is made precise by E. Jacobsen in
[
Jac23
, Thm. 8.4]
.

6.2.
Reconstruction theorem

In his recent paper
[
Gal22
]
, M. Gallauer obtained general reconstruction results in the framework of tensor-triangulated geometry. The general question is how to recover topological information about a variety
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
from the structure of a tensor-triangulated category attached to it. Gallauer’s approach relies on the notion of
constructible systems
, which allows him to study the theory of supports using the general machinery of six functor formalisms.

The starting point for our applications is
[
Gal22
, Prop. 3.1]
, which asserts (as a particular case) that the
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(\cdot))
defines a constructible system in the sense of
[
Gal22
, Defn. 2.3]
; moreover, if
k
is algebraically closed, this constructible system is
generically simple
in the sense of
[
Gal22
, Defn. 2.13]
. In the latter case, by
[
Gal22
, Thm. 5.21]
it follows that, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the underlying Zariski topological space of
S
can be canonically recovered as the smashing spectrum of the tensor-triangulated category
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))
. Analogous reconstruction results hold in the setting of
\ell
-adic sheaves and mixed Hodge modules.
As an application of our results, we obtain the following motivic refinement:

Theorem 6.5
.

Suppose that
k
is algebraically closed. Then, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the underlying topological space of
S
is canonically homeomorphic to the smashing spectrum of the tensor-triangulated category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
.

We start by proving the following result:

Lemma 6.6
.

For every choice of
k
, the
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
is a constructible system in the sense of
[
Gal22
, Defn. 2.3]
; moreover, if
k
is algebraically closed, this constructible system is generically simple in the sense of
[
Gal22
, Defn. 2.13]
.

Proof.

For every
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, we define the subcategory of lisse objects in
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
to be

D^{b}_{ls}(\mathscr{M}(S)):=\left\{M^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\;|\;H^{i}(M^{\bullet})\in\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(S)\;\;\forall i\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}\subset D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S)).

By construction, a complex
M^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
belongs to
D^{b}_{ls}(\mathscr{M}(S))
if and only if the ordinary cohomology objects of the underlying complex
\iota_{S}(M^{\bullet})\in D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))=D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
are local systems: in other words, the category
D^{b}_{ls}(\mathscr{M}(S))
is nothing but the inverse image of the subcategory

D^{b}_{ls}(S,\mathbb{Q}):=\left\{K^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})\;|\;H^{i}(K^{\bullet})\in\mathsf{Loc}(S)\;\;\forall i\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}

under
\iota_{S}:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))=D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
.
Since
D^{b}_{ls}(S,\mathbb{Q})
is a tensor-triangulated subcategory of
D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
, we deduce that
D^{b}_{ls}(\mathscr{M}(S))
is a tensor-triangulated subcategory of
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
. The fact that, as
S
varies in
\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, the subcategories
D^{b}_{ls}(S,\mathbb{Q})\subset D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
satisfy the conditions in
[
Gal22
, Defn. 2.3]
, easily implies the validity of the same conditions for the subcategories
D^{b}_{ls}(\mathscr{M}(S))\subset D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
.

Finally, suppose that
k
is algebraically closed. In this case, we need to show that, for every
S\in\mathsf{Sm}_{k}
, the tensor-triangulated category

2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}D^{b}_{ls}(\mathscr{M}(U))

is simple. To this end, note that we have canonical equivalences of triangulated tensor categories

\displaystyle 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}D^{b}_{ls}(\mathscr{M}(U))=

\displaystyle 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(U))

by cofinality

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}D^{b}(\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(U))

by cofinality

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D^{b}(2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(U))

\displaystyle\textup{by \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GalNote}{}{}, Lemma\penalty\ 2.6]}}.

Since, by
6.2
, the abelian tensor categories
\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(U)
for
U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
are neutral Tannakian over
\mathbb{Q}
, and the restriction functors along inclusions in
\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)
form a filtered system of exact tensor functors, the simplicity of the latter tensor-triangulated category

D^{b}(2-\varinjlim_{U\in\mathsf{Sm}\mathsf{Op}(S)^{op}}\mathscr{M}\mathsf{Loc}_{p}(U))

follows from
[
Gal22
, Prop. 2.18]
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
6.5
.

We only need to check that the tensor-triangulated category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
satisfies the hypothesis of
[
Gal22
, Thm. 5.21]
. In view of
6.6
, it remains to show that the the constructible system
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(\cdot))
satisfies the Lefschetz-type condition stated at the beginning of
[
Gal22
, § 5.2]
: we have to show that, for every irreducible curve
C\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the following two conditions hold:

(i)

We have
\textup{Hom}_{D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(C))}(\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{C},\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{C}[-1])=0
.

(ii)

For every Zariski-dense open inclusion
j:U\hookrightarrow C
, the restriction map

\textup{Hom}_{D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(C))}(\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{C},\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{C})\rightarrow\textup{Hom}_{D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(U))}(\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{U},\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{U})

is a monomorphism.

To this end we exploit the fact that, for every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
, the triangulated category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
is endowed with a constructible
t
-structure as defined at the end of
[
IM19
, § 5.3]
; let
\mathscr{M}_{ct}(S)
denote its heart.
By construction, an object
M^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
belongs to
\mathscr{M}_{ct}(S)
if and only if the object
\iota_{S}(M^{\bullet})\in D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))=D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
belongs to the abelian category
\mathscr{C}\textit{t}(S)
of ordinary algebraically constructible sheaves over
S^{\sigma}
; in particular, the motivic unit object
\mathbf{1}_{S}^{\mathscr{M}}
obtained from
4.3
belongs to
\mathscr{M}_{ct}(S)
. The conservative functor
\iota_{S}:D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))\rightarrow D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))
restricts to a faithful exact functor

(95)

\iota_{S}:\mathscr{M}_{ct}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}\textit{t}(S).

Hence condition (i) above follows directly from the semi-orthogonality axiom of
t
-structures
[
BBD82
, Defn. 1.3.1(i)]
. To check condition (ii), it suffices to form the commutative diagram of abelian groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}_{ct}(C)}(\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{C},\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}_{ct}(U)}(\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{U},\mathbf{1}^{\mathscr{M}}_{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}\textit{t}(C)}(\mathbf{1}_{C},\mathbf{1}_{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}\textit{t}(U)}(\mathbf{1}_{U},\mathbf{1}_{U})}

where the two vertical maps are the monomorphisms defined by (
95
). Since the lower horizontal arrow is known to be a monomorphism, the same must be true for the upper horizontal arrow. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

As remarked in
[
Gal22
, Cor. 5.2]
, in certain cases the scheme
S
is completely determined by the knowledge of its underlying topological space. In these cases, it follows that the scheme
S
is determined by the tensor-triangulated category
D^{b}(\mathscr{M}(S))
.
